Dynamical Billiard and a long-time behavior of the Boltzmann equation in general 3D toroidal domains
Gyounghun Ko, Chanwoo Kim, and Donghyun Lee

Abstract
. Establishing global well-posedness and convergence toward equilibrium of the Boltzmann equation with specular reflection boundary condition has been one of the central questions in the subject of kinetic theory. Despite recent significant progress in this question when domains are strictly convex, as shown by Guo and Kim-Lee, the same question without the strict convexity of domains is still totally open in 3D. The major difficulty arises when a billiard map has an infinite number of bounces in a finite time interval or when the map fails to be Lipschitz continuous, both of which happen generically when the domain is non-convex. In this paper, we develop a new method to control a billiard map on a surface of revolution generated by revolving any planar analytic convex closed curve (e.g., typical shape of tokamak reactors’ chamber). In particular, we classify and measure the size (to be small) of a pullback set (along the billiard trajectory) of the infinite-bouncing and singular-bouncing cases. As a consequence, we solve the open question affirmatively in such domains. To the best of our knowledge, this work is the first construction of global solutions to the hard-sphere Boltzmann equation in generic non-convex 3-dimensional domains. In Appendix, we introduce a novel method for constructive coercivity of a linearized collision operator L L when the specular boundary condition is imposed. In particular, this method works for a periodic cylindrical domain with an annulus cross-section.



1.
Introduction

The Boltzmann equation
is one of the fundamental kinetic models of collisional particles. Mathematically, the Boltzmann equation is a PDE for
F(t,x,v)\geq 0
, a probability distribution function of particles in the phase space. Without external fields, the Boltzmann equation is expressed as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F=Q(F,F)\ \ \text{in}\ \mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3},

(1.1)

\displaystyle F|_{t=0}=F_{0}\ \ \text{on}\ \Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}.

(1.2)

Here,
F(t,x,v)
is the probability distribution function of gas particles with velocity
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
at time
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}=[0,\infty)
and position
x\in\Omega
where
\Omega
is spatial domain. The Boltzmann collision operator
Q(\cdot,\cdot)
is given by

\begin{split}Q(F,G)=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(v-u,\omega)&[F(u^{\prime})G(v^{\prime})+F(v^{\prime})G(u^{\prime})\\
-&F(u)G(v)-G(u)F(v)]\;d\omega du.\end{split}

(1.3)

The collision kernel
B(v-u,\omega)
in (
1.3
) depends on
|v-u|
and
\cos\theta:=\frac{v-u}{|v-u|}\cdot\omega
. In this paper, we consider the hard-sphere:

B(v-u,\omega)=|(v-u)\cdot\omega|,

(1.4)

as well as the hard-potential with the Grad’s angular cut-off assumption:

B(v-u,\omega)=|v-u|^{\zeta}b(\theta),\ \ \text{with}\ \ 0<\zeta\leq 1,\;0\leq b(\theta)\leq C|\cos\theta|,

(1.5)

for some positive constant
C
.

In (
1.3
), the post-collision velocity
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
and the pre-collision velocity
(u,v)
have the following relations:

\displaystyle u^{\prime}=u+[(v-u)\cdot\omega]\,\omega,\quad v^{\prime}=v-[(v-u)\cdot\omega]\,\omega,\quad\text{for }\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}.

Note that above expression implies binary elastic collision:

\displaystyle u+v=u^{\prime}+v^{\prime},\quad|u|^{2}+|v|^{2}=|u^{\prime}|^{2}+|v^{\prime}|^{2}.

Inheriting from these local conservation laws in the collision, the Boltzmann operator enjoys so-called the collisional invariant property:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(\begin{matrix}1\\
v\\
|v|^{2}\end{matrix}\right)Q(F,F)(v)\mathrm{d}v=\left(\begin{matrix}0\\
0\\
0\end{matrix}\right).

(1.6)

In many physical applications, e.g., tokamak reactor, solar wind, etc., particles interact with a physical boundary. The interaction is governed by a boundary condition among various types (
[
2
]
). In this paper, we focus on a basic ideal law, a specular reflection boundary condition: when a gas particle hits the boundary, then it bounces back with the opposite normal velocity and the same tangential velocity, as a
billiard
:

\displaystyle F(t,x,v)=F(t,x,R_{x}v),\ \ \text{on}\ \mathbb{R}_{+}\times\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3},

(1.7)

with a reflection operator
R_{x}v:=v-2({n}(x)\cdot v){n}(x)
where
{n}(x)
is the outward unit normal vector at
x\in\partial\Omega
. In particular, the specular reflection at the boundary conserves the local mass and energy in general. Therefore, the collisional invariance successfully leads conservation of total mass and total energy: for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}F(t,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}F(0,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x,

(1.8)

\displaystyle\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}|v|^{2}F(t,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}|v|^{2}F(0,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x.

(1.9)

The momentum can be conserved in a special case. A domain
\Omega
is axis-symmetric if there are vectors
x_{0}
and
\varpi
such that

[(x-x_{0})\times\varpi]\cdot n(x)=0\ \ \text{for all }\ x\in\partial\Omega.

(1.10)

We note that the specular reflection at the boundary conserves a local angular momentum when the domain is axis-symmetric. Hence, when the domain is axis-symmetric (
1.10
), the total angular momentum is conserved: for all
t\geq 0
,

\begin{split}&\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}[(x-x_{0})\times\varpi]\cdot vF(t,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}[(x-x_{0})\times\varpi]\cdot vF(0,x,v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

(1.11)

Perhaps the most important feature of the Boltzmann equation is its time-irreversibility. In 1872, Boltzmann derived a celebrated
H-theorem
, namely

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}F\ln F\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x+D_{H}(F)=0,\end{split}

(1.12)

with an entropy dissipation
D_{H}(F):=-\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}Q(F,F)\ln F\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x\geq 0
. This non-negativity of the entropy dissipation comes from an algebraic structure of the Boltzmann operator.
Suppose
F(t,x,v)
attains its limit
F_{\infty}(x,v)
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. Then we may expect that an entropy dissipation vanishes at the limit,
D_{H}(F_{\infty})=0
, which implies that
F_{\infty}(u^{\prime})F_{\infty}(v^{\prime})=F_{\infty}(u)F_{\infty}(v)
. In this case, we know that
F
should take a form of local Maxwellian (
[
2
]
), namely

F_{\infty}=\exp\left\{a+b\cdot v+c|v|^{2}\right\}\ \text{with $a,c\in\mathbb{R}$ and $b\in\mathbb{R}^{3}$. }

(1.13)

In general, we can determine the parameters
a,b,
and
c
using the conservative quantities (e.g., (
1.8
), (
1.9
) (
1.11
)). In this paper, we are interested in a normalized global Maxwellian with zero angular momentum:

\displaystyle\mu(v)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|v|^{2}}{2}\right).

(1.14)

Asymptotic stability of equilibria naturally arises as a central subject in mathematical physics and kinetic theory due to the H-theorem. There are basically two types of available results. The first type is a small perturbation framework based on linear analysis and strong control of the perturbation. Classical works of Ukai
[
33
]
and Guo, Strain
[
15
,
13
]
refer to this type. In particular, Guo initiated a novel nonlinear energy method in high Sobolev space in collisional kinetic theory without a physical boundary in
[
15
]
. Using this method, he has solved many open problems, namely constructing a smooth global-in-time solution near the global Maxwellian and proving its convergence as
t\rightarrow\infty
in various collisional models.

The second type of results is an asymptotic stability study of possibly-large solutions that are assumed to exist globally a priori. Desvillettes-Villani obtained a convergence of the solutions to equilibria polynomially fast as
t\rightarrow\infty
, under strong smoothness assumptions and a Gaussian lower bound for the solutions in
[
6
]
.
Later, Imbert-Silvestre replaces the strong smoothness assumption of Desvillettes-Villani with a priori pointwise bound of local mass, momentum, and energy by proving a global regularity of solutions in
[
21
]
under the same assumption when the angular cross section
b(\theta)
is not integrable. In all these results, they work on assumed-to-exist global-in-time solutions with strong a priori uniform-in-time conditions. However, even a weaker condition of a local energy conservation law for the weak global solution ‘a la DiPerna-Lions (
[
7
]
) has not been settled affirmative over three decades. On the other hand, such class of solutions is non-empty due to Guo’s smooth solutions (and due to Strain-Gressman’s solutions in
[
13
]
when the angular cross section is not integrable).

We also refer to
[
8
,
9
,
29
]
for an interesting recent construction of global solutions with large amplitude but small-in-
L^{p}
data. Unlike small perturbation problems, it is very hard to study large amplitude problems for other kinetic equations such as BGK, of which nonlinear order (in collision term) of perturbation is higher than that of the Boltzmann equation. Therefore, constructing large amplitude solutions for such higher nonlinear models would be very interesting. Recently, in
[
1
]
, they obtained the large amplitude solutions in the BGK model by careful analysis to treat highly nonlinear structure.

1.1.
Coercivity in
L^{2}

As the H-theorem suggests, the main stabilizing mechanism comes from the entropy dissipation. It is convenient to look at a relative entropy: with
\mathfrak{h}(z)=(1+z)\ln(1+z)-z
,

H(F|\mu):=\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mu\mathfrak{h}\left(\frac{F-\mu}{\mu}\right)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x.

(1.15)

The relative entropy has the same dissipation
D_{H}
of (
1.12
). As the variation of relative entropy gives rise to an energy
\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{1}{2\mu}(F(t,x,v)-\mu(v))^{2}\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x
(since
\mathfrak{h}(z)\sim\frac{z^{2}}{2}
), a natural perturbation form turns to be

F=\mu+\sqrt{\mu}f.

(1.16)

On the other hand, the entropy dissipation gives rise to a linearized Boltzmann operator

Lf:=\frac{-1}{\sqrt{\mu}}Q(\mu,\sqrt{\mu}f).

(1.17)

Due to the inherited collisional invariance (
1.6
),
L
has a 5-dimensional null space, whose projection is given by

\mathbf{P}f=\big\{a_{f}(t,x)+b_{f}(t,x)\cdot v+c_{f}(t,x)\frac{|v|^{2}-3}{\sqrt{6}}\big\}\sqrt{\mu}.

(1.18)

Furthermore, for the hard-potential models,
Lf=\nu(v)f-Kf
has a natural decomposition by a compact operator
K
and the collisional frequency
\nu(v)
, which is given by

\nu(v):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|v-u|^{\zeta}q_{0}(\theta){\mu}(u)d\omega du\sim\langle v\rangle^{\zeta}:=[1+|v|^{2}]^{\zeta/2}.

(1.19)

Weyl’s theorem implies that
L
is non-negative with respect to the local energy modulo
\mathbf{P}f
:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}fLfdv\gtrsim\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P})f\|_{L^{2}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\nu|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P})f|^{2}\mathrm{d}v.

(1.20)

Clearly, it originally comes from the non-negativity of the entropy dissipation
D_{H}
in (
1.12
), which is the major
L^{2}
-relaxation mechanism. By the missing part in the lower bound of (
1.20
), we need coercivity estimate to obtain
L^{2}
-decay. In
[
16
]
, Guo developed his micro-macro decomposition method in the presence of the boundary, in which the proof is non-constructive. In
[
10
]
, Esposito-Guo-Kim-Marra devised a constructive method to prove the coercivity in the presence of diﬀusive reﬂection boundary condition. It is natural to ask the same question of the constructive coercivity proof in the presence of specular reﬂection boundary. To the best of our knowledge, however, this is not available yet. The key diﬃculty is the lack of clear decomposition of tangential momentum and normal momentum, which is crucial to cancel a boundary terms eﬀectively. In Appendix, we present a special case study in which we can prove the coercivity constructively.

1.2.
L^{\infty}
-Control

Now let us count the nonlinear operator

\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f+Lf=\Gamma(f,f):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}Q(\sqrt{\mu}f,\sqrt{\mu}f).

(1.21)

A major challenge to this nonlinear problem is then to achieve a pointwise-in-
x
bound of
f
in order to control the quadratic nonlinearity. It should be noted that a strategy to use high Sobolev norms via the embedding (e.g.
[
15
,
6
]
) breaks down in the presence of boundary in general. In fact, solutions are singular in the presence of boundary
[
23
,
26
,
19
,
18
,
3
]
, in general.

To overcome this difficulty, Guo developed a method of characteristics for the Boltzmann equation to bootstrap the
L^{2}
bound into a pointwise bound, in
[
16
]
. The basic idea follows: Let
U(t)
be the semigroup generated by
v\cdot\nabla_{x}+L
and
G(t)
be the semigroup generated by
v\cdot\nabla_{x}+\nu
with the specular reflection boundary condition. By
two
iterations, one can establish

\begin{split}U(t)&=G(t)+\int^{t}_{0}G(t-s)KG(s)ds\\
&\quad+\int^{t}_{0}\int^{s}_{0}G(t-s)KG(s-s_{1})KU(s_{1})ds_{1}ds.\end{split}

From the compactness property of
K
, the main contribution is roughly

\begin{split}&\int^{t}_{0}ds_{1}e^{-\nu(t-s)}\int^{s_{1}}_{0}dse^{-\nu(s-s_{1})}\\
&\times\iint_{|v^{\prime}|\leq N,|v^{\prime\prime}|\leq N}|f(s_{1},X(s_{1};s,X(s;t,x,v),v^{\prime}),v^{\prime\prime})|dv^{\prime}dv^{\prime\prime},\end{split}

(1.22)

where
(X(s;t,x,v),V(s;t,x,v))
denotes the generalized characteristics associated with the specular reflection boundary condition for given
t\geq s\geq 0
(See Definition
1.6
), and
N
is some chosen large number.

The key idea of Guo in
[
16
]
, inspired by the Vidav’s idea
[
34
]
, is to control the main term (
1.22
) by
L^{2}
-in-
space
-norm of
f
via a crucial change of variables

v^{\prime}\mapsto X(s_{1};s,X(s;t,x,v),v^{\prime})\ \ \ \ \ \text{({a billiard mixing map})}.

(1.23)

In other words, a mixing effect of the transport operator transfers the
L^{p}_{x}
-control in Section
1.1
to a pointwise control if the billiard mixing map has full rank in the following sense

\begin{split}&\det\left(\frac{\partial X(s_{1};s,X(s;t,x,v),v^{\prime})}{\partial v^{\prime}}\right)\gtrsim_{\varepsilon}1,\\
&\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{except a small subset of }(s,v^{\prime})\in[0,s_{1}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}.\end{split}

(1.24)

If the trajectory is a straight line, then the change of variables is valid for the most of time
s_{1}
. However, in the presence of a reflexive boundary, verifying such a change of variables is obviously extremely difficult for its possible
singularity
and
complexity
in a general domain.

In our opinion, verifying (
1.24
) (or (
2.2
) in general) is not just a technical matter of some particular method. We call the authors’ attention to the fact that the exponential relaxation mechanism of Boltzmann comes from the H-theorem of relative entropy. In general domains, any spatial derivative of solutions does not hold the H-theorem as they do not satisfy a boundary condition. Moreover, the characteristics of the specular reflection do not propagate
C^{1}
-norm (only propagates a
C^{0,\frac{1}{2}}
-Hölder norm is known
[
27
]
) whenever it has hit the boundary tangentially (a grazing bounce). Therefore we expect that derivatives of solutions could oscillate (See
[
27
]
), while the
L^{2}
-relaxation comes through (cf. relaxation of the gradient solutions in the case of diffuse reflection boundary
[
4
]
). In this situation, if the change of variables (
1.23
) fails when
X(s;t,x,v)
belongs to a codimension-
1
subset of the phase space, then the
L^{2}
-relaxation does not transfer to a pointwise control and the pointwise bound of the nonlinear problem may not be available.

In various areas of mathematics such as differential topology, geometric measure theory, and symplectic geometry, several weaker theorems close to the nondegeneracy condition (
1.24
) appear as important subjects. For example, in the field of differential topology, the transversality theorem is a major result that describes the transverse intersection properties of a family of smooth maps.

Theorem 1.1

(The Transversality Theorem, page 68 in Guillemin-Pollack
[
14
]
)
.

Suppose

\mathfrak{F}:\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{S}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Y}

(1.25)

is a smooth map of manifold
\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{Y}
without boundary. Let
\mathfrak{Z}
be any boundaryless submanifold of
\mathfrak{Y}
. Suppose
\mathfrak{F}
is transversal to
\mathfrak{Z}
, i.e. (see page 28, Section 5 of Chapter 1 in the Guillemin-Pollack
[
14
]
)

D_{a}\mathfrak{F}(T_{a}(\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{S}))+T_{\mathfrak{F}(a)}\mathfrak{Z}=T_{\mathfrak{F}(a)}\mathfrak{Y}\ \ \text{for every }a\in\mathfrak{F}^{-1}(\mathfrak{Z}).

(1.26)

Then for almost every
s\in\mathfrak{S}
, the map
\mathfrak{F}_{s}:=\mathfrak{F}(\cdot,s):\mathfrak{X}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Y}
is transversal to
\mathfrak{Z}.

Now let us see how this theorem can apply to our case. Assume that the generalized characteristics
(X(s;t,x,v),V(s;t,x,v))
is smooth locally.
Then the map
(x,v)\mapsto(\mathcal{X}(x,v),\mathcal{V}(x,v)):=(X(s;t,x,v),V(s;t,x,v))\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a local diffeomorphism. Because of that, a map
(\mathcal{X},0):(x,v)\mapsto(X(s;t,x,v),0)
is
transversal
to
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}
(see (
1.26
)), i.e. for all
(x,v)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
,

\begin{split}&\frac{\partial(\mathcal{X},0)}{\partial(x,v)}(T_{(x,v)}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}))+T_{(\mathcal{X},0)(x,v)}(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})\\
&=\text{Proj}_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\{0\}}\left(\frac{\partial(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{V})}{\partial(x,v)}(\mathbb{R}^{6})\right)+\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}\\
&=\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\{0\}+\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}=\mathbb{R}^{6}=T_{\mathcal{(}\mathcal{X},0)(x,v)}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}).\end{split}

(1.27)

Here, a project on the space variables is denoted by
\text{Proj}_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\{0\}}=\begin{bmatrix}Id_{3\times 3}&0_{3\times 3}\\
0_{3\times 3}&0_{3\times 3}\end{bmatrix}
. From the second to the third line in (
1.27
), we have used that
(x,v)\mapsto(\mathcal{X}(x,v),\mathcal{V}(x,v))
is a local diffeomorphism so that the Jacobian matrix
\frac{\partial(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{V})}{\partial(x,v)}
has a full rank 6, and hence
\text{Proj}_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\{0\}}\frac{\partial(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{V})}{\partial(x,v)}
has a full rank 3 in
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\{0\}
. Now we apply the transversality theorem. Set
\mathfrak{F}=(\mathcal{X},0)
,
\mathfrak{X}=\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}
,
\mathfrak{S}=\Omega
,
\mathfrak{Y}=\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}
, and
\mathfrak{Z}=\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}
. We can conclude that the map
(\mathcal{X},0):v\mapsto(\mathcal{X}(x,v),0)
is transversal to
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}
for almost every
x\in\Omega
, assuming the non-grazing condition (
1.29
) holds. This means that, under the same assumption of (
1.29
),

\frac{\partial(\mathcal{X},0)}{\partial v}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})+\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}=\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\ \ \text{ for almost every }x.

Therefore we conclude that if the non-grazing condition (
1.29
) holds then

\det\left(\frac{\partial X(s;t,x,v)}{\partial v}\right)\neq 0\ \ \text{ for almost every $x$, and all $v$.}

(1.28)

It is important to understand that this
almost-every-in-x
result (
1.28
) is not sufficient for the
L^{2}
-
L^{\infty}
bootstrap scheme! For example if the nondegeneracy condition fails in some subset of codimension-
1
then a pointwise control may not be possible in 3D via the
L^{2}
-
L^{\infty}
bootstrap argument.

1.3.
Major difficulty-I: Singularity of the billiard mixing map

In general, the map
v^{\prime}\mapsto X(s_{1};s,*,v^{\prime})
in (
1.23
) is not Lipschitz continuous, and hence the Jacobian matrix
\partial X/\partial v^{\prime}
is not always well-defined (the spatial argument is
*=X(s;t,x,v)
, in our case). When the boundary is smooth such a singularity happens if and only if when the following
non-grazing condition
(
1.29
) fails:

(X(\tau;s,*,v^{\prime}),V(\tau;s,*,v^{\prime}))\notin\gamma_{0}\ \text{ for all }\tau\in(s_{1},s).

(1.29)

Here, the grazing phase boundary is defined as
\gamma_{0}=\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}:n(x)\cdot v=0\}
.

In a general non-convex domain, the generalized characteristics emanating from the grazing boundary
\gamma_{0}
propagate into the interior of the phase space. It is very hard to locate such singularity due to loss of regularity of the billiard map and a possible infinite number of bouncing. We think that these two difficulties are best understood in the language of the billiard theory. Following
[
5
]
, let a collision space
\mathcal{M}:=\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{S}^{2}:n(x)\cdot v\leq 0\}
with a natural measure
|n(x)\cdot v|\mathrm{d}S_{x}\mathrm{d}v
and a collision map
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
such that
(x,v)\mapsto(x_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v),R_{x_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)}v)
. The collision map
\mathcal{F}
preserves a measure on
\mathcal{M}
. Here, we used a backward exit time and position defined as

\displaystyle\begin{split}t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)&:=\sup\{\tau\geq 0:x-sv\in\Omega\textrm{ for all }0\leq s\leq\tau\},\\
x_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)&:=x-t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)v\in\partial\Omega,\end{split}

(1.30)

with a special convention
\sup\emptyset=0
. Now we can construct singular sets inductively. Set
\mathcal{S}_{0}=\partial\mathcal{M}
, which actually is the grazing phase boundary
\gamma_{0}
. Now we define a singular set for the map
\mathcal{F}
which is
\mathcal{S}_{1}:=\mathcal{S}_{0}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}_{0})
. We also set
\mathcal{S}_{-1}:=\mathcal{S}_{0}\cup\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{S}_{0})
. In other words,
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}_{0})=\{(x,v)\in\mathcal{M}:n(x_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v))\cdot v=0\}
, which is indeed a codimension-1 subset of
\mathcal{M}
and a smooth submanifold away from
\mathcal{S}_{0}
. Therefore we can view
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}_{0})
as a disjoint union of codimension-1 smooth submanifolds whose intersections are always on
\mathcal{S}_{0}
. These submanifolds fail to be
C^{1}
on the intersection. Then we have that
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{M}\backslash\mathcal{S}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\backslash\mathcal{S}_{-1}
is a smooth diffeomorphism. Inductively we construct a singular set of the k
{}^{\text{th}}
composition
\mathcal{F}^{k}:={\mathcal{F}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{F}}
which is
\mathcal{S}_{k}=\mathcal{S}_{k-1}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}_{k-1})
. Similarly to
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(S_{0})
, the set
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}_{k-1}):=\{(x,v)\in\mathcal{M}:n(x^{k}(x,v))\cdot v^{k-1}(x,v)=0\}
is a disjoint union of submanifolds intersecting each other on
\mathcal{S}_{k-1}
. Here, we have used a billiard cycle, which is defined as
(t^{0},x^{0},v^{0})=(t,x,v)
and
(t^{1},x^{1},v^{1})=(t-t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v),x_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v),R_{x_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)}v)
, and successively

(t^{k+1},x^{k+1},v^{k+1})=(t^{k}-t_{\mathbf{b}}(x^{k},v^{k}),x^{k}-t_{\mathbf{b}}(x^{k},v^{k})v^{k},R_{x^{k+1}}v^{k}).

(1.31)

Finally, we can identify a singular set for given
s_{1},s
and arbitrary position
*
, in which (
1.29
) fails, with

\bigcup_{k:t^{k+1}(s,*,v^{\prime})\geq s_{1}}^{\infty}\left\{v^{\prime}:\left(x_{\mathbf{b}}(*,v^{\prime}),\frac{v_{\mathbf{b}}(*,v^{\prime})}{|v_{\mathbf{b}}(*,v^{\prime})|}\right)\in\mathcal{S}_{k}\right\}.

(1.32)

To realize Vidav’s idea in the
L^{\infty}
estimate of (
1.22
), it is essential to construct a continuous
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of the singular set (
1.32
) which has small measure in
v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. This is an extremely hard task because of the following reasons: The first reason is that
\left(x_{\mathbf{b}}(*,v^{\prime}),\frac{v_{\mathbf{b}}(*,v^{\prime})}{|v_{\mathbf{b}}(*,v^{\prime})|}\right)\in\mathcal{S}_{k}
could be a large set in
v^{\prime}
for
k\geq 1
(it has zero measure for
k=0
by Sard’s theorem
[
16
]
). Second,
S_{k}
is not a uniformly
C^{1}
smooth manifold, but a disjoint union of many (
\#\sim(C_{\Omega})^{k}
) codimension-1 submanifolds which are Hölder regular at their intersections (
[
27
]
). Lastly,
\bigcup_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{S}_{k}
is dense in
\mathcal{M}
(
[
5
]
)! In other words, the singular set could be dense, large-in-measure, and ill-behaved, in general!

1.4.
Major difficulty-II: Number of bouncing

We already have seen why we have to control the number of bouncing in the construction of the singular set, otherwise the singular set could be dense! More precisely, we want to bound the maximal number
k\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
t^{k+1}(s,*,v^{\prime})\geq s_{1}
.

Now we discuss another reason which is closely related to our method (
[
25
]
) studying the lower bound estimate of Jacobian in (
1.24
). In
[
25
]
, Kim-Lee develops a method to investigate the Jacobian (
1.28
) using the billiard map
\mathcal{F}^{k}
. The key of the method is to relate the Jacobian
v\mapsto X(s;t,x,v)
to the billiard map
(x,v)\mapsto\mathcal{F}^{k}(x,v)
using a triple iteration of (
1.22
). Hence they are able to prove a lower bound of Jacobian (
1.28
), which depends on a pointwise bound of Jacobian matrix
(x,v)\mapsto\mathcal{F}^{k}(x,v)
. Unfortunately, such a map blows up on the singular set
\mathcal{S}_{k}
. In a strictly
C^{3}
convex domain, they can control the number of bounces using the velocity lemma. On the other hand, the number of bouncing could be unbounded if the domain fails either convexity or a
C^{3}
boundedness
[
20
]
. Therefore it is very challenging and important to control the number of bounces.

Both difficulties are also major topics in the contents of billiard theory. It would be worth mentioning a general aspect of our problem in billiard theory. First of all, our domains are essentially 3-dimensional, while most of the billiard theory is built for 2D domains. Second, our billiard is not hyperbolic, which means Lyapunov exponents could be zero and all dispersing, focusing, and degenerating billiard could happen in a single cycle. In particular, the billiard map is not even defined when it hits the inflection point of the boundary tangentially. Moreover, this set could have a large measure in the collision space. Thirdly, modern billiard theory based on the ergodic theory and many available results hold almost everywhere, which is not sufficient for our purpose as we have discussed across (
1.28
).

1.5.
Decomposition of 3D general toroidal domains

In this paper, we consider a
toroidal
domain with
convex
planar cross section whose boundary is a
strictly convex analytic
closed curve. This type of domain appears in many applications, such as modern tokamak reactors.

Definition 1.2

(Domains
\Omega
and their boundary
\partial\Omega
)
.

Suppose a closed curve is sitting on the
xz
-plane away from the
z
-axis: with
\gamma(a)=\gamma(b)
and
\gamma_{1}>0
,

\gamma(\tau)=(\gamma_{1}(\tau),0,\gamma_{2}(\tau))\ \ \text{for}\ a\leq\tau<b.

(1.33)

We assume that this curve is positively oriented, real analytic, and unit-speed, namely
\gamma_{i}:\tau\in[a,b)\rightarrow xz\text{-plane}
is real-analytic with respect to
\tau
; and
(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau))^{2}+(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau))^{2}=1
for all
\tau\in[a,b)
. Furthermore, we assume that the curve is strictly convex, i.e.

\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)\gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau)-\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau)>0\ \ \text{for}\ a\leq\tau<b.

(1.34)

Here and after, we always regard
[a,b)
as a periodic interval. Then there exist
\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*}
, and
\lambda_{*}
in
[a,b)
such that
\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1,*})=0=\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{2,*})
,
\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)<0
for
\tau_{1,*}<\tau<\tau_{2,*}
, and
\lambda_{*}\in(\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*})
is the unique zero of the function
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}
in
(\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*})
:
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\lambda_{*})=0
. Also it is easy to check that
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)<0
for
\tau\in(\tau_{1,*},\lambda_{*})
and
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)>0
for
\tau\in(\lambda_{*},\tau_{2,*})
. See Figure
1.36
.

We obtain our boundary
\partial\Omega
by rotating this curve about the
z
-axis, which can be parameterized as

\sigma(\tau,\varphi)=(\gamma_{1}(\tau)\cos\varphi,\gamma_{1}(\tau)\sin\varphi,\gamma_{2}(\tau)),\ \ a\leq\tau<b,\;0\leq\varphi<2\pi.

(1.35)

When we define the boundary, it suffices to restrict
\varphi\in[0,2\pi)
. However, later we will extend
\varphi
to the whole real number
\mathbb{R}
to count a winding number of billiard trajectory winding around the
z
-axis. Finally, we define our domain
\Omega
to be the bounded open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
surrounded by the boundary
\partial\Omega
.

Figure 1
.
Analytic convex curve
\gamma
in
xz
-plane

Although the above
\Omega
and
\partial\Omega
are well-defined, it turns out that introducing an auxiliary indication function of the domain is convenient. Let
\overline{\xi}:xz\text{-plane}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a real-analytic function and satisfies that
\overline{\xi}<0
in
\Omega\cap xz\text{-plane}
,
\overline{\xi}>0
in
\Omega^{C}\cap xz\text{-plane}
, and

\overline{\xi}=0\ \text{on}\ \partial\Omega\cap xz\text{-plane}.

(1.36)

For the construction of such functions, we refer to
[
3
]
. With this choice,
\overline{\xi}
is real-analytic and convex. Finally we set an indication function in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which is real-analytic and convex, by

\xi(x,y,z):=\overline{\xi}(\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}},z).

(1.37)

Now, we have the following relations

\begin{split}\Omega&:=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\xi(x,y,z):=\overline{\xi}(\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}},z)<0\},\\
\partial\Omega&:=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\xi(x,y,z):=\overline{\xi}(\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}},z)=0\}.\end{split}

(1.38)

For
(t,x,v)\in[0,\infty)\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, (
\overline{\Omega}
is a closure of
\Omega
)
we let
\gamma=\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
denote the boundary of phase space
\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We further split
\gamma
into an outgoing boundary
\gamma_{+}
, an incoming boundary
\gamma_{-}
, and a grazing boundary
\gamma_{0}
:

\begin{split}\gamma_{+}&:=\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}:n(x)\cdot v>0\},\\
\gamma_{-}&:=\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}:n(x)\cdot v<0\},\\
\gamma_{0}&:=\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}:n(x)\cdot v=0\}.\end{split}

(1.39)

Here,
n(x)
denotes the outward normal at
x\in\partial\Omega
such that
n(x)=\frac{\nabla\xi(x)}{|\nabla\xi(x)|}
.

Recall the backward exit time
t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)
in (
1.30
). Remark that
(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\mapsto(x_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v),R_{x_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)}v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
is the billiard map in the mathematical theory of billiard
[
5
]
. Here, the reflection operator is denoted by
R
:

R_{x}v:=v-2({n}(x)\cdot v){n}(x).

(1.40)

We set
(t^{0},x^{0},v^{0})=(t,x,v)
and define backward cycles successively, for
k\geq 0
,

(t^{k+1},x^{k+1},v^{k+1})=(t^{k}-t_{\mathbf{b}}(x^{k},v^{k}),x^{k}-(t^{k}-t^{k+1})v^{k},R_{x^{k+1}}v^{k}),

(1.41)

where
R_{x^{k+1}}=I-2{n}(x^{k+1})\otimes{n}(x^{k+1})
. We also define the forward exit time and position

\displaystyle t_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v)

\displaystyle:=\sup\{\tau\geq 0:x+sv\in\Omega\textrm{ for all }s\in(0,\tau)\},

\displaystyle x_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v)

\displaystyle:=x+t_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v)v\in\partial\Omega.

We set
(t_{\mathbf{f}}^{0},x_{\mathbf{f}}^{0},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{0})=(0,x,v)
. Then, we define forward cycles, for
k\geq 0
,

(t_{\mathbf{f}}^{k+1},x_{\mathbf{f}}^{k+1},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{k+1})=(t_{\mathbf{f}}^{k}+t_{\mathbf{f}}(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{k},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{k}),x_{\mathbf{f}}^{k}-(t_{\mathbf{f}}^{k+1}-t_{\mathbf{f}}^{k})v_{\mathbf{f}}^{k},R_{x_{\mathbf{f}}^{k+1}}v_{\mathbf{f}}^{k}).

(1.42)

In our analysis, it is important to classify points in the phase space where
v\mapsto x^{k}-(t^{k}-s)v^{k}
is Lipschitz continuous. It turns out that such a map fails to be Lipschitz continuous whenever there exists
\ell\leq k
such that
n(x^{\ell})\cdot v^{\ell}=0
, namely the billiard trajectory hits the grazing set
\gamma_{0}
in past. The major difficulty arises from the fact that the billiard trajectory emanating from the grazing set is not confined near the boundary but impact on the interior dynamics globally in general. As the first step to analyze this case, we categorize the grazing set into several subsets:

Definition 1.3
.

We split the grazing set
\gamma_{0}
into three parts: the convex grazing
\gamma_{0}^{V}
, concave grazing
\gamma_{0}^{C}
, and inflection grazing
\gamma_{0}^{I}
of

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gamma_{0}^{V}&:=\{(x,v)\in\gamma_{0}:t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)=0\textrm{ and }t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,-v)=0\},\\
\gamma_{0}^{C}&:=\{(x,v)\in\gamma_{0}:t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)\neq 0\textrm{ and }t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,-v)\neq 0\},\\
\gamma_{0}^{I}&:=\gamma_{0}^{I_{+}}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I_{-}},\end{split}

(1.43)

where the inflection grazing
\gamma_{0}^{I}
is further decomposed as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gamma_{0}^{I_{+}}&:=\left\{(x,v)\in\gamma_{0}:\begin{array}[]{cc}t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)\neq 0,\ t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,-v)=0,\\
\textrm{ and }\exists\;\delta>0\textrm{ s.t. }x+sv\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\overline{\Omega}\ \textrm{ for }s\in(0,\delta)\end{array}\right\},\\
\gamma_{0}^{I_{-}}&:=\left\{(x,v)\in\gamma_{0}:\begin{array}[]{cc}t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)=0,\ t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,-v)\neq 0,\\
\textrm{ and }\exists\;\delta>0\textrm{ s.t. }x+sv\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\overline{\Omega}\ \textrm{ for }s\in(-\delta,0)\end{array}\right\}.\end{split}

(1.44)

The inflection grazing
\gamma_{0}^{I}
can be redefined as follows using the analytic function
\xi
defined in (
1.38
).

Remark 1.4
.

(Inflection grazing) For an arbitrary boundary point
x\in\partial\Omega
, let
\sigma(\tau,\varphi)=x
in (
1.35
). The fact
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v)\in\gamma_{0}^{I}
is equivalent to the following:
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v)\in\gamma_{0}
and there exists
\delta>0
such that

\xi(\sigma(\tau,\varphi)+sv)>0\ \text{ and }\ \xi(\sigma(\tau,\varphi)-sv)<0\ \text{ for all }\ s\in(0,\delta).

In this case,
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v)\in\gamma_{0}^{I_{+}}
and
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),-v)\in\gamma_{0}^{I_{-}}
.

Remark 1.5
.

Due to our special convention
\sup\emptyset=0
, either
t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)
or
t_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v)
could be zero so that the forward or backward cycles can stop. There are two cases:

1.

If
(x,v)\in\gamma_{0}^{V}
, then
t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)=t_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v)
. In this case,
t^{\ell}(t,x,v)=t
and
t^{\ell}_{\mathbf{f}}(t,x,v)=t
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, and hence
x^{\ell}(x,v)=x=x^{\ell}_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v)
and
v^{\ell}(x,v)=v=v^{\ell}_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v)
for all
\ell
.

2.

If
(x,v)\in\gamma_{0}^{I+}
(resp,
(x,v)\in\gamma_{0}^{I-}
), then
t_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v)=0
(resp,
t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)=0
) and hence
x^{\ell}_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v)=x
and
v^{\ell}_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v)=v
(resp,
x^{\ell}(x,v)=x
and
v^{\ell}(x,v)=v
) for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 1.6

(Billiard Trajectory)
.

Let
(t,x,v)\in[0,\infty)\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We define a billiard trajectory (or generalized specular characteristics)

\begin{split}X(s;t,x,v)&=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{s\in[t^{\ell+1}(t,x,v),t^{\ell}(t,x,v))}\big(x^{\ell}(x,v)-(t^{\ell}(t,x,v)-s)v^{\ell}(x,v)\big),\\
V(s;t,x,v)&=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{s\in[t^{\ell+1}(t,x,v),t^{\ell}(t,x,v))}v^{\ell}(x,v).\end{split}

(1.45)

Hence the billiard trajectory solves weakly, for
s\in(t^{\ell+1},t^{\ell})
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{d}{ds}X(s;t,x,v)=V(s;t,x,v),\quad\frac{d}{ds}V(s;t,x,v)=0,\quad&\\
X(s;t,x,v)=x,\quad V(s;t,x,v)=v,\quad&\text{for }s=t.\end{split}

Remark 1.7
.

It is a standard application of the Poincare recurrence theorem (
[
5
]
) to guarantee that

\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}|t^{\ell}(t,x,v)-t^{\ell+1}(t,x,v)|=\infty
for almost every
(x,v)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
for each
t\geq s\geq 0
. Therefore (
1.45
) is well-defined almost everywhere in the phase space.

1.6.
Main Theorem

Global well-posedness of the Boltzmann equation and the long-time behavior of solutions have been central subjects (e.g., Boltzmann’s H-theorem) in mathematical kinetic theory. Unfortunately, any rigorous answer to the
hard-sphere
Boltzmann equation of the
specular reflection
at a boundary of general convex domains had not been provided until a recent date, and yet much is unknown for the general domains!

In a famous 1977’s announcement
[
32
]
, Shizuta-Asano claimed to construct a global-in-time solution of the Boltzmann equation in a general smooth convex domain with a specular reflection boundary, while no proof of their logic has been available yet. As a matter of fact, a real breakthrough happened after several decades. In 2010, Guo developed a new method, called
L^{2}
-
L^{\infty}
framework, and first rigorously constructed a global-in-time solution to the Boltzmann equation with the specular reflection boundary condition and proved its convergence toward the global Maxwellian, when a domain is both
real-analytic
and convex (
[
16
]
). In 2018, Kim-Lee removed a crucial real-analytic condition (
[
25
]
), in which they completely resolved a longstanding open problem after the famous unsettled 1977’s announcement. They made a major advance in the study of billiard trajectory by discovering its generic relation to the billiard map.

The same problem without the convex condition of domain is very challenging due to the presence of infinitely-many bouncing trajectories and interior grazing sets. In this question, there is only one result so far by Kim-Lee
[
26
]
in 2018, in which they construct a global solution and prove its asymptotic stability near the Maxwellian in cylindrical domains whose cross-section is an analytic and non-convex closed planar curve. This result is essentially two dimensional and it is crucial in the proof of control number of bounces and interior grazing sets.

The hard-sphere model of specular reflection boundary is particularly hard in the study of global behavior because the problem is very hyperbolic! If the boundary reflection is not specular but either diffuse reflection of inflow then the problem turns out to be dissipative even without the intermolecular collision
[
24
,
22
]
. If the intermolecular collision is grazing (e.g., Landau equation), then the analysis is not suffered from infinitely many bounces as the hard-sphere model due to a regularity gain
[
17
]
. On the other hand, it is worth noting that the hard-sphere model of specular reflection boundary is yet the sole boundary problem that can be rigorously derived from the particle systems (e.g., Lanford’s theory of Boltzmann-Grad limit)
[
11
]
.

In this paper, we consider a 3D toroidal domain, whose cross-section is a general analytic convex curve. To the best of the authors’ knowledge, this is the first result on the global solution of the Boltzmann in a generic 3D non-convex domain with the specular reflection boundary.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
w(v)=(1+|v|)^{\beta}
with
\beta>\frac{5}{2}
. Consider a general toroidal domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
in Definition
1.2
. We assume
F_{0}=\mu+\sqrt{\mu}f_{0}\geq 0
with
\mu
in (
1.14
) satisfies a normalization condition:

\begin{split}\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}(F_{0}(x,v)-\mu)dxdv&=0,\\
\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}|v|^{2}(F_{0}(x,v)-\mu)dxdv&=0,\\
\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\{x\times\hat{z}\}\cdot vF_{0}(x,v)dxdv&=0.\end{split}

(1.46)

Then, there exists
0<\delta\ll 1
such that if

\|wf_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3})}<\delta,

then the Boltzmann equation (
1.1
) with the specular boundary condition (
9.2
) has a unique global solution
F(t)=\mu+\sqrt{\mu}f(t)\geq 0
. Moreover, there exists
\lambda>0
such that

\sup_{t\geq 0}e^{\lambda t}\|wf(t)\|_{\infty}\lesssim\|wf_{0}\|_{\infty},

and the following conservation laws hold:

\displaystyle\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}(F(t,x,v)-\mu)dxdv=\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\{x\times\hat{z}\}\cdot vF(t,x,v)dxdv

\displaystyle=\iint_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}|v|^{2}(F(t,x,v)-\mu)dxdv=0.

Remark 1.9
.

Using the method in
[
28
]
, our proof also works for a non-zero angular momentum case. For the sake of simplicity, we only give a proof for the zero angular momentum case.

Remark 1.10
.

Extending the theorem to a general non-convex domain beyond the category of Definition
1.2
is challenging. We discuss two types of domains and anticipated difficulties.

•

No structural condition: In this paper, axis-symmetric structure is used crucially several ways, in particular when we prove Proposition
5.2
. The proposition says that “Sticky Grazing” points, which was introduced in
[
26
]
, do not exist. (See Figure 2 and Lemma 8 in
[
26
]
.) Without any special symmetric structures, it seems hard to control the number (or the measure) of such Sticky Grazing points. The presence of inflection points is another major difficulty, in which the billiard map is not a union of straight lines. In general, billiard trajectory emanating from such inflection points could be large set in the velocity.

•

No real analytic condition: From the example of Halpern’s billiard
[
20
]
, infinitely many bounces would happen in finite travel length ,i.e., accumulation of bouncing points. This is totally local phenomenon, and without real analytic condition of the boundary, this can happen generically.

1.7.
Billiard Trajectory

Definition 1.11
.

(Velocity set
\mathbb{V}^{N}
and winding number)
For fixed
N\geq 1
,

\mathbb{V}^{N}:=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\frac{1}{N}\leq|v|\leq N\}.

(1.47)

When
(X(s;t,x,v),V(s;t,x,v))
travels inside of
\Omega
under the specular reflection boundary condition up to total travel length
L>0
, we define a uniformly finite upper bound for the winding number around the
z
-axis:

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}:=\left(\left[\frac{L}{2\pi r}\right]+1\right),

(1.48)

where
r=\gamma_{1}(\lambda_{*})
is the minimum distance between
z
-axis and
\partial\Omega
. Here
[\cdot]
is the Gauss function.

Remark 1.12
.

We often consider only a normalized speed
\frac{v}{|v|}
with fixed travelling length
L
. We can easily study the trajectory of unscaled
v\in\mathbb{V}^{N}
, by setting
L=NT
(in Theorem
5.7
) where
NT
is the maximal travel length of the trajectory for fixed time interval
[0,T]
and
v\in\mathbb{V}^{N}
. See Theorem
5.7
.
Also, note that the smallest perimeter around
z
-axis of the domain
\Omega
is
2\pi r
. Hence, the winding number
\mathbf{w}
of (
1.48
) is the maximum number that a trajectory can rotate
z
-axis during whole travel length
L
.

Since a trajectory can rotate around the
z
-axis multiple times, it is important to distinguish two trajectory on the same cross-section depending on how many times the trajectory rotated around the
z
-axis. So, it is natural to extend
\varphi
into
\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 1.13
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathbb{R}
. We define
\varphi
cross-section
S_{\varphi}
as follows

\displaystyle S_{\varphi}:=\{x\in\Omega:\phi(x)=\varphi\pmod{2\pi}\},

(1.49)

where
\phi(x)
is the angle between the
x
-axis and the projected point
(x_{1},x_{2},0)
of
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
. In other words, the map
\phi:\overline{\Omega}\mapsto[0,2\pi)
satisfies

\displaystyle\phi(x)=\cos^{-1}\left(\frac{(x_{1},x_{2},0)}{\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}}\cdot(1,0,0)\right)=\cos^{-1}\left(\frac{x_{1}}{\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}}\right).

We define the closure
\overline{S_{\varphi}}
of
S_{\varphi}
as

\displaystyle\overline{S_{\varphi}}:=\{x\in\overline{\Omega}:\phi(x)=\varphi\pmod{2\pi}\},

and the boundary
\partial S_{\varphi}
of
S_{\varphi}

\displaystyle\partial S_{\varphi}:=\{x\in\partial\Omega:\phi(x)=\varphi\pmod{2\pi}\}.

We also define a function
\hat{\varphi}=\hat{\varphi}(\varphi):(-2\pi\mathbf{w},0]\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{2}
as a direction which is perpendicular to
S_{\varphi}
with positive orientation w.r.t the
z
-axis. Since the cross section
S_{0}
is in the
xz
-plane,
\hat{\varphi}(0)=(0,1,0)
and

\displaystyle\hat{\varphi}(\varphi)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\varphi&-\sin\varphi&0\\
\sin\varphi&\cos\varphi&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\hat{\varphi}(0).

Remark 1.14
.

In this paper, we carefully distinguish the cross-sections
S_{\varphi}
and
S_{\varphi+2\pi}
. For fixed
x\in\Omega
, let us choose a
\varphi\in(-2\pi\mathbf{w},0]
such that
\phi(x)=\varphi\pmod{2\pi}
. Then for
(x,\varphi,v)\in\overline{\Omega}\times(-2\pi\mathbf{w},0]\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
, we will define the arrival time function
s(x,\varphi,v)
in Lemma
4.12
which represents time taking from
x\in S_{\varphi}
with velocity
v
to
S_{0}
along the trajectory(
1.45
), rotating around the
z
-axis multiple times if necessary. Then, the arrival time function is uniquely determined with respect to
(x,\varphi,v)
, instead of
(x,v)
.

Definition 1.15
.

(Definition for
x,y,\varphi
coordinates)
We define a new coordinate on fixed
S_{0}
,
\varphi=0
cross-section. We use the coordinate for
v
:

\begin{pmatrix}v_{x}\\
v_{\varphi}\\
v_{y}\end{pmatrix}.

(1.50)

Here,
(v_{x},v_{y})
are perpendicular coordinates in the cross-section
S_{0}
and
v_{\varphi}
is coordinate of
\hat{\varphi}(0)
direction which is perpendicular (positive orientation WLOG) to
S_{0}
cross-section. See below Figure
2
.

Figure 2
.
Solid torus and the cross section

In the figure above, the left-hand side represents the
\varphi=0
cross section
S_{0}
in (
1.49
). On the right-hand side, we express the new coordinates as
v=(v_{x},v_{\varphi},v_{y})
. Here,
v_{x}
and
v_{y}
are perpendicular to each other, and
v_{\varphi}
-direction is perpendicular to
S_{0}
, as shown.

2.
Ideas and Scheme to overcome the Main Difficulties

The major difficulty arises as the map
v^{\prime}\mapsto X(s_{1};s,X(s;t,x,v),v^{\prime})
can fail to be Lipschitz in a positive measure subset when the domain is not convex, in general. To overcome this difficulty, we first carefully study the inflection points of
\partial\Omega
. In
[
26
]
, the billiard trajectory is two-dimensional essentially in the cylindrical domain. As the boundary is a real-analytic curve, inflection points (and corresponding tangential direction) are at most finitely many. On the other hand, in our case of Definition
1.2
, structure of inflection points and directions are complex. In fact, every point of the inner part (where
\tau_{1,*}<\tau<\tau_{2,*}
) is a saddle point, and inflection grazing always exists at all of these points. Therefore, in contrast to the cylindrical case of
[
26
]
,
there are uncountably many inflection grazing in
\Omega
. Moreover, it is impossible to keep track of the chaotic billiard trajectory emanating from such inflection grazing in
\Omega
.
We resolve this difficulty by classifying and measuring the size of trajectories of the inflection grazing using axial symmetric property and analyticity crucially. We explain the scheme for the trajectory with positive angular momentum without loss of generality:

Step 1.
(Study the structure of inflection grazing and nonexistence of converging bounces.) In Section 3, we show all the point of the inner part, i.e.,
\tau_{1,*}<\tau<\tau_{2,*}
(See Figure
1
), are saddle points with exactly two directions
I^{1},I^{2}
, of which normal curvatures vanish. For another directions, we have positive or negative normal curvatures. (See Figure
4
.) Moreover, it is important to distinguish whether an inflection grazing is
\gamma_{0}^{I_{+}}
or
\gamma_{0}^{I_{-}}
as the backward in time trajectory must stop at
\gamma_{0}^{I_{-}}
and a particle trajectory cannot propagate forward in time at
\gamma_{0}^{I_{+}}
by definition (
1.44
). We classify such cases using the intersection of
\partial\Omega
and tangential plane
T_{p}(\partial\Omega)
at
p\in\partial\Omega
, which is determined by local convexity/concavity at the point. In Lemma
3.6
, using analyticity and saddle structure of the boundary, we will prove that every saddle point
p\in\partial\Omega
has one
\gamma_{0}^{I_{-}}
direction and one
\gamma_{0}^{I_{+}}
direction on the tangential plane
T_{p}(\partial\Omega)
away from some finite points
\gamma(\tau)
of
\tau\in(\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*})
.

For simplicity, let us consider standard solid torus (of which cross-section is a circle) with inner radius
r
and revolving radius
R
. Then, boundary
\partial\Omega
can be parametrized by

\displaystyle\sigma(\theta,\varphi)=((R+r\cos\theta)\cos\varphi,(R+r\cos\theta)\sin\varphi,r\sin\theta),\quad 0\leq\theta<2\pi,\;0\leq\varphi<2\pi,

where
\theta
represents rotation around the cross-section circle and
\varphi
represents rotation around
z
-axis of revolution. Figure
3
below visualizes the intersection of tangent plane
T_{p}(\partial\Omega)
at
p=\sigma(\theta,0)\in\partial\Omega
and the torus
(\partial\Omega)
for each
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac{\pi}{12},\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac{\pi}{3}
, and
\pi
. Except for
\theta=\pi
, there are two directions
I^{1}(\theta,\varphi)\in\gamma_{0}^{I_{+}}
and
I^{2}(\theta,\varphi)\in\gamma_{0}^{I_{-}}
on the tangent plane
T_{p}(\partial\Omega)
. (See (A) and (B) of Figure
3
and Figure
4
.) The point
\theta=\pi
is the case where there is no inflection grazing. ((C) of Figure
3
.)

(a)

\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac{\pi}{12}

(b)

\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac{\pi}{3}

(c)

\theta=\pi

Figure 3
.
Intersection between solid torus (
\partial\Omega
) and tangent plane
T_{p}
at
p=\sigma(\theta,0)\;(\varphi=0)

Figure 4
.
General intersection structure of
T_{p}\cap\partial\Omega
and inflection directions

Without loss of generality, let us fix a point
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and consider velocity
v
with unit speed
|v|=1
. We consider (
1.45
), the backward in time trajectory under specular reflection BC from
(x,v)
and will study how many bounces are available up to finite travel length
L<\infty
. If there are infinitely many bounces in length
L
, bouncing points must be convergent. Such a phenomenon cannot happen in the outer part (where
\tau_{2,*}<\tau<\tau_{1,*}
) by uniform convexity (See velocity lemma : Lemma 1 of
[
16
]
or Lemma 2.7 of
[
25
]
, for example). Therefore we can assume the trajectory converges to a point in the inner part. See Figure
5
. From axial symmetric structure, specular reflection, and convexity of the cross-section, we can derive (See Lemma
4.4
)

\displaystyle\Delta\tau_{i+1}-\Delta\tau_{i}=\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{i+1}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{i}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{i+1}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{i}^{2}),\quad\text{for }\;i=1,2,\cdots,

for sufficiently small difference
\Delta\theta
and
\Delta\tau
except some cases : for example, if
\tau
is too close to
\tau_{1,*}
or
\tau_{2,*}
of Figure
1.36
, we remove inflection directions uniformly as in Figure
7
. If
p\in\partial\Omega
is an inflection point on the intersection
T_{p}(\partial\Omega)\cap\partial\Omega
, we also remove inflection direction as in Figure
9
. (Also see Figure (
10(b)
).) The above estimate implies that
\varphi
(axisymmetric) directional perturbation affects in higher order with small effect. Or equivalently, we can say that for converging bouncing points (small
\Delta\varphi
and
\Delta\tau
),
\varphi
-directional perturbation is too weak to change the behavior of the
\tau
-directional property. However, this is a contradiction, because if
\Delta\tau
is dominant, the trajectory cannot converge by the convexity of the cross-section with help of the velocity lemma again. In fact, we obtain
\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Delta\tau_{i}\rightarrow\infty
, and hence infinite bounce cannot happen. See Lemma
4.6
for a precise statement.

Figure 5
.
Perturbation
\Delta\tau_{i}
and
\Delta\varphi_{i}

Step 2.
(Uniform number of bounce and small neighborhood of bad directions.) In general toroidal domains, billiard trajectory (
1.45
) is extremely chaotic with unpredictable encounter with inflection grazing. We will rely on analyticity, axis-symmetry, convex cross-section of
\partial\Omega
, and compactness argument to derive some properties of the linear trajectory in
\Omega
. Of course, we note that the trajectory
(X(s;t,x,v),V(s;t,x,v))
is not analytic in general if the trajectory belongs to
\gamma_{0}^{C}
or
\gamma_{0}^{I}
. First, in previous
Step 1
, we introduced some bad direction sets which have something to do with the possibility of infinite bounce in finite travel length, e.g., inflection directions at some points on
\partial\Omega
. By conservation of angular momentum, such velocities look like a ring on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
centered at
x\in\overline{\Omega}
as in (
4.44
) of Definition
4.14
. Since we can confine such bad directions in some small sets on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, we can define an open cover
\bigcup_{i=1}^{l_{R}}\{B(x_{i}^{R},r_{i}^{R})\cap\overline{S_{0}}\}
for
\overline{S_{0}}
and the corresponding small bad directions
\mathcal{O}_{i}^{R}\subset\mathbb{S}^{2}
such that the backward in time trajectory from
(x,v)
avoids becoming bad phase points which described in
Step 1
,

\text{ if $x\in B(x_{i}^{R},r_{i}^{R})\cap\overline{S_{0}}$ and $v\notin\bigcap_{j\in I_{x}^{R}}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{R}$},

where
I_{x}^{R}
defined in (
4.59
).

Using
\{B(x_{i}^{R},r_{i}^{R})\cap\overline{S_{0}}\}_{i=1}^{l_{R}}
and
\{\mathcal{O}_{i}^{R}\}_{i=1}^{l_{R}}
, we carefully construct compact set
X^{\varepsilon}
from which the backward in time trajectory for given finite length is “good”. (See Lemma
4.16
for precise statement.) Unfortunately, however,
X^{\varepsilon}
still contains some phase points whose trajectory becomes inflection grazing or concave grazing. For these cases, the backward in time trajectory is not analytic. In fact, we can expect Hölder regularity at most. So we define

\begin{split}B^{\varepsilon}_{L}&:=\left\{(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon}:\exists k\in\mathbb{N}:(x^{k},v^{k-1}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{I-},\ \text{and}\ \sum_{j=1}^{k}|x^{j}(x,v)-x^{j-1}(x,v)|\leq L\right\},\\
\end{split}

which is proved to be closed set. By compactness of
B_{L}^{\varepsilon}
and continuity of trajectory, we claim that there exist
K_{\varepsilon,L}<\infty
such that the backward in time trajectory from
(x,v)\in B_{L}^{\varepsilon}
does not generate inflection grazing after its
K_{\varepsilon,L}
bounces up to length
L
. Motivated by this fact, we define the following sets

\displaystyle(G_{1})_{x}

\displaystyle:=\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon},\ (x^{1}(x,v),v^{0}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I}\},

\displaystyle(B_{1})_{x}

\displaystyle:=\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon},\ (x^{1}(x,v),v^{0}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I}\},

\displaystyle(G_{j})_{x}

\displaystyle:=\{v\in(G_{j-1})_{x}:(x^{j}(x,v),v^{j-1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I}\},

\displaystyle(B_{j})_{x}

\displaystyle:=\{v\in(G_{j-1})_{x}:(x^{j}(x,v),v^{j-1}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I}\},

for all
1\leq j\leq K_{\varepsilon,L}
for a fixed point
x\in\overline{\Omega}
. We note that the backward in time trajectory does not graze until its
j
-th bounce and is analytic if
(x,v)\in(G_{j})_{x}
. And then combining all
\{(B_{j})_{x}\}_{j=1}^{K}
and using continuity, we construct a finite open cover
\{B(x_{i}^{\mathcal{K}},r_{i}^{\mathcal{K}})\cap\overline{S_{0}}\}_{i=1}^{l_{\mathcal{K}}}
and corresponding bad direction sets
\{\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\mathcal{K}}\}_{i=1}^{l_{\mathcal{K}}}
such that the backward in times trajectory from
(x,v)
avoids being
\gamma_{0}^{I}
or
\gamma_{0}^{C}

\text{ if $x\in B(x_{i}^{\mathcal{K}},r_{i}^{\mathcal{K}})\cap\overline{S_{0}}$ and $v\notin\bigcap_{j\in I_{x}^{\mathcal{K}}}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathcal{K}}$},

where
I_{x}^{\mathcal{K}}
is defined in (
4.78
). As we constructed
X^{\varepsilon}
before, we can also construct an open set
\mathcal{K}
and compact set
\mathcal{K}^{c}
so that for
\mathcal{K}^{c}
, trajectory does not contain converging bounces and does not generate inflection grazing up to length
L
. And using compactness and continuity again, we obtain the uniform number of bounces
M_{\varepsilon,L}
away from
\mathcal{K}
. See Lemma
4.22
.

Step 3.
(
(B_{j})_{x}
has measure zero) Roughly speaking, each
\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\mathcal{K}}
must contain all
\bigcup_{x\in B(x_{i}^{\mathcal{K}},r_{i}^{\mathcal{K}})}\bigcup_{j=1}^{M}(B_{j})_{x}
. Therefore, we should claim
\mathfrak{m}_{2}((B_{j})_{x})=0
to conclude that
\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\mathcal{K}}
can be chosen arbitrarily small. Here, let us briefly explain
\gamma_{0}^{I}
case which is easier than
\gamma_{0}^{C}
case. (See Proposition
5.2
.) We crucially use axial symmetry of
\Omega
. Throughout one-to-one corresponding property between
(\theta,\varphi)
(parametrization of inflection grazing) or
(x,v)\in B_{L}^{\varepsilon}
(when trajectory is well-defined and there are no converging bounces), we define

F_{j}(\theta,\varphi):=X(s(\theta,\varphi);0,\sigma(\theta,\varphi),I^{2}(\theta,\varphi))-x,

(2.1)

when forward in time trajectory from
(\sigma(\theta,\varphi),I^{2}(\theta,\varphi))
passes
\overline{S_{0}}
after
j
-th bouncing. Here
s(\theta,\varphi)
is arrival time function which measures traveling time from
(y,u)=(\sigma(\theta,\varphi),I^{2}(\theta,\varphi))\in\gamma_{0}^{I_{-}}
to
S_{0}
. See Lemma
4.12
for detail. Once we prove that
F_{j}
is analytic, we have the following dichotomy (a) or (b) :

(a) If the above function is identically zero, all the inflection grazing which pass
x
yield
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(B_{j})_{x}>0
in general. Fortunately, however, we can exclude such cases, away from small sets, using the axial symmetry structure of
\Omega
. From the following observation,

\frac{\partial X(s;0,\tau,\varphi,\eta)}{\partial\varphi}=C\hat{\varphi}(0),\quad\text{where $\hat{\varphi}(0)$ is defined in Definition \ref {cross section}},

the direction of
V(s(\theta,\varphi);0,\sigma(\theta,\varphi),I^{2}(\theta,\varphi))
must satisfy some specific direction which we can exclude in the definition of
X^{\varepsilon}
.

(b) Therefore, from analyticity, the zero set of (
2.1
) has measure zero in
(\theta,\varphi)
space. And by Lusin’s Theorem (Lemma
5.1
), corresponding
v=V(s(\theta,\varphi);0,\sigma(\theta,\varphi),I^{2}(\theta,\varphi))
has also measure zero in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

Treating concave grazing
\gamma_{0}^{C}
in
(B_{j})_{x}
is more tricky since concave grazing is parametrized by
(\theta,\varphi,\tau)
for
0<\tau<1
. We carefully apply analytic version of implicit function theorem to identify dimension between domain and target space. (Note that we need the same dimension between domain and range to apply Lusin’s theorem.) See Proposition
5.2
for detail. At last, after taking a small neighborhood and performing some simple scaling for general speed
\frac{1}{N}\leq|v|\leq N
, we obtain the phase decomposition Theorem
5.7
which says our trajectory up to fixed length is nongrazing away from small sets as follows.

Theorem 2.1

(Informal statement of Theorem
5.7
)
.

There exists a compact set
\mathcal{Z}^{\varepsilon}\subset\overline{S_{\varphi}}\times\mathbb{V}^{N}
such that if
(x,v)\in\mathcal{Z}^{\varepsilon}
, then the backward in time trajectory from
(x,v)
up to length
L
is uniformly nongrazing with uniformly finite number for bounce. Moreover, for each
x\in\overline{\Omega}
,

\mathfrak{m}_{3}\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\ :\ (x,v)\in\{\overline{S_{\varphi}}\times\mathbb{V}^{N}\}\backslash\mathcal{Z}^{\varepsilon}\}\lesssim\varepsilon\ll 1.

We lastly give some comments about
sticky grazing points
which were introduced in
[
26
]
. Sticky grazing points are a point
x\in\overline{\Omega}
from which backward in time trajectory with some directions of considerable measure in
\mathbb{V}^{N}
hits boundary grazingly,
\gamma_{0}^{I}
or
\gamma_{0}^{C}
. In the case of a general 2D analytic non-convex domain such points exist in general, but at most uniformly finite number of such points only. Such an issue can be resolved by choosing an arbitrarily small neighborhood around the sticky grazing point and considering small traveling time of the trajectory near the point. In this paper, however, axial symmetry plays a crucial role to confine all such directions in a measure zero set as shown in
\mathfrak{m}_{2}((B_{j})_{x})=0
.

Step 4.
(Triple iteration technique and constructive
L^{2}
-coercivity)
Nondegeneracy condition

\Big|\det\frac{\partial X(s;t,x,v)}{\partial v}\Big|\geq\epsilon>0

(2.2)

is the heart of
L^{2}
-
L^{\infty}
bootstrap argument. The most generalized tool to obtain (
2.2
) is the triple iteration technique which was introduced in
[
25
]
. Once we have phase decomposition theorem stated above (or see Theorem
5.7
for full statement), we can apply triple Duhamel iteration analysis to obtain nondegeneracy (
2.2
) away from some bad sets. Performing triple Duhamel iteration in
[
25
]
is very complicated and lengthy. In this paper, we do not provide full detail of triple iteration process, because we can directly adopt many key computation and Lemmas from
[
25
]
.

In this paper, we often use the following convenient notation:

Definition 2.2
.

(Big O notation)
Let
f(x)
and
g(x)
be two real-valued functions defined on some subset
\subset\mathbb{R}
. For some
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, we say

\displaystyle f(x)=\mathcal{O}(g(x)),\quad as\;x\rightarrow x_{0},

if there exist positive numbers
\delta
and
M
such that

\displaystyle|f(x)|\leq M|g(x)|\quad when\;0<|x-x_{0}|<\delta,

where
M
is a generic constant. For some variable
y\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

\displaystyle f(x)=\mathcal{O}_{y}(g(x)),\quad as\;x\rightarrow x_{0},

if there exist positive numbers
\delta
and
M_{y}
(depending on
y
) such that

\displaystyle|f(x)|\leq M_{y}|g(x)|\quad when\;0<|x-x_{0}|<\delta.

3.
Phase Boundary Decomposition

Recall that the analytic convex curve
\gamma(\tau)=(\gamma_{1}(\tau),0,\gamma_{2}(\tau))
satisfies
\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)<0
for
\tau\in(\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*})
where
\tau_{1,*}
and
\tau_{2,*}
be the zeros of
\gamma_{2}^{\prime}
, see Figure
1.36
. Also, we assume that
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)<0
for
\tau\in(\tau_{1,*},\lambda_{*})
and
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)>0
for
\tau\in(\lambda_{*},\tau_{2,*})
. Here,
\lambda_{*}
be the zero of
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

[
16
]

Suppose that
f
is a non-constant real-analytic function on a connected open domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, the zero set

\displaystyle Z_{f}:=\{x\in D:f(x)=0\}

has zero
n
-dimensional Lebesgue measure. If
n=1
, the zero set
Z(f)
has no limit point. Moreover,
f
has only finitely many zeros on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

From Lemma 4 in
[
16
]
, the zero set
Z_{f}
has zero
n
-dimensional Lebesgue measure and has no limit point in
\mathbb{R}
. Thus, it suffices to show that a non-constant real analytic function
f
in a compact region has finitely many zeros. If
f
has infinitely many zeros in a compact region
K\subset\mathbb{R}
, then there is a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

\displaystyle f(x_{n})=0\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\quad\text{and}\quad x_{n}\rightarrow x_{*}\in K

by compactness. Due to continuity of
f
, it holds that
f(x_{*})=0
. However, in
\mathbb{R}
, the zero set of identically nonzero real-analytic function has no limit point, which leads to a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

[
31
]

(Euler’s formula) Let
\gamma
be a regularized (unit speed) curve on an oriented surface
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The scalar

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}(\tau):=\ddot{\gamma}(\tau)\cdot n(\gamma(\tau)),

is called the normal curvature of
\gamma
where
n(\gamma(\tau))
is a fixed unit normal vector of
\mathcal{S}
at
\gamma(\tau)\in\mathcal{S}
. If
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
be the principal curvatures with non-zero principal vectors
\mathbf{t}_{1}
and
\mathbf{t}_{2}
, then the normal curvature
\kappa_{n}
of
\gamma
is

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}=\kappa_{1}\cos^{2}\vartheta+\kappa_{2}\sin^{2}\vartheta,

where
\vartheta
is the oriented angle between vectors
\mathbf{t}_{1}
and
\dot{\gamma}
.

The following lemma is obvious every point is a saddle point in inner part
\tau_{1,*}<\tau<\tau_{2,*}
.

Lemma 3.3
.

(\gamma(\tau),\dot{\gamma}(\tau))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I}
only if
\tau_{1,*}<\tau<\tau_{2,*}
. (See Section 1.1 and Figure
1.36
for the definition of
\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*}
.)

Definition 3.4
.

At
\sigma(\tau_{0},0)=(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0}),0,\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))\in\partial\Omega\;(\varphi=0)
, the outward normal vector
N_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
is

\displaystyle N_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}=(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0}),0,-\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})).

In general,
N_{\sigma(\tau,\varphi)}
denotes the outward normal vector at
\sigma(\tau,\varphi)
throughout this paper. We define the tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
at
\sigma(\tau_{0},0)
as

\displaystyle T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}:=\Big\{(x,y,z):x=\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})+\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}(z-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))\Big\}.

We define a new 2D coordinate of the tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
,

\displaystyle(\tilde{y},\tilde{z}):=\Big(y,\frac{z}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\Big),\quad\text{for }(x,y,z)\in T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}.

(3.1)

Moreover, direction
\hat{\tilde{y}},\hat{\tilde{z}}
of
\tilde{y},\tilde{z}
are

\displaystyle\hat{\tilde{y}}=\hat{y},\quad\hat{\tilde{z}}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta_{*}&0&\sin\theta_{*}\\
0&1&0\\
-\sin\theta_{*}&0&\cos\theta_{*}\end{pmatrix}\hat{z},

(3.2)

where
\hat{y}=(0,1,0),\hat{z}=(0,0,1)
and
\theta_{*}
satisfies that
\cos\theta_{*}=|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|
. Using the new coordinate (
3.1
) and direction (
3.2
) of the tangent space
T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
, we have the following coordinate map

\displaystyle(x,y,z)\mapsto y\hat{\tilde{y}}+\frac{z}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\hat{\tilde{z}},

(3.3)

for
(x,y,z)\in T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
.

Remark 3.5
.

In (
3.3
),
\hat{\tilde{y}}
and
\hat{\tilde{z}}
satisfy that

\displaystyle N_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}\cdot\hat{\tilde{y}}=(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0}),0,-\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0}))\cdot(0,1,0)=0,

\displaystyle N_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}\cdot\hat{\tilde{z}}=(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0}),0,-\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0}))\cdot(\sin\theta_{*},0,\cos\theta_{*})=0,

where we have used
\cos\theta_{*}=-\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})
and
\sin\theta_{*}=-\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})
. Hence, we can consider
y\hat{\tilde{y}}+\frac{z}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\hat{\tilde{z}}
in (
3.3
) as a coordinate in the tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
. The reason why we set
\tilde{z}
in (
3.1
) will be explained through Figure
6
. Figure
6
shows the tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
at
\sigma(\tau_{0},0)
in
xz
-plane
(\varphi=0)
. Since
\cos\theta_{*}=N_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}\cdot(-1,0,0)=|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|
,
\tilde{z}
was put as
\frac{z}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}
. Throughout this paper, points in
T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
can be expressed through a new coordinate system such as (
3.3
).

Figure 6
.
New coordinate
\tilde{y},\tilde{z}
of the tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
(direction of
\hat{y}=\hat{\tilde{y}}
is inward to the paper)

3.1.
Inflection grazing set

Now, we study local inflection structure.

Lemma 3.6
.

Assume that
\tau_{1,*}
and
\tau_{2,*}
are zeros of
\gamma_{2}^{\prime}
for the unit-speed analytic convex curve
\gamma(\tau)=(\gamma_{1}(\tau),0,\gamma_{2}(\tau))
. Let us define

\displaystyle Z_{h}:=\Big\{\tau\in(\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*})\ :\ h(\tau):=\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)}{\gamma_{1}(\tau)}(\gamma_{1}(\tau)\kappa(\tau)+|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)|)+\frac{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)|\kappa^{\prime}(\tau)}{3\kappa(\tau)}=0\Big\},

(3.4)

where

\displaystyle\kappa(\tau):=\sqrt{(\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau))^{2}+(\gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau))^{2}}

(3.5)

is the curvature of a unit-speed curve
\gamma
at
\tau
. Then, the zero set
Z_{h}
is a finite union of points, denoted by

\displaystyle Z_{h}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell}\{\tau_{j}^{\mathbf{z}}\},\quad\text{for some finite }\ell=\ell_{\Omega}<\infty.

(3.6)

Moreover, for
(\tau,\varphi)\in(\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*})\backslash Z_{h}\times[0,2\pi)
, there exist two directions
I^{1}(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi)

\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
which satisfy

\displaystyle(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{1}(\tau,\varphi))\in\gamma_{0}^{I_{+}},\quad(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))\in\gamma_{0}^{I_{-}},

and have positivie angular momentums with respect to
z
-axis. (See Figure
4
.) Here,
\gamma_{0}^{\pm}
are defined in (
1.44
). In other words,
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{k}(\tau,\varphi))\in\gamma_{0}^{I}
for
k=1,2
.

Proof.

Step 1.
In this step, we consider the intersection of the boundary
\partial\Omega
and the tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
at point
\sigma(\tau_{0},0)
. Here, the tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
was defined in Definition
3.4
. The aim of this step is to calculate a tangent line of the intersection (or
\frac{d\tilde{z}}{dy}
) at
\sigma(\tau_{0},0)
.

To find where inflection grazing occurs, we only focus the domain
\tau\in(\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*})
by Lemma
3.3
. Also, to ensure
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau),\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)<0
, we restrict our domain from
(\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*})
to
(\tau_{1,*},\lambda_{*})
where
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\lambda_{*})=0
. Since our domain
\Omega
is symmetric about the
z
-axis, it would be enough to consider
\varphi=0
case without loss of generality. Recall that
\gamma
is a unit speed curve. For
\tau_{0}\in(\tau_{1,*},\lambda_{*})
, we calculate a outward normal vector
N_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
at
\sigma(\tau_{0},0)=(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0}),0,\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))\in\partial\Omega

\displaystyle N_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}=(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0}),0,-\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})).

(3.7)

Then, the tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
at
\sigma(\tau_{0},0)
is

\displaystyle\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:x=\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})+\tan\theta_{0}(z-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))\},\quad\text{where }\tan\theta_{0}=\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}.

(3.8)

In general, a smooth curve
(x(\tau),y(\tau),z(\tau))\in\partial\Omega
on the general toroidal domain satisfies

\displaystyle\sqrt{x^{2}(\tau)+y^{2}(\tau)}=\gamma_{1}(\tau),\quad z(\tau)=\gamma_{2}(\tau).

(3.9)

As we mentioned before, we consider the intersection of the boundary
\partial\Omega
and the tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
at
\sigma(\tau_{0},0)
. Since our domain is axisymmetric about the
z
-axis, we firstly consider the case
\left(y>0,\tilde{z}>\frac{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\right)
where
\tilde{z}
in Definition
3.4
. From (
3.8
) and (
3.9
), we denote the intersection as

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}:=\left\{(x,y,z)=(x(\tau),y(\tau),z(\tau)):\begin{array}[]{cc}x(\tau)=\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})+\tan\theta_{0}(z(\tau)-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})),\\
y(\tau)=\sqrt{\gamma_{1}^{2}(\tau)-x^{2}(\tau)}\end{array}\right\}.

Using the new coordinate (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) in the tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
, the intersection
\mathcal{I}_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
becomes

\displaystyle\Pi_{\widetilde{yz}}I_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}:=\Big\{(\tilde{y},\tilde{z})_{\widetilde{yz}}=\Big(y,\frac{z}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\Big)_{\widetilde{yz}}:(x,y,z)\in\mathcal{I}_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}\Big\},

where
(a,b)_{\widetilde{yz}}=a\hat{\tilde{y}}+b\hat{\tilde{z}}
. Especially, the point
\sigma(\tau_{0},0)=(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0}),0,\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))\in T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
becomes

\displaystyle\Pi_{\widetilde{yz}}\sigma(\tau_{0},0):=\frac{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\hat{\tilde{z}}.

(3.12)

We check whether the tangent line
T_{\ell}(y)
in (
3.22
) to the intersection
\Pi_{\widetilde{yz}}\mathcal{I}_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
at
\Pi_{\widetilde{yz}}\sigma(\tau_{0},0)
has the direction of which the sign of normal curvature
\kappa_{n}
in Lemma
3.2
changes.

Recall that boundary
\partial\Omega
can be represented by
\{(x,y,z):\xi(\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}},z)=0\}
using (
1.36
). Then, equation of the intersection
\Pi_{\widetilde{yz}}\mathcal{I}_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
becomes

\displaystyle\xi\left(\sqrt{\left(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})+\tan\theta_{0}\left(|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|\tilde{z}-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})\right)\right)^{2}+y^{2}},|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|\tilde{z}\right)=0.

(3.13)

Since it is hard to calculate
\frac{d\tilde{z}}{dy}
at
\tau=\tau_{0}
directly from (
3.13
), we calculate
\frac{d\tilde{z}}{dy}
at
\tau=\tau_{0}
using the Chain rule and (
3.9
). Due to
\left.\frac{d\tilde{z}}{dy}\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}=\frac{1}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\left.\frac{dz}{dy}\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}
, it suffices to compute
\left.\frac{dz}{dy}\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}
. To apply the Chain rule, we calculate
\frac{dz}{d\tau}
and
\frac{dy}{d\tau}
. From (
3.9
),

\displaystyle y(\tau)=\sqrt{\gamma_{1}^{2}(\tau)-x^{2}(\tau)},\quad y^{\prime}(\tau)=\frac{\gamma_{1}(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)-x(\tau)x^{\prime}(\tau)}{\sqrt{\gamma_{1}^{2}(\tau)-x^{2}(\tau)}},\quad z^{\prime}(\tau)=\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau).

(3.14)

Then,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left.\frac{dz}{dy}\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}=\left.\frac{\frac{dz}{d\tau}}{\frac{dy}{d\tau}}\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}&=\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\tau_{0}-}\frac{\sqrt{\gamma_{1}^{2}(\tau)-x^{2}(\tau)}}{\gamma_{1}(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)-x(\tau)x^{\prime}(\tau)}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)\\
&=\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\tau_{0}-}\frac{\sqrt{|\gamma_{1}(\tau)-x(\tau)|}\sqrt{\gamma_{1}(\tau)+x(\tau)}}{\gamma_{1}(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)-x(\tau)x^{\prime}(\tau)}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau).\end{split}

(3.15)

Using Taylor’s expansion at
\tau=\tau_{0}
, (
3.9
), and (
3.1
), we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(\tau)-x(\tau)

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})+\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})}{2}(\tau-\tau_{0})^{2}+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma_{1}}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{3})\right)

\displaystyle\quad-(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})+\tan\theta_{0}(\gamma_{2}(\tau)-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})+\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})}{2}(\tau-\tau_{0})^{2}+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma_{1}}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{3})\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})+\tan\theta_{0}\gamma^{\prime}_{2}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\tan\theta_{0}\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{2}(\tau_{0})}{2}(\tau-\tau_{0})^{2}+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma_{2}}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{3})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})-\tan\theta_{0}\gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})\right)(\tau-\tau_{0})^{2}+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})}{2|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}(\tau-\tau_{0})^{2}+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{3}),

(3.16)

where we used

\displaystyle(\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0}),\gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0}))=\kappa(\tau_{0})(-\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0}),\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})),

(3.17)

\tan\theta_{0}=\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}
from (
3.8
), and
\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})=-|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|
for
\tau_{0}\in(\tau_{1,*},\lambda_{*})
. Using
\tan\theta_{0}=-\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}
, (
3.1
), (
3.17
) and Taylor expansion at
\tau=\tau_{0}
, we derive

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)-x(\tau)x^{\prime}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\Big(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})+\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma_{1}}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2})\Big)\times\Big(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})+\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma_{1}}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-\Big(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})+\tan\theta_{0}(\gamma_{2}(\tau)-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))\Big)\times(\tan\theta_{0}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau))

\displaystyle=\Big(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})+\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0}))^{2}(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma_{1}}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-\Big(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})+\tan\theta_{0}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma_{2}}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big(\tan\theta_{0}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})+\tan\theta_{0}\gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\tan\theta_{0}\mathcal{O}_{\gamma_{2}}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2})\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})-\tan\theta_{0}\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})\Big)(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2}).

(3.18)

Then, plugging (
3.1
) and (
3.1
) into
\frac{\sqrt{|\gamma_{1}(\tau)-x(\tau)|}}{\gamma_{1}(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)-x(\tau)x^{\prime}(\tau)}
of (
3.15
), we have for
\tau-\tau_{0}<0

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{|\gamma_{1}(\tau)-x(\tau)|}}{\gamma_{1}(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)-x(\tau)x^{\prime}(\tau)}=\frac{\sqrt{\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})}{2|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}+\mathcal{O}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|)}}{-\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}+\mathcal{O}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|)}.

(3.19)

From (
3.15
) and (
3.19
), we derive that

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\tau_{0}-}\frac{dz}{dy}=\sqrt{\frac{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}}|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|,\quad\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\tau_{0}-}\frac{d\tilde{z}}{dy}=\sqrt{\frac{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}},

(3.20)

where
\tilde{z}=\frac{z}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}
. Using the first and second fundamental forms of the surface patch
\sigma
at
(\tau,\varphi)=(\tau_{0},0)
, the principal curvatures are the roots of the equation

\displaystyle\left.\det\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{\tau\tau}\cdot N_{\sigma(\tau,\varphi)}-\kappa(\sigma_{\tau}\cdot\sigma_{\tau})&\sigma_{\tau\varphi}\cdot N_{\sigma(\tau,\varphi)}-\kappa(\sigma_{\tau}\cdot\sigma_{\varphi})\\
\sigma_{\tau\varphi}\cdot N_{\sigma(\tau,\varphi)}-\kappa(\sigma_{\tau}\cdot\sigma_{\varphi})&\sigma_{\varphi\varphi}\cdot N_{\sigma(\tau,\varphi)}-\kappa(\sigma_{\varphi}\cdot\sigma_{\varphi})\end{pmatrix}\right|_{(\tau,\varphi)=(\tau_{0},0)}

\displaystyle=\det\begin{pmatrix}\kappa(\tau_{0})-\kappa&0\\
0&\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})-\kappa(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0}))^{2}\end{pmatrix}=0,

where
\kappa
is curvature of
\gamma
as in (
3.5
). Thus, the principal curvatures of
\sigma
at
(\tau,\varphi)=(\tau_{0},0)
are

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}(\tau_{0})=\frac{\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})},\quad\kappa_{2}(\tau_{0})=\kappa(\tau_{0}).

Note that the principal vectors corresponding to the principal curvature
\kappa
are
\xi\sigma_{\tau}+\eta\sigma_{\varphi}
such that

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\kappa(\tau_{0})-\kappa&0\\
0&\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})-\kappa(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0}))^{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\xi\\
\eta\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

Since
\kappa_{2}(\tau_{0})-\kappa_{1}(\tau_{0})\neq 0
, the principal vector corresponding to
\kappa_{1}(\tau_{0})=\frac{\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}
is
\sigma_{\varphi}(\tau_{0},0)=(0,\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0}),0)
. Then, by using Lemma
3.2
and
\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})=-|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|
, the normal curvature
\kappa_{n}
of
\gamma
at
\tau=\tau_{0}
is zero if

\displaystyle\tan\vartheta=\sqrt{\frac{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}},

(3.21)

where
\vartheta
is an angle between the
y
-axis (the principal vector corresponding to
\kappa_{1}
) and
\dot{\gamma}
. Hence, the tangent line
T_{\ell}
to the intersection
\Pi_{\widetilde{yz}}\mathcal{I}_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
at
\Pi_{\widetilde{yz}}\sigma(\tau_{0},0)
in (
3.12
) is

\displaystyle T_{\ell}(y)=\left.\frac{d\tilde{z}}{dy}\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}y+\frac{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}=(\tan\vartheta)y+\frac{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}.
