(3.22)

and from (
3.15
) and (
3.20
), we have

\displaystyle\tan\vartheta=\left.\frac{d\tilde{z}}{dy}\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}=\frac{1}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\left.\frac{dz}{dy}\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}=\sqrt{\frac{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}},

and the normal curvature
\kappa_{n}=0
on the tangent line
T_{\ell}
define in (
3.22
). See
I_{1}
direction in the Figure
7
.

In terms of defining normal curvature in Lemma
3.2
, let us choose inward normal vector
-N_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
instead of outward normal vector for convenience. Then, in the upper region of the tangent line
T_{\ell}(y)

\displaystyle\left\{({y},\tilde{z}):{y}>0,\tilde{z}>\frac{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|},\tilde{z}>T_{\ell}({y})\right\},

a direction of which slope larger than
\tan\vartheta
in
\tilde{y}\tilde{z}
-plane has a positive normal curvature (
\kappa_{n}>0
) at
\tau=\tau_{0}
. Similarly, in the lower region of the tangent line
T_{\ell}

\displaystyle\left\{(y,\tilde{z}):y>0,\tilde{z}>\frac{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|},\tilde{z}<T_{\ell}(y)\right\},

a direction of which slope lesser than
\tan\vartheta
in
\tilde{y}\tilde{z}
-plane has a negative normal curvature (
\kappa_{n}<0
) at
\tau=\tau_{0}
.

Step 2.
Next, we calculate
\frac{d^{2}\tilde{z}}{dy^{2}}
at
\tau=\tau_{0}
and check when it becomes zero. Using chain rule,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}z}{dy^{2}}=\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\frac{\frac{dz}{d\tau}}{\frac{dy}{d\tau}}\right)\frac{1}{\frac{dy}{d\tau}}=\frac{z^{\prime\prime}(\tau)y^{\prime}(\tau)-z^{\prime}(\tau)y^{\prime\prime}(\tau)}{(y^{\prime}(\tau))^{3}}.

(3.23)

Notice that, from (
3.14
) and (
3.17
), as
\tau\rightarrow\tau_{0}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}z^{\prime\prime}(\tau)&=\gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau)=\kappa(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)\rightarrow\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0}),\\
y^{\prime}(\tau)&=\frac{\gamma_{1}(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)-x(\tau)x^{\prime}(\tau)}{\sqrt{\gamma_{1}^{2}(\tau)-x^{2}(\tau)}}\rightarrow-\sqrt{\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}},\\
z^{\prime}(\tau)&=\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)\rightarrow\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0}),\end{split}

(3.24)

where the limit of
y^{\prime}(\tau)
comes from (
3.19
). Thus, to obtain
\frac{d^{2}z}{dy^{2}}
at
\tau=\tau_{0}
, it suffices to compute
\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\tau_{0}}y^{\prime\prime}(\tau)
. From (
3.14
), we have

\displaystyle y(\tau)y^{\prime}(\tau)=\gamma_{1}(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)-x(\tau)x^{\prime}(\tau).

Taking derivative yields that

\displaystyle\begin{split}y(\tau)y^{\prime\prime}(\tau)+(y^{\prime}(\tau))^{2}&=(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau))^{2}+\gamma_{1}(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau)-(x^{\prime}(\tau))^{2}-x(\tau)x^{\prime\prime}(\tau)\\
&=(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)-x^{\prime}(\tau))(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)+x^{\prime}(\tau))+(\gamma_{1}(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau)-x(\tau)x^{\prime\prime}(\tau))\\
&:=I+II.\end{split}

(3.25)

For part
I
above, we use Taylor expansion at
\tau=\tau_{0}
,
\tan\theta_{0}=-\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}
, and (
3.1
):

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)-x^{\prime}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})+\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2})\right)-\left(\tan\theta_{0}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})+\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2})\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\tan\theta_{0}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})+\tan\theta_{0}\gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})-\tan\theta_{0}\gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})\right)(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2}).

(3.26)

Thus, from (
3.1
), the part
I
be

\displaystyle I=(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)+x^{\prime}(\tau))\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2}).

(3.27)

For part
II
, using (
3.1
) and (
3.17
), each term in
II
will be represented by Taylor’s expansion:

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau)=-\kappa(\tau)\gamma_{1}(\tau)\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)

\displaystyle=-\kappa(\tau)\left(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})+\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2})\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})+\gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=-\kappa(\tau)\left(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})+(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})+\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0}))(\tau-\tau_{0})\right)+\mathcal{O}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2}),

(3.28)

\displaystyle x(\tau)x^{\prime\prime}(\tau)=\tan\theta_{0}x(\tau)\gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\tan\theta_{0}\kappa(\tau)x(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\tan\theta_{0}\kappa(\tau)(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})+\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2}))

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})+\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=\kappa(\tau)\tan\theta_{0}\left(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(\tau_{0})+(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})+(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0}))^{2})(\tau-\tau_{0})\right)+\mathcal{O}(|\tau-\tau_{0}|^{2}).

(3.29)

Thus, by (
3.28
) and (
3.29
), the part
II
becomes

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle=-\kappa(\tau)\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\left(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})+\tan\theta_{0}\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})\right)

(3.30)

\displaystyle-\kappa(\tau)\left(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})+\tan\theta_{0}\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})+\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})+\tan\theta_{0}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0}))^{2}\right)(\tau-\tau_{0})

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{O}((\tau-\tau_{0})^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa(\tau)\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}+\frac{\kappa(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}((\tau-\tau_{0})^{2}).

Hence, from (
3.25
),(
3.27
), and (
3.30
), one obtains that

\displaystyle y(\tau)y^{\prime\prime}(\tau)+(y^{\prime}(\tau))^{2}=I+II

(3.31)

\displaystyle=(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)+x^{\prime}(\tau))\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}(\tau-\tau_{0})+\frac{\kappa(\tau)\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}+\frac{\kappa(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}((\tau-\tau_{0})^{2}).

Also, from the calculation (
3.1
) and Taylor expansion at
\tau=\tau_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}y(\tau)&=\sqrt{\gamma_{1}^{2}(\tau)-x^{2}(\tau)}=\sqrt{\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})}{2|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}(\tau-\tau_{0})^{2}+\mathcal{O}((\tau-\tau_{0})^{3})}\sqrt{\gamma_{1}(\tau)+x(\tau)},\\
(y^{\prime}(\tau))^{2}&=(y^{\prime}(\tau_{0}))^{2}+2y^{\prime}(\tau_{0})y^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}((\tau-\tau_{0})^{2})\\
&=\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}+2y^{\prime}(\tau_{0})y^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})(\tau-\tau_{0})+\mathcal{O}((\tau-\tau_{0})^{2}).\end{split}

(3.32)

We substitute (
3.32
) for the left-hand side in (
3.31
), use (
3.24
), and then divide both sides by
|\tau-\tau_{0}|=-(\tau-\tau_{0})
to get

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})}{2|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}+\mathcal{O}(\tau-\tau_{0})}\sqrt{\gamma_{1}(\tau)+x(\tau)}y^{\prime\prime}(\tau)+2\sqrt{\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}}y^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\frac{\kappa(\tau)-\kappa(\tau_{0})}{\tau-\tau_{0}}-(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)+x^{\prime}(\tau))\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}-\frac{\kappa(\tau)\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}+\mathcal{O}(\tau-\tau_{0}).

As
\tau\rightarrow\tau_{0}
, one obtains that

\displaystyle 3\sqrt{\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}}y^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\kappa^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}-3\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|},

\displaystyle y^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1/3\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\kappa^{\prime}(\tau_{0})+\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{\sqrt{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}}.

(3.33)

Hence, (
3.23
),(
3.24
), and (
3.1
) yield
\frac{d^{2}z}{dy^{2}}
at
\tau=\tau_{0}
:

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d^{2}z}{dy^{2}}\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{z^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})y^{\prime}(\tau_{0})-z^{\prime}(\tau_{0})y^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{0})}{(y^{\prime}(\tau_{0}))^{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\kappa(\tau_{0}){\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}}+\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})\frac{1/3\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\kappa^{\prime}(\tau_{0})+\kappa(\tau_{0}){\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}}{\sqrt{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}}}{\left(-\sqrt{\frac{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}}\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}^{2}(\tau_{0})}(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\kappa(\tau_{0})+|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|)+\frac{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|^{2}\kappa^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{3\kappa^{2}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})},

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d^{2}\tilde{z}}{dy^{2}}\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}^{2}(\tau_{0})}(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\kappa(\tau_{0})+|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|)+\frac{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|\kappa^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{3\kappa^{2}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})},

where
\tilde{z}=\frac{z}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}
. Finally, at
\tau=\tau_{0}
, we derive

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d^{2}\tilde{z}}{dy^{2}}\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}^{2}(\tau_{0})}(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\kappa(\tau_{0})+|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|)+\frac{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|\kappa^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{3\kappa^{2}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}.

So far, we have only considered
\left(y>0,\tilde{z}>\frac{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\right)
case. Since our domain is
z
-axial symmetry, we have
\tilde{z}(y)=\tilde{z}(-y)
, which implies that
\frac{d\tilde{z}(y)}{dy}=-\frac{d\tilde{z}(-y)}{dy}
and
\frac{d^{2}\tilde{z}(y)}{dy^{2}}=\frac{d^{2}\tilde{z}(-y)}{dy^{2}}
. Thus, for the case
\left(y<0,\tilde{z}>\frac{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\right)
, we have

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d\tilde{z}}{dy}\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}=-\sqrt{\frac{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}},

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d^{2}\tilde{z}}{dy^{2}}\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}=\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{\kappa(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}^{2}(\tau_{0})}(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\kappa(\tau_{0})+|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|)+\frac{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|\kappa^{\prime}(\tau_{0})}{3\kappa^{2}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})}.

Because two cases
\left(y>0,\tilde{z}>\frac{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\right)
and
\left(y<0,\tilde{z}<\frac{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\right)
share the same values
\frac{d\tilde{z}}{dy}
and
\frac{d^{2}\tilde{z}}{dy^{2}}
at
\tau_{0}
, we obtain the values for the region
\left(y<0,\tilde{z}<\frac{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\right)
. On the other hand, for the case
\left(y>0,\tilde{z}<\frac{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\right)
, values
\frac{d\tilde{z}}{dy}
and
\frac{d^{2}\tilde{z}}{dy^{2}}
at
\tau_{0}
can be deduced from the case
\left(y<0,\tilde{z}>\frac{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})|}\right)
.

Step 3.
In this argument, we change the direction of the normal vector
N_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
from outward to inward. Thus, in Lemma
3.2
, we consider

\displaystyle n(\gamma(\tau_{0}))=(-\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{0}),0,\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{0})).

By Lemma
3.2
, the sign of the normal curvature
\kappa_{n}
at
\sigma(\tau_{0},0)
does not change if the direction
v\in T_{\sigma}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
at
\sigma(\tau_{0},0)
is between tangent lines. When an angle between
\hat{\tilde{y}}
and the direction
v
in the tangent space
T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
is in
(-\vartheta,\vartheta)\cup(\pi-\vartheta,\pi+\vartheta)
where
\vartheta
is defined in (
3.21
), the normal curvature
\kappa_{n}
is negative. Thus, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\xi(\sigma(\tau_{0},0)+tv)<0
for any
t\in(-\varepsilon,0)\cup(0,\varepsilon)
. By Remark
1.4
,
(\sigma(\tau_{0},0),v)
cannot be an inflection point.

Similarly, when an angle between
\hat{\tilde{y}}
and the direction
v
in the tangent space
T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
is in
(\vartheta,\pi-\vartheta)\cup(\pi+\vartheta,2\pi-\vartheta)
, the normal curvature
\kappa_{n}
is positive. Hence, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\xi(\sigma(\tau_{0},0)+tv)>0
for any
t\in(-\varepsilon,0)\cup(0,\varepsilon)
, so that
(\sigma(\tau_{0},0),v)
cannot be an inflection point. Thus,
(\sigma(\tau_{0},0),v)
can be an inflection point only if the direction
v
have angles
\vartheta
and
2\pi-\vartheta
in
T_{\sigma(\tau_{0},0)}
.

To occur an inflection grazing at
\sigma(\tau_{0},0)
,
\frac{d^{2}\tilde{z}}{dy^{2}}
at
\tau=\tau_{0}
must not be zero. Thus, we exclude the part that satisfies
\left.\frac{d^{2}\tilde{z}}{dy^{2}}\right|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}=0
. Since
\gamma
is an analytic convex curve and
\gamma_{1}(\tau)>0
for
\tau\in(\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*})
,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\tilde{z}}{dy^{2}}(\tau)=0\Leftrightarrow\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)}{\gamma_{1}(\tau)}(\gamma_{1}(\tau)\kappa(\tau)+|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)|)+\frac{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)|\kappa^{\prime}(\tau)}{3\kappa(\tau)}=0,

and the function

\displaystyle h(\tau):=\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau)}{\gamma_{1}(\tau)}(\gamma_{1}(\tau)\kappa(\tau)+|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)|)+\frac{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)|\kappa^{\prime}(\tau)}{3\kappa(\tau)}

is also analytic. Notice that if
1
-dimensional real analytic function is not identically zero, then the zero set has no limit point in
(\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*})
by Lemma
3.1
. Also,

\displaystyle h(\tau_{i,*})=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{i,*})\kappa(\tau_{i,*})\neq 0,\quad\text{for}\;i=1,2,

which means that the zero set
Z_{h}
of
h
in
(\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*})
is equal to the zero set in
[\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*}]
. Then, the zeros of
h(\tau)
in
(\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*})
consists of only finite points, say
\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell}\{\tau_{j}^{\mathbf{z}}\}
for some constant
\ell<\infty\;(\tau_{1,*}<\tau_{1}^{\mathbf{z}}<\tau_{2}^{\mathbf{z}}<\cdots<\tau_{\ell}^{\mathbf{z}}<\tau_{2,*})
.

Recall that we set an angle
\vartheta
satisfying
\tan\vartheta=\sqrt{\frac{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)|}{\kappa(\tau)\gamma_{1}(\tau)}}
. Due to axis-symmetry about
z
-axis and the specular reflection boundary condition, trajectory preserves the angular momentum. Thus, WLOG, we only consider trajectories with positive angular momentum. Then, for
\tau\in(\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*})\backslash\cup_{j=1}^{l}\{\tau_{j}^{\mathbf{z}}\}
and
\varphi\in[0,2\pi)
, we only consider two cases
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{1}(\tau,\varphi))
and
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))
where directions
I^{1}(\tau,\varphi)
and
I^{2}(\tau,\varphi)
have angles
\vartheta
and
2\pi-\vartheta
respectively in the tangent plane. By Remark
1.4
, we have
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{1}(\tau,\varphi))\in\gamma_{0}^{I_{+}}
and
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))\in\gamma_{0}^{I_{-}}
.
∎

Next, we introduce concave grazing directions using
I^{1}(\tau,\varphi)
and
I^{2}(\tau,\varphi)
defined in Lemma
3.6
. Although
I^{1}(\tau,\varphi)
and
I^{2}(\tau,\varphi)
were not defined for
\tau\in Z_{h}=\cup_{j=1}^{\ell}\{\tau_{z}^{\textbf{z}}\}
in Lemma
3.6
, we will define them as the directions in the tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau,\varphi)}
that make angles of
\vartheta
and
2\pi-\vartheta
(defined in the proof of Lemma
3.6
), respectively, to define the concave grazing direction at
\sigma(\tau,\varphi)
for
\tau\in Z_{h}
. Note that
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{1}(\tau,\varphi)),(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}
for
(\tau,\varphi)\in Z_{h}\times[0,2\pi)
.

Definition 3.7
.

At
\sigma(\tau,\varphi)\in\partial\Omega
, let the direction
v_{c}\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
be concave grazing direction which is parametrized by parameter
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}
. For
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v_{c}(\eta))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}
,
v_{c}
must be between directions
I^{1}(\tau,\varphi)
and
I^{2}(\tau,\varphi)
defined in Lemma
3.6
and above. Thus,
v_{c}
can be parametrized as

\displaystyle v_{c}=v_{c}(\eta):=\frac{\eta I^{1}(\tau,\varphi)+(1-\eta)I^{2}(\tau,\varphi)}{|\eta I^{1}(\tau,\varphi)+(1-\eta)I^{2}(\tau,\varphi)|},

(3.34)

for
0<\eta<1
. See Figure
7
. Directions between
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
in the shaded region corresponds to concave grazings.

Notice that the sign of the normal curvature is always negative if the direction at
\sigma(\tau,\varphi)
has
v_{c}
from Euler’s theorem. Thus, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\xi(\sigma(\tau,\varphi)+tv_{c}(\eta))<0
for all
t\in(-\varepsilon,0)\cup(0,\varepsilon)
. In other words,
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v_{c}(\eta))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}
.

4.
Control the Number of Bounces

4.1.
Finite number of bounce away from inflection grazing

Recall the definition of velocity set (
1.47
).
Let
(t,x,v)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{V}^{N}
. For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
x^{i}(x,v)\in\partial\Omega
can be parametrized as

\displaystyle x^{i}(x,v)=\sigma(\tau_{i},\varphi_{i}),\quad(\tau_{i},\varphi_{i})\in[a,b)\times(-2\pi\mathbf{w},0]:=U.

where
\mathbf{w}
defined in (
1.48
) with maximal travel length
L=NT
.

We define non-negative angular momentum (since we consider only trajectory with positive orientation WLOG) of
(x,v)
, i.e,

\begin{split}\omega(x,v)&:=|(x\times\hat{z})\cdot v|\\
&=\text{angular momentum of a trajectory with respect to $z$-axis which passes $(x,v)$}\\
&=\omega(X(s;t,x,v),V(s;t,x,v)).\end{split}

(4.1)

This implies that the angular momentum is conserved due to
z
-axial symmetric domain and specular reflection BC. For
x\in\Omega
, this (
4.1
) can be easily checked:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\omega(X(s;t,x,v),V(s;t,x,v))=\{(V\times\hat{z})\cdot V+(X\times\hat{z})\cdot 0\}\frac{X\times\hat{z}\cdot V}{|X\times\hat{z}\cdot V|}=0.

Now, we only consider the case
x\in\partial\Omega
. WLOG we assume
x\in\partial S_{0}
where
S_{0}
is
\varphi=0
cross section. It suffices to check that

\displaystyle|(x\times\hat{z})\cdot v|=|(x\times\hat{z})\cdot R(x)v|,

where
R(x)=I-2n(x)\otimes n(x)
. Since
x\times\hat{z}
is parallel to
\hat{\varphi}
which is normal to
n(x)
,

\displaystyle|(x\times\hat{z})\cdot R(x)v|=|(x\times\hat{z})\cdot(v-2(n(x)\cdot v)n(x))|=|(x\times\hat{z})\cdot v|.

Lemma 4.1
.

Define
\Delta\tau_{i}=\tau_{i}-\tau_{i-1}
and
\Delta\varphi_{i}=\varphi_{i}-\varphi_{i-1}
for
i=1,2,\cdots
. Let
\sigma_{i}:=\sigma(\tau_{i},\varphi_{i})\in\partial\Omega
with
\tau_{i}\in[\tau_{1,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}+\varepsilon]
for
i=0,1,\cdots
. If the trajectory hits
\sigma_{i-1}
and then
\sigma_{i}
, we denote the projection of
\sigma_{i-1}
into the tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau_{i},\varphi_{i})}
as
Proj\sigma_{i-1}
. We define an angle
\eta_{i}
between
\overrightarrow{\sigma_{i-1,\widetilde{yz}}\sigma_{i,\widetilde{yz}}}
and
\tilde{z}
-axis where
\sigma_{i-1,\widetilde{yz}}
and
\sigma_{i,\widetilde{yz}}
are representation of
Proj\sigma_{i-1}
and
\sigma_{i}
in
T_{\sigma(\tau_{i},\varphi_{i})}
using (
3.1
) and (
3.2
). See Figure
7
. (Note that we will specify the angle
\eta_{i}
in this proof (
4.5
).)
Assume

\displaystyle\eta_{i}\leq\frac{\pi}{2}-C\varepsilon\quad

\displaystyle\text{if }\tau_{i}\in[\tau_{1,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{1,*}+\varepsilon]\cup[\tau_{2,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}+\varepsilon],

(4.2)

in addition for all
i\geq 1
and fixed small
\varepsilon\ll 1
, where
C
is some generic constant.
There exists
\delta\ll 1
such that if there exists
I\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle|\Delta\tau_{i}|<\delta\quad\text{and}\quad|\Delta\varphi_{i}|<\delta,\quad\forall i\geq I,

(4.3)

we have

\displaystyle|\Delta\varphi_{i}|\leq C_{\varepsilon}|\Delta\tau_{i}|,\quad\forall i\geq I,

(4.4)

where
C_{\varepsilon}
is a constant depending on
\varepsilon
.

Remark 4.2
.

In Lemma
4.1
, we consider the trajectory segment between
\sigma_{0}
and
\sigma_{1}
(See Figure
7
). If
\tau_{1}\in[\tau_{1,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{1,*}+\varepsilon]\cup[\tau_{2,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}+\varepsilon]
, then
\vartheta_{1}=\tan^{-1}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})|}}\right)
is near
\frac{\pi}{2}
. So, the two directions
I_{1}(\tau_{1},\varphi_{1})
and
I_{2}(\tau_{1},\varphi_{1})
are very close and almost identical. Later, in Lemma
4.16
, we will exclude
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
directions uniformly using the angular momentum conservation.
(If
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small, near
\vartheta=\frac{\pi}{2}
directions are removed as in shaded region in Figure
7
.) Hence, for
\tau_{1}\in[\tau_{1,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{1,*}+\varepsilon]\cup[\tau_{2,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}+\varepsilon]
, we assume that
\eta_{1}\leq\frac{\pi}{2}-C\varepsilon
for some constant
C>0
and
\varepsilon\ll 1
. If
\tau_{1}\in[\tau_{1,*}+\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}-\varepsilon]
, then direction of
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
are uniformly away from
\eta=\frac{\pi}{2}
angle.

Figure 7
.
The trajectory between
\sigma_{0}
and
\sigma_{1}
in the tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau_{1},\varphi_{1})}
(
\tau_{1}
near
\tau_{1,*}
or
\tau_{2,*}
)

Proof.

For notational convenience, we set
I=1
and consider two boundary points
\sigma_{0}
and
\sigma_{1}

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}:=\sigma(\tau_{i},\varphi_{i})=(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{i})\cos\varphi_{i},\gamma_{1}(\tau_{i})\sin\varphi_{i},\gamma_{2}(\tau_{i})),\quad i=0,1,

satisfying
|\Delta\tau_{1}|,|\Delta\varphi_{1}|\ll 1
. Since our domain
\Omega
is symmetric with respect to
\varphi
, we set
\varphi_{0}=-\varphi
and
\varphi_{1}=0
to satisfy
\Delta\varphi_{1}=\varphi
. The normal vector
N_{\sigma(\tau_{1},0)}
at
\sigma_{1}
is

\displaystyle N_{\sigma(\tau_{1},0)}=(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}),0,-\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})),

and the tangent plane at
\sigma_{1}
is

\displaystyle\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(x-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1}))-\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(z-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1}))=0\}.

We project the point
\sigma_{0}
into the tangent plane at
\sigma_{1}
and calculate the projected point

\displaystyle Proj\sigma_{0}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{0}-((\sigma_{0}-\sigma_{1})\cdot N_{\sigma(\tau_{1},0)})N_{\sigma(\tau_{1},0)}

\displaystyle=(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\cos\varphi-A\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}),-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\sin\varphi,\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})+A\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})),

where
A=(\sigma_{0}-\sigma_{1})\cdot N_{\sigma(\tau_{1},0)}=(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\cos\varphi-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1}))\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})+(\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})
. Using the new coordinate (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) in the tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau_{1},0)}
at
\sigma_{1}
, the points
Proj\sigma_{0}
and
\sigma_{1}
become

\displaystyle Proj\sigma_{0}\rightarrow\sigma_{0,\widetilde{yz}}=\Big(-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\sin\varphi,\frac{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})+A\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})|}\Big)_{\widetilde{yz}},\quad\sigma_{1}\rightarrow\sigma_{1,\widetilde{yz}}=\Big(0,\frac{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})|}\Big)_{\widetilde{yz}},

where
(a,b)_{\widetilde{yz}}=a\hat{\tilde{y}}+b\hat{\tilde{z}}
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\eta_{1}
be the angle between the
\tilde{z}
-axis and
\overrightarrow{\sigma_{0,\widetilde{yz}}\sigma_{1,\widetilde{yz}}}
, and then

\displaystyle\tan\eta_{1}=\frac{\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})|\sin\varphi}{\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})-A\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})}.

(4.5)

Using Taylor expansion yields that

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})-A\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=(\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))(1-(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})+(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\cos\varphi)\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=(\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}+(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0}))\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})

\displaystyle\quad+(1-\cos\varphi)\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(\Delta\tau_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varphi^{2})),

where we have used
(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}+(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}=1
.

Meanwhile, note that we are assuming
|\Delta\varphi_{1}|,|\Delta\tau_{1}|\ll 1
. In Figure
8
, imagine a sufficiently small circle
B_{1}
near
\sigma_{1}
so that
\sigma_{0}
locates inside of
B_{1}
. Since the trajectory hits
\sigma_{0}
and
\sigma_{1}
,
(\sigma_{1},\overrightarrow{\sigma_{0}\sigma_{1}})
must belong to
\gamma_{0}
or
\gamma_{-}
, i.e.,
(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0})\cdot N_{\sigma(\tau_{1},0)}\geq 0
. (Shaded region in Figure
8
)

Figure 8
.
The trajectory between
\sigma_{0}
and
\sigma_{1}
in the tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau_{1},\varphi_{1})}
in a small ball
B_{1}

First, let us consider
\tau_{i}\in[\tau_{1,*}+\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}-\varepsilon]
case. Considering the direction of zero normal curvature, (limit of
y^{\prime}(\tau)
in (
3.24
)) and sufficiently small circle
B_{1}
(with sufficiently small radius
\delta
by (
4.3
)), we obtain

\tan\eta_{1}\leq(1+C\delta)\sqrt{\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})|}},

(4.6)

for some
C>0
when
\tau_{1}\notin[\tau_{1,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{1,*}+\varepsilon]\cup[\tau_{2,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}+\varepsilon]
. Coefficient
(1+C\delta)
comes from the fact that tangential direction of
I_{1,2}
and intersection curve
T_{\sigma_{1}}\cap\partial\Omega
are nearly parallel in sufficiently small ball
B_{1}
. (See Figure
8

.)
If
\tau_{1}\notin[\tau_{1,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{1,*}+\varepsilon]\cup[\tau_{2,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}+\varepsilon]
, then

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})|}}\leq C_{1,\varepsilon},

by continuity of
\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)
and
\sup_{\tau\in[a,b]}|\kappa(\tau)\gamma_{1}(\tau)|<\infty
.

On the other hand, for
\tau_{1}\in[\tau_{1,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{1,*}+\varepsilon]\cup[\tau_{2,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}+\varepsilon]
, we have

\displaystyle\tan\eta_{1}\leq\tan(\frac{\pi}{2}-C\varepsilon)=C_{2,\varepsilon},

due to our assumption (
4.2
).
Since
\varphi=\Delta\varphi_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\sin(\Delta\varphi_{1})}{\Delta\tau_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{1}^{2})}\right|\leq C_{\varepsilon},

where
C_{\varepsilon}:=\max\{(1+C\delta)C_{1,\varepsilon},C_{2,\varepsilon}\}
. Since we assume smallness condition
|\Delta\tau_{1}|,|\Delta\varphi_{1}|<\delta\ll 1
, we have
|\Delta\varphi_{1}|\leq C_{\varepsilon}|\Delta\tau_{1}|
. By applying the above argument for every bound, we get (
4.4
).
∎

The following analyltic implicit function theorem will be used in the proof of Lemma
4.4
.

Lemma 4.3
.

[
30
]

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a real analytic function in a neighborhood of
(x_{0},y_{0})
for some
(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
. If
f(x_{0},y_{0})=0
and the Jacobian matrix

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\partial f_{i}}{\partial y_{j}}(x_{0},y_{0})\right]

is invertible, then there exists a function
g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
which is real-analytic in a neighborhood of
x_{0}
and satisfies

\displaystyle f(x,g(x))=0

in a neighborhood of
x_{0}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

We suppose the same assumption in Lemma
4.1
. Using the same notation in Lemma
4.1
, we also assume that

\displaystyle|\tan\eta_{i}-\tan\vartheta_{i}|\geq\varepsilon,\quad\text{for }\tau_{i}\in(Z_{h})_{\varepsilon},

(4.7)

where
\tan\vartheta_{i}:=\sqrt{\frac{\kappa(\tau_{i})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{i})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{i})|}}
and
(Z_{h})_{\varepsilon}
is
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
Z_{h}
in Lemma
3.6

\displaystyle(Z_{h})_{\varepsilon}:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}(\tau_{i}^{\mathbf{z}}-\varepsilon,\tau_{i}^{\mathbf{z}}+\varepsilon),\quad Z_{h}=\{\tau_{i}^{\mathbf{z}}\}_{i=1}^{\ell},\quad\textrm{for some constant }\ell<\infty,

(4.8)

so that
(\sigma_{i},\overrightarrow{\sigma_{i-1}\sigma_{i}})
is not an inflection of the zero curvature curve
\partial\Omega\cap T_{\sigma(\tau_{i},\varphi_{i})}
on tangent plane
T_{\sigma(\tau_{i},\varphi_{i})}
, i.e.,
\partial\Omega\cap T_{\sigma(\tau_{i},\varphi_{i})}
is concave or convex in
T_{\sigma(\tau_{i},\varphi_{i})}
. Then, we have the following relation:

\displaystyle|\Delta\tau_{i+1}-\Delta\tau_{i}|=\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}(\Delta\tau_{i+1}^{2})+\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}(\Delta\tau_{i}^{2})+\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}(\Delta\varphi_{i+1}^{2})+\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}(\Delta\varphi_{i}^{2}),\quad\forall i\geq I,

where the notation
\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}
was defined in Definition
2.2
and
I\in\mathbb{N}
in Lemma
4.1
.

Remark 4.5
.

4.4
, we assumed (

\Delta\tau_{i+1}-\Delta\tau_{i}
. Assumption (

\tau_{i}\in(Z_{h})_{\varepsilon}
is covered through these two pictures in Figure

(\sigma(\tau_{i},\varphi_{i}),I_{2}(\tau_{i},\varphi_{i}))
whenever

.

Step 2.
For convenience of notation, we set

in assumption (

) of Lemma

and consider three boundary points

which are in sufficiently small ball
B_{i}
. Without loss of generality, we set
\varphi_{1}=0
due to axis-symmetry. Suppose that

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\sigma_{0}\sigma_{1}}\parallel v\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\overrightarrow{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\parallel R_{\sigma(\tau_{1},0)}v,

where

is reflection operator at the point
\sigma_{1}=\sigma(\tau_{1},0)
. From the above, we have

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\gamma_{1}(\gamma_{2})\cos\varphi_{2}-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})\\
\gamma_{1}(\tau_{2})\sin\varphi_{2}\\
\gamma_{2}(\tau_{2})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})\end{bmatrix}=l\begin{bmatrix}1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}&0&2\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\\
0&1&0\\
2\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})&0&2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\cos\varphi_{0}\\
-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\sin\varphi_{0}\\
\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})\end{bmatrix},

(4.9)

for some constant

. From the first and third components, we have

\begin{split}&\gamma_{1}(\tau_{2})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})\\
&=l\Big((1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\cos\varphi_{0})+2\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))\Big)\\
&\ \ +(1-\cos\varphi_{2})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{2}),\end{split}

(4.10)

\begin{split}&\gamma_{2}(\tau_{2})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})\\
&=l\Big(2\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\cos\varphi_{0})+(2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-1)(\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))\Big).\end{split}

(4.11)

Using the second component of (
4.9
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(\tau_{2})=-\frac{l\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\sin\varphi_{0}}{\sin\varphi_{2}}.

Then, the condition (
4.10
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\gamma_{1}(\tau_{2})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})\\
&=l\Big((1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\cos\varphi_{0})\\
&\ \ \ +2\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\frac{1-\cos\varphi_{2}}{\sin\varphi_{2}}\sin\varphi_{0}\Big)\\
&=l\Big((1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0}))+2\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))\\

(4.12)

Multiply (LHS) in (

) by (RHS) in (

):

\displaystyle l(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{2})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1}))

\displaystyle\times\Big(2\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\cos\varphi_{0})+(2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-1)(\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))\Big)

\displaystyle=l(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{2})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1}))

\displaystyle\times\Big(2\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0}))

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ +(2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-1)(\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))+2\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(1-\cos\varphi_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\Big).

And, multiply (LHS) in (

\displaystyle l(\gamma_{2}(\tau_{2})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1}))\Big((1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0}))+2\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})(1-\cos\varphi_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\frac{1-\cos\varphi_{2}}{\sin\varphi_{2}}\sin\varphi_{0}\Big).

Since two forms above are the same, we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{2})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1}))\Big(2\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0}))+(2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-1)(\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))\\
&\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +2\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(1-\cos\varphi_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\Big)\\
&=(\gamma_{2}(\tau_{2})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1}))\Big((1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0}))+2\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0}))\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})(1-\cos\varphi_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\frac{1-\cos\varphi_{2}}{\sin\varphi_{2}}\sin\varphi_{0}\Big).\end{split}

(4.13)

We expand some terms in (
4.13
) by using Taylor’s expansion at
\tau=\tau_{1}
:

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(\tau_{2})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(\tau_{1})\Delta\tau_{2}+\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{1})}{2}(\Delta\tau_{2})^{2}+\mathcal{O}((\Delta\tau_{2})^{3})

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(\tau_{1})\Delta\tau_{2}-\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})}{2}(\Delta\tau_{2})^{2}+\mathcal{O}((\Delta\tau_{2})^{3}),

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\Delta\tau_{1}-\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{1})}{2}(\Delta\tau_{1})^{2}+\mathcal{O}((\Delta\tau_{1})^{3})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\Delta\tau_{1}+\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})}{2}(\Delta\tau_{1})^{2}+\mathcal{O}((\Delta\tau_{1})^{3}),

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}(\tau_{2})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\prime}_{2}(\tau_{1})\Delta\tau_{2}+\frac{\gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{1})}{2}(\Delta\tau_{2})^{2}+\mathcal{O}((\Delta\tau_{2})^{3})

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\prime}_{2}(\tau_{1})\Delta\tau_{2}+\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})}{2}(\Delta\tau_{2})^{2}+\mathcal{O}((\Delta\tau_{2})^{3}),

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{2}(\tau_{0})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\Delta\tau_{1}-\frac{\gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}(\tau_{1})}{2}(\Delta\tau_{1})^{2}+\mathcal{O}((\Delta\tau_{1})^{3})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\Delta\tau_{1}-\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})}{2}(\Delta\tau_{1})^{2}+\mathcal{O}((\Delta\tau_{1})^{3}),

\displaystyle(1-\cos\varphi_{0})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})

\displaystyle=(\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{0}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{4}))\times(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\Delta\tau_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}^{2}))

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})}{2}\varphi_{0}^{2}-\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})}{2}\varphi_{0}^{2}\Delta\tau_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}^{2})\varphi_{0}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{4}),

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\frac{1-\cos\varphi_{2}}{\sin\varphi_{2}}\sin\varphi_{0}

\displaystyle=(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})-\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\Delta\tau_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}^{2}))\times(\frac{\varphi_{2}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2}^{3}))\times(\varphi_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{3}))

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})}{2}\varphi_{0}\varphi_{2}-\frac{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})}{2}\varphi_{0}\varphi_{2}\Delta\tau_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{3})\varphi_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2}^{3})\varphi_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}^{2})\varphi_{0}\varphi_{2},

where
\Delta\tau_{k}=\tau_{k}-\tau_{k-1}
and
\Delta\varphi_{k}=\varphi_{k}-\varphi_{k-1}
for
k=1,2
. We compare coefficients of the left and right terms in (
4.13
):

\displaystyle(\Delta\tau_{1}\Delta\tau_{2})\quad(LHS)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(2(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})+\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-1))

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(2(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}+2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-1)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}(\tau_{1}),

\displaystyle(RHS)

\displaystyle=(1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})+2\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{3}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}+2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}),

\displaystyle(\Delta\tau_{1})^{2}(\Delta\tau_{2})^{2}\quad(LHS)

\displaystyle=-\frac{k^{2}(\tau_{1})}{2}\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{3}+\frac{k^{2}(\tau_{1})}{4}\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-1)

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}(\tau_{1})}{4}\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}+2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-1)

\displaystyle=-\frac{k^{2}(\tau_{1})}{4}\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}),

\displaystyle(RHS)

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}(\tau_{1})}{4}\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})-\frac{k^{2}(\tau_{1})}{2}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{3}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}(\tau_{1})}{4}\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-2(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{k^{2}(\tau_{1})}{4}\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}),

\displaystyle(\Delta\tau_{2})(\Delta\tau_{1})^{2}\quad(LHS)

\displaystyle=\kappa(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})}{2}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}(2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-1)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})}{2}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}(2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}+1)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})}{2}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2},

\displaystyle(RHS)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})}{2}(1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-\kappa(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})}{2}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})(1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-2(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})}{2}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2},

\displaystyle(\Delta\tau_{1})(\Delta\tau_{2})^{2}\quad(LHS)

\displaystyle=-\kappa(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})}{2}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}(2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-1)

\displaystyle=-\kappa(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}((\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}+(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}-1/2)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})}{2}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2},

\displaystyle(RHS)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})}{2}(1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}+\kappa(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})}{2}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}(1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}+2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})}{2}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2},

\displaystyle(\varphi_{0}^{2}\Delta\tau_{2})\quad(LHS)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})),

\displaystyle(RHS)

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})}{2}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(1-2(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}),

\displaystyle(\varphi_{0}\varphi_{2}\Delta\tau_{2})\quad(LHS)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle(RHS)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})}{2}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}),

\displaystyle(Error\;term)\quad(LHS)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{3})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}^{3})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{4})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}),

\displaystyle(RHS)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{3})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}^{3})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{4})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0})\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0})\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{3})\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2}^{3})\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}).

Therefore, summing the above yields that

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})}{2}(\Delta\tau_{2})^{2}(\Delta\tau_{1})-\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})}{2}(\Delta\tau_{1})^{2}(\Delta\tau_{2})-\frac{\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})}{2}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\varphi_{0}^{2}\Delta\tau_{2}-\frac{\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})}{2}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\varphi_{0}\varphi_{2}\Delta\tau_{2}

\displaystyle\quad=\mathcal{O}((\Delta\tau_{1})^{3})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})+\mathcal{O}((\Delta\tau_{2})^{3})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0})\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0})\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{3})\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2}^{3})\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{4})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}),

where we have used
(\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(\tau_{1}))^{2}+(\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1}))^{2}=1
. Divide both sides above by
\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})}{2}\Delta\tau_{1}\Delta\tau_{2}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta\tau_{2}-\Delta\tau_{1}&=\frac{\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})}{\kappa(\tau_{1})}(\varphi_{0}+\varphi_{2})\frac{\varphi_{0}}{\Delta\tau_{1}}\\
&\quad+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0})\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2})+\frac{1}{\Delta\tau_{1}}\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{\Delta\tau_{1}}\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0})\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{\Delta\tau_{1}}\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{3})\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2})+\frac{1}{\Delta\tau_{1}}\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2}^{3})\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0})+\frac{1}{\Delta\tau_{1}}\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{4}).\end{split}

(4.14)

Since we set
\varphi_{1}=0
, we have
\varphi_{2}=\varphi_{2}-\varphi_{1}=\Delta\varphi_{2}
,
\varphi_{0}=-(\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{0})=-\Delta\varphi_{1}
, and

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}+\varphi_{2}=\Delta\varphi_{2}-\Delta\varphi_{1}.

Thus, we have to derive that
\varphi_{0}+\varphi_{2}=\Delta\varphi_{2}-\Delta\varphi_{1}
has the second-order truncation error.

Step 3.
From the second component in (
4.9
), we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(\tau_{2})\sin\varphi_{2}=-l\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\sin\varphi_{0}.

(4.15)

By using the Taylor expansion,

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(\tau_{2})\sin\varphi_{2}

\displaystyle=(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}))(\varphi_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2}^{3}))=\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})\varphi_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})\varphi_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{2}^{3}),

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(\tau_{0})\sin\varphi_{0}

\displaystyle=(\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}))(\varphi_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{3}))=\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})\varphi_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})\varphi_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{0}^{3}),

which implies that

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})(\varphi_{2}+l\varphi_{0})=\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})\varphi_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})\varphi_{0}.

(4.16)

Since
\tau_{2}
and
\varphi_{2}
are determined by
\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\tau_{1},
and
\varphi_{1}
,
l
in (
4.15
) can be seen as a function of
\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\tau_{1}

l=l(\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\varphi_{0}).

(Note that we have chosen
\varphi_{1}=0
WLOG.)

We will show that
\tau_{2}
,
\varphi_{2}
is locally smooth function of
\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\varphi_{0},\varphi_{2}
when
(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0})\cdot N_{\sigma(\tau_{1},0)}\neq 0
. In fact, by
Step 1
of this proof, it suffices to consider non-grazing cases only. By definition parametrization, map

\sigma:(\tau,\varphi)\mapsto\sigma(\tau,\varphi)\in\partial\Omega

is smooth and injective locally. Hence
\det\frac{\partial\sigma(\tau,\varphi)}{\partial(\tau,\varphi)}\neq 0
and by inverse function theorem,
(\tau_{2},\varphi_{2})(\sigma)
is locally smooth function of boundary point
\sigma\in\partial\Omega
. Now, let us read (
4.9
) as (
\sigma_{2}:=\sigma(\tau_{2},\varphi_{2})
satisfies)

[\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{1}]\times R_{\sigma_{1}}(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0})=0,

which implies

\sigma_{2}=\sigma_{1}+kR_{\sigma_{1}}(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0})\quad\text{for some $k\in\mathbb{R}$.}

(4.17)

If
\sigma\in\partial\Omega
is locally parametrized by analytic function
F(\sigma)=0
, condition
F(\sigma_{2})=0
yields

\tilde{F}(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},k):=F(\sigma_{1}+kR_{\sigma_{1}}(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0}))=0.

Observe that

\partial_{k}\tilde{F}(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},k)=\nabla F|_{\sigma_{2}}\cdot R_{\sigma_{1}}(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0})\neq 0,

because we assume that the trajectory hits
\sigma_{2}
non-grazingly. Note that
\nabla F
is normal to the boundary at the boundary point
\sigma_{2}
. Therefore, by Lemma
4.3
,
k
is (local) smooth function of
\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1}
(or equivalently
\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1}
) . From (
4.17
),
\sigma_{2}
is smooth function of
\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1}
and by inverse function theorem as explained above,
(\tau_{2},\varphi_{2})(\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})
is smooth. Finally, we obtain that
l
is smooth function of
(\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})
.

In particular, if there is no
\varphi_{0}
perturbation, trajectory lies on the
\varphi_{1}=0
cross-section and
\varphi_{2}=0
obviously. In this case, we consider a regularized parametrization
r(\tau)=(r_{1}(\tau),r_{2}(\tau))
for a closed uniformly convex curve in a plane. If we write the normal vector of the curve
r
at
\tau_{1}
as
N(\tau_{1})
, from

r(\tau_{2})-r(\tau_{1})=r^{\prime}(\tau_{1})(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})+\frac{\kappa(\tau_{1})}{2}N(\tau_{1})(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{3}),

(4.18)

we have

\begin{split}|N(\tau_{1})\cdot\big(r(\tau_{2})-r(\tau_{1})\big)|&=\frac{|\kappa(\tau_{1})|}{2}(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{3}),\\
|r^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\cdot\big(r(\tau_{2})-r(\tau_{1})\big)|&=\Delta\tau_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{3}).\end{split}

The specular reflection condition

\frac{\frac{|\kappa(\tau_{1})|}{2}(\Delta\tau_{2})^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{3}),}{\Delta\tau_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{3})}=\frac{\frac{|\kappa(\tau_{1})|}{2}(\Delta\tau_{1})^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}^{3})}{\Delta\tau_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}^{3})},

yields

(\Delta\tau_{2}-\Delta\tau_{1})=\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}^{2}).

(4.19)

From (
4.19
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\tau_{2}}{\Delta\tau_{1}}-1

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})+\frac{1}{\Delta\tau_{1}}\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})+\frac{\Delta\tau_{2}}{\Delta\tau_{1}}\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}),

which implies

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\tau_{2}}{\Delta\tau_{1}}=\frac{1+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})}{1+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})}=(1+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}))(1+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})=1+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}),

where we have used
\frac{1}{1+x}=1+\mathcal{O}(x)
whenever
x\ll 1
. Combining this fact with (
4.18
),

\begin{split}l(\tau_{0},\tau_{1},0)&=\frac{|r(\tau_{2})-r(\tau_{1})|}{|r(\tau_{1})-r(\tau_{0})|}\\
&=1+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}).\end{split}

Divide both sides in (
4.16
) by
\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})\varphi_{0}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi_{2}}{\varphi_{0}}+l=\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})\frac{\varphi_{2}}{\varphi_{0}}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}).

Since
\lim_{\varphi_{0}\rightarrow 0}l(\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\varphi_{0})=1+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})
, one obtains that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varphi_{0}\rightarrow 0}\frac{\varphi_{2}}{\varphi_{0}}=-1.

Since
\varphi_{2}
is a smooth function if
(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0})\cdot N_{\sigma(\tau_{1},0)}\neq 0
(non-grazing case), we get
\varphi_{2}=-\varphi_{0}+\mathcal{O}_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\varepsilon}(\varphi_{0}^{2})
.
Since
\mathcal{O}_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\varepsilon}(\varphi_{0}^{2})
is smooth function that continuously depends also on
\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\varepsilon
and we are assuming that
\tau_{i}
converges, we can impose smallness condition
|\tau_{i}-\tau_{j}|\leq\delta\ll 1
for all
i,j
and hence

\lim_{\varphi_{0}\rightarrow 0}\big|\frac{\mathcal{O}_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\varepsilon}(\varphi_{0}^{2})}{\varphi_{0}^{2}}\big|\leq C_{\varepsilon},

for some constant
C_{\varepsilon}
depending on
\varepsilon
. The same bound
C_{\varepsilon}
can be used when we apply above argument for next bounces to get

\lim_{\varphi_{i}\rightarrow 0}\big|\frac{\mathcal{O}_{\tau_{i},\tau_{i+1},\tau_{i+2},\varepsilon}(\varphi_{i}^{2})}{\varphi_{i}^{2}}\big|\leq C_{\varepsilon},\quad\forall i.

In other words,

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}+\varphi_{2}=\Delta\varphi_{2}-\Delta\varphi_{1}=\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{0}^{2}).

(4.20)

We rewrite
\varphi_{0}
and
\varphi_{2}
as
-\Delta\varphi_{1}
and
\Delta\varphi_{2}
, and then (
4.14
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta\tau_{2}-\Delta\tau_{1}&=-\frac{\gamma_{1}(\tau_{1})\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})}{\kappa(\tau_{1})}(\Delta\varphi_{2}-\Delta\varphi_{1})\frac{\Delta\varphi_{1}}{\Delta\tau_{1}}\\
&\quad+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{1}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{1})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{2})+\frac{1}{\Delta\tau_{1}}\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{1}^{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{\Delta\tau_{1}}\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{1})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{2})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2})\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{\Delta\tau_{1}}\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{1}^{3})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{2})+\frac{1}{\Delta\tau_{1}}\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{2}^{3})\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{1})+\frac{1}{\Delta\tau_{1}}\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{1}^{4}).\end{split}

(4.21)

Using
\left|\frac{\Delta\varphi_{1}}{\Delta\tau_{1}}\right|\leq C_{\varepsilon}
from (
4.4
) in Lemma
4.1
and (
4.20
), we obtain the following relation through (
4.20
):

\displaystyle|\Delta\tau_{2}-\Delta\tau_{1}|

\displaystyle=(1+C_{\varepsilon})\big(\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\tau_{1}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{2}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta\varphi_{1}^{2})\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}(\Delta\tau_{2}^{2})+\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}(\Delta\tau_{1}^{2})+\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}(\Delta\varphi_{2}^{2})+\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}(\Delta\varphi_{1}^{2}).

When we apply above argument and (
4.4
) in Lemma
4.1
for next bounces, we obtain

\displaystyle|\Delta\tau_{i+1}-\Delta\tau_{i}|=\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}(\Delta\tau_{i}^{2})+\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}(\Delta\tau_{i+1}^{2})+\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}(\Delta\varphi_{i}^{2})+\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}(\Delta\varphi_{i+1}^{2}),\quad\forall i\geq I.

∎

From Lemma
4.4
, we can prove that accumulation of bouncing points never happens away from inflection grazing.

Lemma 4.6
.

Under the same assumption as in Lemma
4.4
, if
[x^{i}(x,v),v^{i}(x,v)]\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}|x^{i}(x,v)-x^{i-1}(x,v)|=\infty.

Proof.

For a contradiction argument, we suppose that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}|x^{i}(x,v)-x^{i-1}(x,v)|<\infty.

And then,
x^{i}(x,v)(=\sigma(\tau_{i},\varphi_{i}))\rightarrow x^{\infty}(=\sigma(\tau_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty}))\in\partial\Omega
. If
x^{\infty}=\sigma(\tau_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})
with
\tau_{\infty}\notin[\tau_{1,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}+\varepsilon]
, the
x^{\infty}
is an uniformly convex point of
\partial\Omega
. Then by velocity lemma (see Lemma 1 of
[
16
]
or Lemma 2.7 of
[
25
]
), the trajectory cannot converge.

Therefore, let us assume
\tau_{\infty}\in[\tau_{1,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}+\varepsilon]
for
x^{\infty}=\sigma(\tau_{\infty},\varphi_{\infty})
. By Lemma
4.4
, there exist constants
C_{1,\varepsilon},C_{2,\varepsilon}>0
depending on
\varepsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle C_{1,\varepsilon}(|\Delta\tau_{i+1}|^{2}+|\Delta\tau_{i}|^{2}+|\Delta\varphi_{i+1}|^{2}+|\Delta\varphi_{i}|^{2})\leq|\Delta\tau_{i+1}-\Delta\tau_{i}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{2,\varepsilon}(|\Delta\tau_{i+1}|^{2}+|\Delta\tau_{i}|^{2}+|\Delta\varphi_{i+1}|^{2}+|\Delta\varphi_{i}|^{2}),

(4.22)

for all
i\geq I
. Under assumptions of Lemma
4.4
, Lemma
4.1
holds obviously. For notational convenience, we assume that (
4.22
) and (
4.4
) in Lemma
4.1
hold for all
i\geq 1\;(I=1)
, WLOG. Then, for any
N>0
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\Delta\tau_{i+1}|

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}(|\Delta\tau_{i}|-C_{2,\varepsilon}(|\Delta\tau_{i+1}|^{2}+|\Delta\tau_{i}|^{2}+|\Delta\varphi_{i+1}|^{2}+|\Delta\varphi_{i}|^{2}))

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}(|\Delta\tau_{i}|-C_{2,\varepsilon}(1+C_{\varepsilon}^{-2})|\Delta\tau_{i+1}|^{2}-C_{2,\varepsilon}(1+C_{\varepsilon}^{-2})|\Delta\tau_{i}|^{2})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}(|\Delta\tau_{i-1}|-C_{2,\varepsilon}(|\Delta\tau_{i}|^{2}+|\Delta\tau_{i-1}|^{2}+|\Delta\varphi_{i}|^{2}+|\Delta\varphi_{i-1}|^{2})

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ -C_{2,\varepsilon}(1+C_{\varepsilon}^{-2})|\Delta\tau_{i+1}|^{2}-C_{2,\varepsilon}(1+C_{\varepsilon}^{-2})|\Delta\tau_{i}|^{2})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}(|\Delta\tau_{i-1}|-C_{2,\varepsilon}(1+C_{\varepsilon}^{-2})|\Delta\tau_{i+1}|^{2}-2C_{2,\varepsilon}(1+C_{\varepsilon}^{-2})|\Delta\tau_{i}|^{2}-C_{2,\varepsilon}(1+C_{\varepsilon}^{-2})|\Delta\tau_{i-1}|^{2})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(|\Delta\tau_{1}|-C_{2,\varepsilon}(1+C_{\varepsilon}^{-2})|\Delta\tau_{i+1}|^{2}-2C_{2,\varepsilon}(1+C_{\varepsilon}^{-2})\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}|\Delta\tau_{j+1}|^{2}-C_{2,\varepsilon}(1+C_{\varepsilon}^{-2})|\Delta\tau_{1}|^{2}\right).

(4.23)

Notice that constants
C_{\varepsilon}
and
C_{2,\varepsilon}
depend on
\varepsilon
wth fixed
\varepsilon
. Since
\Delta\tau_{i}\rightarrow 0
as
i\rightarrow\infty
, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
N_{1}>0
such that
|\Delta\tau_{i}|<\varepsilon
for
i>N_{1}
. Let
m=\arg\max_{j\leq N_{1}}|\Delta\tau_{j}|
. If we take
\varepsilon
small enough to satisfy
|\Delta\tau_{m}|>\varepsilon
, then
|\Delta\tau_{m}|\geq|\Delta\tau_{i}|
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We change an index such as
\Delta\tau_{m}\rightarrow\Delta\tau_{1}
, so that
|\Delta\tau_{1}|\geq|\Delta\tau_{i}|
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Since

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}|\Delta\tau_{i}|<\infty,

for any
N>0
, there exists
N_{2}=N_{2}(N)>0
such that

\displaystyle|\Delta\tau_{i}|<1/N,\quad\forall i\geq N_{2},

and

\displaystyle|\Delta\tau_{1}|+\cdots+|\Delta\tau_{N_{2}}|<\frac{1}{4C_{2,\varepsilon}(1+C_{\varepsilon}^{-2})}.

Thus, from our assumption of
\Delta\tau_{i}
above, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{i+1}|\Delta\tau_{j}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{2}-1}\sum_{j=1}^{i+1}|\Delta\tau_{j}|^{2}+\sum_{i=N_{2}}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{2}-1}|\Delta\tau_{j}|^{2}+\sum_{i=N_{2}}^{N}\sum_{j=N_{2}}^{i}|\Delta\tau_{j}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N_{2}-1}\sum_{j=1}^{i+1}|\Delta\tau_{j}|^{2}+\sum_{i=N_{2}}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{2}-1}|\Delta\tau_{j}|^{2}+\sum_{i=N_{2}}^{N}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=N_{2}}^{i}|\Delta\tau_{j}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N}(N-N_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{2}-1}(|\Delta\tau_{1}|^{2}+\cdots+|\Delta\tau_{i+1}|^{2})+\sum_{i=N_{2}}^{N}(|\Delta\tau_{1}|^{2}+\cdots+|\Delta\tau_{N_{2}-1}|^{2})

\displaystyle\leq C+\sum_{i=1}^{N}(|\Delta\tau_{1}|^{2}+\cdots+|\Delta\tau_{N_{2}}|^{2})

\displaystyle\leq C+\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\Delta\tau_{1}|(|\Delta\tau_{1}|+\cdots+|\Delta\tau_{N_{2}}|)

\displaystyle\leq C+\frac{1}{4C_{2,\varepsilon}(1+C_{\varepsilon}^{-2})}|\Delta\tau_{1}|N,

where we have used Lemma
4.1
. Thus, the term in (
4.1
)

\displaystyle C_{2,\varepsilon}(1+C_{\varepsilon}^{-2})\sum_{i=1}^{N}(|\Delta\tau_{i+1}|^{2}+2\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}|\Delta\tau_{j+1}|^{2}+|\Delta\tau_{1}|^{2})

does not dominate
\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\Delta\tau_{1}|
since
\tau_{i}
does not converge to some points near
\tau_{1,*}
and
\tau_{2,*}
. Thus, as
N\rightarrow\infty
,
\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\Delta\tau_{i+1}|
diverges which contradicts the hypothesis
\tau_{i}\rightarrow\tau_{\infty}
.
∎

4.2.
Uniform number of bounce for
\gamma_{0}^{I}

The trajectory with total length
NT
in
\Omega
cannot wind the
z-
axis of the general toroidal domain more than
\mathbf{w}=\left(\left[\frac{NT}{2\pi r}\right]+1\right)
times. So, we consider domain of
\sigma(\tau,\varphi)\in\partial\Omega
:

\tau\in[a,b),\quad\varphi\in(-2\pi\mathbf{w},0].

Note that grazing of trajectory cannot happen for
\tau\in[\tau_{2,*}+\varepsilon,\tau_{1,*}-\varepsilon]
(uniformly convex part) by Lemma
3.3
. If
\tau\in[\tau_{1,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{1,*}+\varepsilon]\cup[\tau_{2,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}+\varepsilon]
, we will exclude near inflection sets
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I_{1,2}(\tau,\varphi))
as mentioned in (
4.2
) in Lemma
4.1
. Similarly, if
\tau\in(Z_{h})_{\varepsilon}
, we also exclude near inflection sets in (
4.7
) of Lemma
4.4
.

Therefore, from now on, we consider the domain
(\tau,\varphi)

\tau\in[\tau_{1,*}+\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}-\varepsilon]\backslash(Z_{h})_{\varepsilon},\quad\varphi\in(-2\pi\mathbf{w},0].\\

(4.24)

where
(Z_{h})_{\varepsilon}
was defined in (
4.8
). Recall that we use
S_{\varphi}
to denote
\varphi
cross-section. Note that we carefully distinguish
S_{\varphi}
and
S_{\varphi+2\pi}
, because
\mathbf{w}
, the winding number of the trajectory with respect to
z
-axis during
[0,T_{0}]
can be large in general.

First, we start with the lemma about the basic property arising from axis-symmetry of
\Omega
.

Lemma 4.7
.

(There is no consecutive grazing in
\Omega
.) Let
(x,v)\in\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{V}^{N}
. We assume that the angular momentum
w(x,v)
defined in (
4.1
) is positive. Then,
n(x^{i}(x,v))\nparallel n(x^{i+1}(x,v))
where
n(y)
is an outward unit normal vector at
y\in\partial\Omega
. Hence, if
[x^{i}(x,v),v^{i-1}(x,v)]\in\gamma_{0}
, then
[x^{i+1}(x,v),v^{i}(x,v)]\notin\gamma_{0}
.

Proof.

Note that trajectory is not located on only one cross-section due to positive angular momentum. If there are two consecutive concave grazing, two tangential planes at
x^{i}
and
x^{i+1}
must coincide, or equivalently,
n(x^{i}(x,v))\parallel n(x^{i+1}(x,v))
which is impossible in
\Omega
. If we set
x^{j}(x,v)=\sigma(\tau_{j},\varphi_{j})\in\partial\Omega
for
j=i,i+1
, then
\varphi_{i+1}=\varphi_{i}+\pi
to satisfy
n(x^{i}(x,v))\parallel n(x^{i+1}(x,v))
. However, it cannot happen in domain
\Omega
which rotates the uniformly convex region.
∎

Let us consider a trajectory segment between two points. By Lemma
4.7
, we can assume at least one point between them is non-grazing.

Lemma 4.8
.

Assume
(x,v)\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}
and
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}
.

(a) Let us assume
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}
.
For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists corresponding
\delta>0
which satisfies : if
(y,u)\in\{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}
and
|(x,v)-(y,u)|<\delta
, then
(y,u)\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}
and satisfies

|(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))-(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u))|<\varepsilon.

(b) Let us assume
(x,v)\notin\gamma_{0}
and
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}
.In this case, we have the following dichotomy for
(y,u)
close to
(x,v)
:

(b-1)
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u))
is closer to
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))
than
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v))
. In the case of (b-1), for sufficiently small
\varepsilon\ll 1
, there exists
\delta>0
such that if
|(x,v)-(y,u)|<\delta
,

|(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(x,v))-(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(y,u),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(y,u))|<\varepsilon,\quad j=1,2.\\

(4.25)

(b-2)
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u))
is closer to
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v))
than
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))
. In the case of (b-2), for sufficiently small
\varepsilon\ll 1
, there exists
\delta>0
such that if
|(x,v)-(y,u)|<\delta
,

|(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v))-(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u))|<\varepsilon.

(4.26)

Moreover, we choose sufficiently small
\delta_{*}>0
so that
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u))\notin\gamma_{0}
whenever
|(x,v)-(y,u)|<\delta_{*}
.

Proof.

Proof of (a) :
First note that
\gamma_{0}^{I}
is parametrized by
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{1,2}(\tau,\varphi))
which is smooth in
(\tau,\varphi)
. Since
I^{1,2}(\tau,\varphi)
is explicit two directions,
\gamma_{0}^{I}
is closed and we can choose sufficiently small
\delta_{*}\ll 1
so that
(y,u)\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}
whenever
|(y,u)-(x,v)|<\delta_{*}
. Now, we have only two cases : (i)
(y,u)\in\gamma_{0}^{C}
or (ii)
(y,u)\notin\gamma_{0}
. For fixed
(x,v)
, we consider
\xi(x+v\ell)
where
\xi(x+vt_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v))=\xi(x_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v))=0
. Since we assume no grazing bounce,

\frac{\partial}{\partial\ell}\xi(x+v\ell)|_{\ell=t_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v)}=\nabla\xi(x_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v))\cdot v\neq 0,

(4.27)

and so by Lemma
4.3
, both
t_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v)
and
x_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v)=x+vt_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v)
are locally analytic function of
(x,v)
. Also,
v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v)=R_{x_{\mathbf{f}}(x,v)}v
is locally analytic. For both (i) and (ii) cases, we have continuity of
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u))
near
(x,v)
by choosing
\delta
smaller than
\delta_{*}
.

Proof of (b) :
We assume
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}
. By dichotomy, (b-1) or (b-2) must happen. If (b-1) happens, there exists
\delta_{*}\ll 1
such that if
(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
satisfies
|(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})-(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))|<\delta_{*}
, then
(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}
since
\gamma_{0}^{I}
is closed. Now let us consider
\overline{B_{\delta}(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))}\subset B_{\delta_{*}}(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))
. Considering invertible (and locally continuous for sufficiently small
\delta>0
by (a)) map,

(x_{\mathbf{b}},v_{\mathbf{b}}):(x,v)\in\{\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash\gamma_{0}^{I}\mapsto(x_{\mathbf{b}},v_{\mathbf{b}})(x,v),

(x_{\mathbf{b}},v_{\mathbf{b}})
is continuously onto map from
\overline{B_{\delta}(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))}
to
(x_{\mathbf{b}},v_{\mathbf{b}})(\overline{B_{\delta}(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),}

\overline{v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))})
where the range includes
(x,v)
. Therefore, the inverse map

(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}):(x_{\mathbf{b}},v_{\mathbf{b}})(\overline{B_{\delta}(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))})\mapsto\overline{B_{\delta}(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))}\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}

is also continuous. Now using (a) again, the map

(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}):\overline{B_{\delta}(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))}\mapsto U((x_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v)))

is also continuous where
U((x_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v)))\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
is a neighborhood of
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v),

v_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v))
. This proves (
4.25
) for (b-1) case.

If (b-2) happens, we compare
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u))
and
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v))
. From Lemma
4.7
,
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}
because we are assuming
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}
. Since we assume (b-2), if we ignore
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(x,v))
, map
(x,v)\mapsto(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{2}(x,v))
must be continuous for sufficiently small
\varepsilon\ll 1
. So we proved (
4.26
). Since
\gamma_{0}
is closed set, by choosing sufficiently small
\delta_{*}\ll 1
WLOG, we can guarantee
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{1}(y,u))

\notin\gamma_{0}
.

∎

Definition 4.9
.

Let
L>0
. We define the number of bounce
\mathcal{N}(x,v,L)
as follows,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(x,v,L):=\sup\left\{k\in\mathbb{N}:(x^{j}(x,v),v^{j-1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I_{-}},\;\;\forall 1\leq j\leq k\;\text{and}\;\ \sum_{j=1}^{k}|x^{j}(x,v)-x^{j-1}(x,v)|\leq L\right\}.

(4.28)

Above lemma helps us to count bounce number of perturbed trajectory.

Lemma 4.10
.

(Analyticity of non-grazing trajectory)
Assume that
(x,v)\in\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{V}^{N}
and

\mathcal{N}(x,v,N(t-s)):=M_{1}<\infty.

i.e., there are finite
M_{1}<\infty
bounces while backward in time trajectory travels total length
L
from
(x,v)
under specular reflection. If

\displaystyle(x^{i}(x,v),v^{i}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq M_{1},

(4.29)

\displaystyle\text{resp, }(x^{i}(x,v),v^{i}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq M_{1},

(4.30)

then

(a)
(t^{i}(t,x,v),x^{i}(x,v),v^{i}(x,v))
is locally analytic function of
(x,v)
(resp, locally continuous function of
(x,v)
up to some proper renumbered bounce number).

(b) Under assumption of (a), if
s\notin t^{i}(t,x,v)
for any
1\leq i\leq M_{1}
, then
(X(s;t,x,v),

V(s;t,x,v))
is locally analytic (resp, locally continuous) function of
(x,v)
for fixed
s
.

(c) There existx
\delta_{x,v}\ll 1
such that if
|(y,u)-(x,v)|<\delta_{x,v}
then
\mathcal{N}(y,u,N(t-s))\leq M_{1}
(resp,
\mathcal{N}(y,u,N(t-s))\leq M_{1}
).

Proof.

Step 1.
Let us prove the case of (
4.29
), first. Similar to the proof of (a) in Lemma
4.8
, for fixed
(x,v)
, we consider
\xi(x-v\ell)
where
\xi(x-vt_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v))=\xi(x_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v))=0
and take derivative

\frac{\partial}{\partial\ell}\xi(x-v\ell)|_{\ell=t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)}=\nabla\xi(x_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v))\cdot v\neq 0,

(4.31)

because there is no grazing bounce. By Lemma
4.3
,
t_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v),x_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)=x-vt_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)
and
v^{1}(x,v)=R_{x_{\mathbf{b}}(x,v)}v
are locally analytic function of
(x,v)
. By the same argument,

(x^{i},v^{i})\mapsto(x^{i+1},v^{i+1}),

is also analytic and by chain rule, we prove (a). Now let us prove (b). By (
4.31
) and chain rule, it is
t^{i}(t,x,v)
is also locally analytic in
(x,v)
for fixed
t
. Therefore, mapping

\begin{split}(x,v)&\mapsto(t^{i}(t,x,v),x^{i}(x,v),v^{i}(x,v))\mapsto(t^{M_{1}}(t,x,v),x^{M_{1}}(x,v),v^{M_{1}}(x,v))\\
&\mapsto(X(s;t,x,v),V(s;t,x,v))\end{split}

is also analytic because we have local formula
X(s;t,x,v)=x^{M_{1}}(x,v)-v^{M_{1}}(x,v)(t^{M_{1}}(t,

x,v)-s)
and
V(s;t,x,v)=v^{M_{1}}(x,v)
when
s\notin t^{M_{1}}(t,x,v)
.

Now let us prove (c). We first note that

v^{i}(x,v)\cdot n(x^{i}(x,v))

is also locally analytic by (a). Therefore, there exists
\delta_{1}\ll 1
such that if
|(y,u)-(x,v)|<\delta_{1}
, then

|v^{M_{1}}(y,u)\cdot n(x^{M_{1}}(y,u))|\geq\frac{1}{2}|v^{M_{1}}(x,v)\cdot n(x^{M_{1}}(x,v))|.

(4.32)

By finite curvature of
\partial\Omega
, we can estimate an upper bound of
|x^{M_{1}}(x,v)-x^{M_{1}+1}(x,v)|=|t^{M_{1}}(t,x,v)-t^{M_{1}+1}(t,x,v)|
. Since it suffices to control for small
|x^{M_{1}}(x,v)-x^{M_{1}+1}(x,v)|

\ll 1
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&=\xi(x^{M_{1}}(x,v))-\xi(x^{M_{1}+1}(x,v))=\int_{t^{M_{1}+1}}^{t^{M_{1}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\xi(X(s;t,x,v))ds\\
&=\int_{t^{M_{1}+1}}^{t^{M_{1}}}\nabla\xi(X(s;t,x,v))\cdot V(s;t,x,v)ds\\
&=\int_{t^{M_{1}+1}}^{t^{M_{1}}}\Big[\nabla\xi(x^{M_{1}}(x,v))+\nabla^{2}\xi(x^{M_{1}}(x,v))v^{M_{1}}|s-t^{M_{1}+1}(t,x,v)|\\
&\qquad\qquad+\mathcal{O}(|s-t^{M_{1}}(t,x,v)|^{2})\Big]\cdot v^{M_{1}}(x,v)ds.\\
\end{split}

Using
|\nabla^{2}\xi|\lesssim 1
and
\min_{x\in\partial\Omega}|\nabla\xi(x)|\gtrsim 1
, we derive

C|v^{M_{1}}(x,v)\cdot n(x^{M_{1}}(x,v))|\leq|t^{M_{1}}(t,x,v)-t^{M_{1}+1}(t,x,v)|,\quad\text{for some generic $C>0$.}

(4.33)

If
t^{M_{1}}(t,y,u)\leq s
,
\mathcal{N}(y,u,N(t-s))\leq M_{1}
by definition. So we only consider
t^{M_{1}}(t,y,u)>s
case. Choose
\delta_{2}\ll 1
(smaller than
\delta_{1}
WLOG) so that

|X(s;t,y,u)-x^{M_{1}}(t,y,u)|\leq\frac{C}{2}|v^{M_{1}}(x,v)\cdot n(x^{M_{1}}(x,v))|,

whenever
|(x,v)-(y,u)|<\delta_{2}
. By (
4.32
) and (
4.33
),

\begin{split}|X(s;t,y,u)-x^{M_{1}}(t,y,u)|&\leq\frac{C}{2}|v^{M_{1}}(x,v)\cdot n(x^{M_{1}}(x,v))\leq C|v^{M_{1}}(y,u)\cdot n(x^{M_{1}}(y,u))|\\
&\leq|t^{M_{1}}(t,y,u)-t^{M_{1}+1}(t,y,u)|.\end{split}

Hence, there cannot be extra bounce between time
s
and
t^{M_{1}}(t,y,u)
. We finish the proof of (c) for the case of (
4.29
).

Step 2.
Now, we prove
\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}
case. Note that
\gamma_{0}^{I+}
cannot happen in finite bounce for backward in time trajectory. We exclude
\gamma_{0}^{I}
and only consider concave grazing
\gamma_{0}^{C}
in addition.

To prove (a), note that from (b) of Lemma
4.8
, we have continuity of backward in time trajectory. For each concave grazing, if (b-2) case happens, bounce number is reduced by
1
and we get continuity of bounce number after some renumbering.

Proof for (b) is nearly the same as
Step 1
. From Lemma
4.8
, we use continuity of the following mapping

\begin{split}(x,v)&\mapsto(t^{i}(t,x,v),x^{i}(x,v),v^{i}(x,v))\mapsto(t^{M_{1}}(t,x,v),x^{M_{1}}(x,v),v^{M_{1}}(x,v))\\
&\mapsto(X(s;t,x,v),V(s;t,x,v)).\end{split}

We omit the detail. Proof for (c) is also the same as
Step 1
case. Note that whenever (b-2) of dichotomy in Lemma
4.8
happens, bounce number is reduced by
1
and hence
\mathcal{N}(y,u,N(t-s))
is maximal when there is no concave grazing.
∎

Next, we define arrival time function
s(x,\varphi,v)
. Before, we define the function we should specify
\varphi
-coordinate of
x
since winding number
\mathbf{w}>1
in general.

Definition 4.11
.

For given
(x,\varphi)\in\overline{\Omega}\times(-2\pi\mathbf{w},0]
, we use
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}(x,\varphi)
to denote position
x
contained in
S_{\varphi}
.

Lemma 4.12
.

(Arrival time function
s(x,\varphi,v)
) Assume positive angular momentum (with respect to the
z
-axis)
\omega(x,v)>0
defined in (
4.1
) and the forward in time trajectory from
(x,v)\in\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
is well-defined until it passes
S_{0}
defined in (
1.49
)(i.e., forward in time trajectory does not belong
\gamma_{0}^{I+}
until it passes
S_{0}
cross-section).

When
\phi(x)=\varphi
in
\mod 2\pi
(where
\phi
is defined in Definition
1.49
), we define arrival time function
s(x,\varphi,v)
as time (or distance since
|v|=1
) to reach
S_{0}
from
(x,v)\in S_{\varphi}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
via forward in time trajectory. Then, the arrival time function
s(x,\varphi,v)
which satisfies

X(s(x,\varphi,v);0,x,v)\in S_{0}

(4.34)

is well-defined and continuous in
(x,\varphi,v)
.

Moreover, if the trajectory does not belong to
\gamma_{0}
(resp,
\gamma_{0}^{I}
) and

X(s(x,\varphi,v);0,x,v)\notin\partial S_{0},

(4.35)

in addition, then both
s(x,\varphi,v)
and
(X,V)(s(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot);0,\cdot,\cdot)
are also locally analytic (resp, locally continuous) in
(x,\varphi,v)
.

Before we give proof for above lemma, let us define some notation. When position and velocity(direction) are parametrized by
(\tau,\varphi)
for grazing phase
(x,v)=(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))\in\gamma_{0}^{I-}
, we abbreviate

s(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),\varphi,I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))=s(\tau,\varphi),

(4.36)

for notational convenience.
Also, we use the following abbreviation for concave grazing case :

X(s(\tau,\varphi,\eta);0,\tau,\varphi,\eta):=X(s(\tau,\varphi,\eta);0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v_{c}(\eta)),

(4.37)

where we used
s(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=s(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),\varphi,v_{c}(\eta))
with definition (
3.34
).

Proof of Lemma
4.12
.

If forward in time trajectory does not belong to
\gamma_{0}^{I+}
, the trajectory is well-defined. Now, we consider
\varphi
-directional angular velocity. Since the distance from a point
x\in\partial\Omega
to the
z
-axis is given by
|x\times\hat{z}|=|(x_{2},-x_{1},0)|\geq C>0
for some uniform
C
, the angular velocity of the trajectory
(X(s;0,x,v),V(s;0,x,v))
which is given by

\begin{split}\omega_{\text{ang}}(X(s;0,x,v),V(s;0,x,v))&:=\frac{1}{|X(s;0,x,v)\times\hat{z}|}\omega(X(s;0,x,v),V(s;0,x,v))\\
&=\frac{1}{|X(s;0,x,v)\times\hat{z}|}\omega(x,v)\\
&=\frac{1}{|(X_{2}(s;0,x,v),-X_{1}(s;0,x,v),0)|}\omega(x,v)\\
&\geq\frac{1}{C}\omega(x,v),\end{split}

is bounded from below. Hence
\varphi
-coordinate of
X(s;0,x,v)
increases monotonically as time
s
increases. Note that
\varphi(x)
is not well-defined if we denote
\varphi(x)
as (negative)
\varphi
-coordinate of
x
. Then,

\Phi(s_{*},x,\varphi,v):=\int_{0}^{s_{*}}\frac{\omega(\mathbf{x}(x,\varphi),v)}{|X(s;0,\mathbf{x}(x,\varphi),v)\times\hat{z}|}ds=-\varphi>0,

(4.38)

where
\mathbf{x}(x,\varphi)
is defined in Definition
4.11
. Since the integrand is uniformly positive and finite,
\Phi(s_{*},x,\varphi,v)
is monotone increasing function of
s_{*}
and there exists a unique
s_{*}
as a function of
(x,\varphi,v)
which solves above equation. Defining
s_{*}=s(x,\varphi,v)
,
s(x,\varphi,v)
is well-defined. Continuity of
s(x,\varphi,v)
comes from (
4.38
) because the integrand is continuous in
(x,\varphi,v)
for all
0\leq s\leq s_{*}
.

Now, we assume forward in time trajectory does not belong
\gamma_{0}
and
X(s(x,\varphi,v);0,x,

v)\notin\partial\Omega
, in addition.
s_{*}=s(x,\varphi,v)
solves

\tilde{\varphi}(s_{*},x,\varphi,v):=X(s_{*};0,\mathbf{x}(x,\varphi),v)\cdot e_{2}=0,

where
e_{2}
is
y
-direction vector, and
S_{0}
belongs
xz
plane.

Note that
\tilde{\varphi}
is locally analytic since
X(s;0,x,v)
is locally analytic by (b) of Lemma
4.10
. Since
(\partial_{s}\tilde{\varphi})(s(x,\varphi,v),x,\varphi,v)=V(s(x,\varphi,v);0,\mathbf{x}(x,\varphi),v)\cdot e_{2}\neq 0
by nonzero angular momentum,
s(x,\varphi,v)
is locally analytic by implicit function theorem. ∎

When arrival time function
s(x,\varphi,v)
is well-defined, we use the following notations,

(X,V)(s)(E):=\{(X,V)(s(x,\varphi,v);0,x,v)\in\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}:(x,v)\in E\}.

(4.39)

In addition, if the trajectory is reversible, we also define

(X,V)^{-1}(s)(F):=\{(y,u)\in\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}:(X,V)(s(y,u);0,y,u)\in F\}.

(4.40)

We control the collection of bad phase sets that are nearly grazing sets for each open cover containing boundary
\partial\Omega
.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
S_{0}
be the
\varphi=0
cross-section defined in (
1.49
). For
\varepsilon\ll 1
, there exist finite points

\{{x}_{1}^{nB},\cdots,{x}_{l_{nB}}^{nB}\}\subset\overline{S_{0}},

and their spatial relatively open neighborhoods

B({x}_{1}^{nB},r_{1}^{nB})\cap\overline{S_{0}},\cdots,B({x}_{l_{nB}}^{nB},r_{l_{nB}}^{nB})\cap\overline{S_{0}},

as well as corresponding open sets in velocity

\mathcal{O}_{1}^{nB},\cdots,\mathcal{O}_{l_{nB}}^{nB}\subset\mathbb{S}^{2},

with
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{i}^{nB})\lesssim\varepsilon
for all
i=1,\cdots,l_{nB}
such that for every
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
, there exists
i\in\{1,\cdots,l_{nB}\}
with
x\in B({x}_{i}^{nB},r_{i}^{nB})\cap\overline{S_{0}}
and satisfies either

\displaystyle B(x_{i}^{nB},r_{i}^{nB})\cap\partial S_{0}=\emptyset,

or

\displaystyle|v^{\prime}\cdot n(x^{\prime})|>\varepsilon/2\ \text{for all}\ x^{\prime}\in B({x}^{nB}_{i},r^{nB}_{i})\cap\partial S_{0}\ \text{and }\ v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{2}\backslash\mathcal{O}_{i}^{nB}.

Proof.

For
{x}\in S_{0}
, we choose
r_{{x}}>0
such that
B(x,r_{{x}})\cap\partial S_{0}=\emptyset
. For each
{x}\in\partial{S_{0}}
, we can define the outward unit normal direction
n(x)
and the outward normal angle
\theta_{n}({x})\in[0,2\pi)
specified uniquely by
n({x})=(\cos\theta_{n}({x}),0,\sin\theta_{n}({x}))
. Using the smoothness and uniform boundedness of curvature of the boundary
\partial{\Omega}
, there exists uniform
r_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for
r_{x}\leq r_{\varepsilon}
,

|\ \theta_{{n}}({x}^{\prime})-\theta_{{n}}({x})\ |<\varepsilon/2\ \ \ \text{for all }\ {x}^{\prime}\in{B}({x},r_{{x}})\cap\partial S_{0}.

(4.41)

By compactness, we have finite integer
l_{nB}>0
, points
\{x_{i}^{nB}\}_{i=1}^{l_{nB}}
, and positive numbers
\{r_{i}^{nB}\}_{i=1}^{l_{nB}}
such that

\overline{S_{0}}\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{l_{nB}}B(x^{nB}_{i},r^{nB}_{i}),\quad r_{i}^{nB}\leq r_{\varepsilon}.

By above construction, for each
1\leq i\leq l_{nB}
, we have either

B(x_{i}^{nB},r_{i}^{nB})\cap\partial S_{0}=\emptyset,

(4.42)

or

x_{i}^{nB}\in\partial S_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad r_{i}^{nB}<r_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{so that}\quad(\ref {near-paral})\quad\text{holds}.

(4.43)

For
i
with case (
4.42
), we set
\mathcal{O}^{nB}_{i}=\emptyset
. For
i
with case (
4.43
), we define
