\begin{split}\mathcal{O}^{nB}_{i}:=&\Big\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:{v}=\big(\sin\phi\cos\theta,\sin\phi\sin\theta,\cos\phi\big)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{where}\\
&\ \phi\in\Big(2\pi-\theta_{i}(mod\;\pi),2\pi-\theta_{i}+\varepsilon(mod\;\pi)\Big)\cup\Big(3\pi-\theta_{i}-\varepsilon(mod\;\pi),3\pi-\theta_{i}(mod\;\pi)\Big),\\
&\ \theta\in\Big(\sqrt{\varepsilon},2\pi-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\Big)\Big\},\\
\end{split}

where we abbreviated
\theta_{n}(x_{i}^{nB})=\theta_{i}
. Obviously,
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{i}^{nB})\leq\frac{1}{3}\times\varepsilon\times 2\pi\lesssim\varepsilon
and

\displaystyle|v^{\prime}\cdot n(x^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|{v}^{\prime}|\times\Big|(\sin\phi^{\prime}\cos\theta^{\prime},\sin\phi^{\prime}\sin\theta^{\prime},\cos\phi^{\prime})\cdot(\cos\theta_{{n}}({x}^{\prime}),0,\sin\theta_{{n}}({x}^{\prime}))\Big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big|\sin\phi^{\prime}\cos\theta_{n}(x^{\prime})+\cos\phi^{\prime}\sin\theta_{n}(x^{\prime})-(1-\cos\theta^{\prime})\sin\phi^{\prime}\cos\theta_{n}(x^{\prime})\Big|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|\sin\varepsilon|-\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\quad\varepsilon\ll 1,

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{2},

for
x^{\prime}\in B({x}^{nB}_{i},r^{nB}_{i})
and
v^{\prime}=(\sin\phi^{\prime}\cos\theta^{\prime},\sin\phi^{\prime}\sin\theta^{\prime},\cos\phi^{\prime})\in\mathbb{S}^{2}\backslash\mathcal{O}^{nB}_{i}
.
∎

Motivated by angular momentum conservation, we define
\tau
-ring and small angular momentum direction on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Recall that we assume angular momentum
\omega>0
, WLOG.

Definition 4.14
.

We define
\tau
-angular momentum ring on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
centered at
x\in\overline{\Omega}
. For
\tau_{1,*}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{2,*}
,

\begin{split}R_{x,\tau}&:=\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:\omega(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))=\omega(x,v)\},\\
R_{x,\tau}^{\varepsilon}&:=\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:|\omega(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))-\omega(x,v)|<\varepsilon\},\\
\end{split}

(4.44)

(it is equivalent to use
I_{1}
instead of
I_{2}
, of course) and other small regimes in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
,

\begin{split}R_{\perp}&:=\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:|v_{\varphi}|=0\},\quad R_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}:=\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:|v_{\varphi}|<\varepsilon\},\\
R_{\hat{\varphi}}&:=\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:|v_{\varphi}|=1\},\quad R_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}:=\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:1-\varepsilon<|v_{\varphi}|\},\\
R_{sym}&:=\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:|v_{x}|=|v_{y}|\},\quad R_{sym}^{\varepsilon}:=\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:||v_{x}|-|v_{y}||<\varepsilon\},\\
\end{split}

(4.45)

where we used the coordinate
v=(v_{x},v_{\varphi},v_{y})
defined in (
1.50
). Note that it is easy to check
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(R_{x,\tau}^{\varepsilon})\leq C\varepsilon
and
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(R_{i}^{\varepsilon})\leq C\varepsilon
for both all
i\in\{\perp,\hat{\varphi},sym\}
with some generic constant
C>0
. Here,
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
is Liouville measure on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Note that
R_{x,\tau}^{\varepsilon}
and
R_{i}^{\varepsilon}
are open on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

Figure 11
.
Sets
R_{\perp}^{\varepsilon},R_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}
, and
R_{sym}^{\varepsilon}

In Figure
11
,
x
is located at the center of cross-section
S_{\varphi}
. In
R_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}
,
S_{\varphi}
containing
x
was projected to be shown as above. Hence, the underlined part of the figure is the same with projection of
R_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}
. In
R_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}
, velocity
v_{\varphi}\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
is perpendicular to cross-section
S_{\varphi}
.
R_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}
is the set of velocity which perturbs
v_{\hat{\varphi}}
as much as
\varepsilon
. Lastly, the set
R_{sym}^{\varepsilon}
consists of velocity which perturbs direction satisfying
v_{x}=v_{y}
in (
1.50
).

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
,
\varepsilon\ll 1
, and
\tau\in[\tau_{1,*},\tau_{2,*}]
. Recall definition of
R_{x,\tau}^{\varepsilon}
in (
4.44
). For each
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
, there exists
r^{\prime}=r^{\prime}(x,\varepsilon)
such that

{R_{y,\tau}^{\varepsilon}}\subset R_{x,\tau}^{2\varepsilon},

whenever
y\in B(x,r^{\prime}(x,\varepsilon))\cap\overline{S_{0}}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
u\in{R_{y,\tau}^{\varepsilon}}
. Then, the velocity
u
satisfies
|\omega(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))-\omega(y,u)|\leq\varepsilon
. By definition of angular momentum (
4.1
), we choose
r^{\prime}=r^{\prime}(x,\varepsilon)
such that

\displaystyle|\omega(x,u)-\omega(y,u)|<\varepsilon,

if
y\in B(x,r^{\prime}(x,\varepsilon))\cap\overline{S_{0}}
. Thus, if
(y,u)\in(B(x,r^{\prime}(x,\varepsilon))\cap\overline{S_{0}})\times R_{y,\tau}^{\varepsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle|\omega(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))-\omega(x,u)|

\displaystyle=|\omega(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))-\omega(y,u)+\omega(y,u)-\omega(x,u)|

\displaystyle\leq|\omega(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))-\omega(y,u)|+|\omega(y,u)-\omega(x,u)|

\displaystyle<2\varepsilon,

which means
u\in R_{x,\tau}^{2\varepsilon}
.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

For given
\varepsilon\ll 1
, there exists a compact set
X^{\varepsilon}\subset\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
such that the followings hold: if
(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon}
,

(i)

\displaystyle|v\cdot n(x)|>\varepsilon/2\ \text{for}\ x\in\partial S_{0},

(ii)

v\notin\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell}{R_{x,\tau_{j}^{\mathbf{z}}}^{\varepsilon}}\cup{R_{x,\tau_{1,*}}^{\varepsilon}}\cup{R_{x,\tau_{2,*}}^{\varepsilon}}\cup{R_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}}\cup{R_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}}\cup{R_{sym}^{\varepsilon}},

(iii)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\{\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash X^{\varepsilon})_{x})\lesssim\varepsilon,

where we used notation

\displaystyle A_{x}:=\{y\in Y:(x,y)\in A\},

(4.46)

for
A\subset X\times Y
.

Proof.

Recall that we constructed the relatively open cover
\bigcup_{i=1}^{l_{nB}}\{B(x_{i}^{nB},r_{i}^{nB})\cap\overline{S_{0}}\}
for
\overline{S}_{0}
and corresponding open sets in velocity
\mathcal{O}_{j}^{nB}
for
j=1,\cdots,l_{nB}
in Lemma
4.13
. For given
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
, we define an index set

\displaystyle I_{x}^{nB}:=\{i\in\mathbb{N}:x\in B(x_{i}^{nB},r_{i}^{nB})\cap\overline{S_{0}}\},

(4.47)

to collect relatively open sets containing
x
. Notice that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\bigcap_{i\in I_{x}^{nB}}\mathcal{O}_{i}^{nB})\leq\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{j}^{nB})\lesssim\varepsilon,

(4.48)

for all
j\in I_{x}^{nB}
. Moreover, if
v\notin\bigcap_{i\in I_{x}^{nB}}\mathcal{O}_{i}^{nB}
for fixed
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
, then it holds that

\displaystyle|v\cdot n(x)|>\varepsilon/2,\quad\text{if }x\in\partial S_{0},

from Lemma
4.13
.
Hence, the following set

\displaystyle\bigcup_{x\in\overline{S_{0}}}\Big\{(x,v):v\in\bigcap_{i\in I_{x}^{nB}}\mathcal{O}_{i}^{nB}\Big\}

(4.49)

contains all near grazing points in phase space. To cover above (
4.49
), we construct an open cover

\displaystyle\mathcal{NB}:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{l_{nB}}\Big\{(x,v)\in\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}:x\in B(x_{i}^{nB},r_{i}^{nB})\cap\overline{S_{0}},\;v\in\bigcap_{j\in I_{x}^{nB}}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{nB}\Big\}_{\varepsilon},

(4.50)

where we used the following general notation
(\cdot)_{\varepsilon}
, a
\varepsilon
-neighborhood for a set in a metric space,

\displaystyle A_{\varepsilon}:=\{x\in O:dist(x,A)<\varepsilon\},\quad\text{$O$ is a metric space and $A\subset O$}.

(4.51)

It follows from (
4.48
) and (
4.50
) that

\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\mathcal{NB}_{x})\lesssim\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\mathcal{O}_{j}^{nB})_{\varepsilon})\lesssim\varepsilon,\quad\forall j\in I_{x}^{nB},

(4.52)

where we used the notation
A_{x}
in (
4.46
).

Meanwhile, by (
4.48
) and definition of
\mathcal{NB}
in (
4.50
), for any
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
, there exists a velocity
v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}\backslash(\mathcal{NB})_{x}
where the notation
(\mathcal{NB})_{x}
is defined in (
4.46
).
Hence, if we define the projection operator
\mathcal{P}_{1}
for
A\subset X\times Y
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{1}(A):=\{x\in X:(x,y)\in A\text{ for some }y\in Y\},

(4.53)

then we obtain
\mathcal{P}_{1}((\mathcal{NB})^{c})=\overline{S_{0}}
where
(\mathcal{NB})^{c}
is a compact set

(\mathcal{NB})^{c}:=\{\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash\mathcal{NB}.

(4.54)

Now, recall definition (
4.54
) and we define

Y:=\Big\{(x,v)\in(\mathcal{NB})^{c}:v\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell}{R_{x,\tau_{j}^{\mathbf{z}}}^{\varepsilon}}\cup{R_{x,\tau_{1,*}}^{\varepsilon}}\cup{R_{x,\tau_{2,*}}^{\varepsilon}}\cup{R_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}}\cup{R_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}}\cup{R_{sym}^{\varepsilon}}\Big\},

(4.55)

where
R_{x,\tau}
,
R_{\perp,\hat{\varphi},sym}
, and
\ell
are defined in (
4.44
), (
4.45
), and (
3.6
), respectively. Then, using compactness of
\mathcal{P}_{1}((\mathcal{NB})^{c})=\overline{S_{0}}
and the argument in the proof of Lemma
4.15
, we obtain an open cover for
\mathcal{P}_{1}((\mathcal{NB})^{c})=\overline{S_{0}}
,

\Big\{B(x_{i}^{R},r_{i}^{R})\cap\overline{S_{0}}\Big\}_{i=1}^{l_{R}},

(4.56)

and corresponding each open set
\mathcal{O}_{i}^{R}\subset\mathbb{S}^{2}
which is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{i}^{R}:=\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell}{R_{x_{i}^{R},\tau_{j}^{\mathbf{z}}}^{2\varepsilon}}\cup{R_{x_{i}^{R},\tau_{1,*}}^{2\varepsilon}}\cup{R_{x_{i}^{R},\tau_{2,*}}^{2\varepsilon}}\cup{R_{\perp}^{2\varepsilon}}\cup{R_{\hat{\varphi}}^{2\varepsilon}}\cup{R_{sym}^{2\varepsilon}},\quad 1\leq i\leq l_{R},

(4.57)

satisfying
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{i}^{R})\lesssim\varepsilon
for all
1\leq i\leq l_{R}
. Also, from Lemma
4.15
, we have

\displaystyle\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell}{R^{\varepsilon}_{x,\tau_{j}^{\mathbf{z}}}}\cup{R^{\varepsilon}_{x,\tau_{1,*}}}\cup{R^{\varepsilon}_{x,\tau_{2,*}}}\cup{R^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}}\cup{R^{\varepsilon}_{\hat{\varphi}}}\cup{R^{\varepsilon}_{sym}}\subset\mathcal{O}_{i}^{R},

(4.58)

for all
x\in B(x_{i}^{R},r_{i}^{R})
and sufficiently small
r_{i}^{R}
.
Similar as (
4.47
), for given
x\in\mathcal{P}_{1}((\mathcal{NB})^{c})=\overline{S_{0}}
, we define an index set

\displaystyle I_{x}^{R}:=\{i\in\mathbb{N}:x\in B(x_{i}^{R},r_{i}^{R})\cap\overline{S_{0}}\}.

(4.59)

Next, let us consider

\bigcup_{x\in\overline{S_{0}}}\Big\{(x,v):v\in\bigcap_{i\in I_{x}^{R}}\mathcal{O}_{i}^{R}\Big\},

(4.60)

which contains
Y
of (
4.55
). Similar to what we did in (
4.50
), we also construct an open cover for (
4.60
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{l_{R}}\Big\{(x,v)\in(\mathcal{NB})^{c}:x\in B(x_{i}^{R},r_{i}^{R})\cap\overline{S_{0}},\;v\in\bigcap_{j\in I_{x}^{R}}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{R}\Big\}_{\varepsilon},

(4.61)

where we have used the notation
A_{\varepsilon}
in (
4.51
). Let us define compact set
X^{\varepsilon}
,

\displaystyle X^{\varepsilon}:=(\mathcal{NB})^{c}\backslash\mathcal{R}.

(4.62)

By the same reason for
\mathcal{P}_{1}((\mathcal{NB})^{c})=\overline{S_{0}}
, we also get

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{1}(X^{\varepsilon})=\overline{S_{0}},

(4.63)

where we have used the notation
\mathcal{P}_{1}
in (
4.53
). Now we check that
X^{\varepsilon}
in (
4.62
) satisfies statements (i)-(iii). Assume
(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon}
. First, since
X^{\varepsilon}\subset(\mathcal{NB})^{c}
, we have

\displaystyle|v\cdot n(x)|>\varepsilon/2,\quad\text{for }x\in\partial S_{0}.

Moreover, since we exclude set
\mathcal{R}
in (
4.62
) and the definition (
4.57
) of
\mathcal{O}_{i}^{R}
, it satisfies that

\displaystyle v\notin\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell}{R^{\varepsilon}_{x,\tau_{j}^{\mathbf{z}}}}\cup{R^{\varepsilon}_{x,\tau_{1,*}}}\cup{R^{\varepsilon}_{x,\tau_{2,*}}}\cup{R^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}}\cup{R^{\varepsilon}_{\hat{\varphi}}}\cup{R^{\varepsilon}_{sym}},

by (
4.55
) and (
4.58
). Lastly, for fixed
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\{\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash X^{\varepsilon})_{x})&=\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\mathcal{NB}\cupdot\mathcal{R})_{x})\\
&\leq\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\mathcal{R}_{x})+\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\mathcal{NB}_{x})\\
&\leq\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\mathcal{O}_{j}^{R})_{\varepsilon})+\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\mathcal{NB}_{x})\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon,\quad\forall j\in I_{x}^{R},\end{split}

(4.64)

by (
4.52
),(
4.61
), and the fact
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{j}^{R})\lesssim\varepsilon
for all
j=1,2,\dots,l_{R}
. Here, we used notation
A_{x}
in (
4.46
).
\varepsilon
-neighborhood notation (
4.51
) comes from the definition (
4.61
). ∎

We decompose above set depending on whether its backward in time trajectory belongs
\gamma_{0}^{I}
before it travels given length
L>0
. We decompose
X^{\varepsilon}=G^{\varepsilon}_{L}\cupdot B^{\varepsilon}_{L}
where
\cupdot
means a disjoint union.

\begin{split}B^{\varepsilon}_{L}&:=\left\{(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon}:\begin{array}[]{cc}\exists k\in\mathbb{N}\text{ such that }(x^{k}(x,v),v^{k-1}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{I-},\\
\text{and}\ \sum_{j=1}^{k}|x^{j}(x,v)-x^{j-1}(x,v)|\leq L\end{array}\right\},\\
G^{\varepsilon}_{L}&:=\left\{(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon}:\begin{array}[]{cc}\nexists k\in\mathbb{N}\text{ such that }(x^{k}(x,v),v^{k-1}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{I-},\\
\text{as long as }\ \sum_{j=1}^{k}|x^{j}(x,v)-x^{j-1}(x,v)|\leq L\end{array}\right\}.\end{split}

(4.65)

Lemma 4.17
.

For
(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon}
, if

\displaystyle(x^{k}(x,v),v^{k-1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I},\;\forall k\quad\text{during finite travel length}\;L,

(4.66)

then the number of bounces in the trajectory within finite travel length
L
is finite. In particular,
\mathcal{N}(x,v,L)<\infty
when
(x,v)\in G_{L}^{\varepsilon}
, i.e., the number of bounces up to travel length
L
is finite. Under the assumption of finite bounce, trajectory (
1.45
) is reversible in time.

Proof.

In
X^{\varepsilon}
, constructed in Lemma
4.16
, we exclude sets
R_{x,\tau_{1,*}}^{\varepsilon}
and
R_{x,\tau_{2,*}}^{\varepsilon}
where
R_{x,\tau}^{\varepsilon}
in (
4.44
). Consider the backward in time trajectory starting from
x
with
v
for
(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon}
. To apply Lemma
4.6
, we first check whether (
4.2
) in Lemma
4.1
holds. We rewrite
x^{i}(x,v)
using
\sigma
:

\displaystyle x^{i}(x,v)=\sigma(\tau_{i},\varphi_{i})\in\partial\Omega,\quad\tau_{i}\in[a,b),\;\varphi_{i}\in(-2\pi\mathbf{w},0],

for all
1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}(x,v,L)
where
\mathcal{N}(x,v,L)
in (
4.28
). If
\tau_{i}\notin[\tau_{1,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{1,*}+\varepsilon]\cup[\tau_{2,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}+\varepsilon]
for all
1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}(x,v,L)
, it does not matter to apply Lemma
4.1
. Hence, we suppose that there exists
j
such that

\displaystyle x^{j}(x,v)=\sigma(\tau_{j},\varphi_{j}),\quad\tau_{j}\in[\tau_{1,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{1,*}+\varepsilon]\cup[\tau_{2,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}+\varepsilon].

Recall definition of
\eta_{j}
in Lemma
4.1
. Since an angle
\vartheta_{j}
between
\hat{\tilde{z}}
and
I_{2}(\tau_{j},\varphi_{j})
satisfies

\displaystyle\tan\vartheta_{j}=\sqrt{\frac{\kappa(\tau_{j})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{j})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{j})|}},

and sets
R_{x,\tau_{1,*}}^{\varepsilon},R_{x,\tau_{2,*}}^{\varepsilon}
are excluded in the definition
X^{\varepsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle\tan\eta_{j}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\kappa(\tau_{j})\gamma_{1}(\tau_{j})}{|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{j})|}}-\varepsilon,

by angular momentum conservation. This implies that
\eta_{j}\leq\frac{\pi}{2}-C\varepsilon
for some positive constant
C
due to
\tau_{j}\in[\tau_{1,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{1,*}+\varepsilon]\cup[\tau_{2,*}-\varepsilon,\tau_{2,*}+\varepsilon]
. It remains to check whether (
4.7
) in Lemma
4.4
holds. Similarly, we suppose that there exists
j
such that

\displaystyle x^{j}(x,v)=\sigma(\tau_{j},\varphi_{j}),\quad\tau_{j}\in(Z_{h})_{\varepsilon},

where
(Z_{h})_{\varepsilon}
was defined in (
4.8
). Since we also exclude
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}R_{x,\tau_{i}^{\mathbf{z}}}^{\varepsilon}
in
X^{\varepsilon}
, it holds that

\displaystyle|\tan\eta_{j}-\tan\vartheta_{j}|\geq\varepsilon,

which is identical to (
4.7
). Therefore, we can apply Lemma
4.6
and obtain finite number of bounce during travel length
L
whenever
(x,v)
satisfies (
4.66
).
G_{L}^{\varepsilon}
satisfies condition of this Lemma by definition (
4.65
). Reversibility of trajectory with under finite bounce is also obvious. ∎

When backward in time trajectory belongs to
\gamma_{0}^{I}
, the trajectory cannot be defined anymore. Hence, using reversibility of trajectory, we can construct corresponding backward in time image of
B_{L}^{\varepsilon}
in
(\tau,\varphi)
domain as follows.

\begin{split}\mathcal{B}_{L}^{\varepsilon}&:=\Big\{(\tau,\varphi)\in[a,b)\times(-2\pi\mathbf{w},0]:(X,V)(s(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),\varphi,I^{2}(\tau,\varphi));0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))\in B_{L}^{\varepsilon}\Big\}\\
&=(X,V)^{-1}(s)(B_{L}^{\varepsilon}),\end{split}

(4.67)

by definition (
4.40
). In particular, we have one-to-one correspondence between
B_{L}^{\varepsilon}
and
\mathcal{B}_{L}^{\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 4.18
.

Recall definition
G^{\varepsilon}_{L}
and
B^{\varepsilon}_{L}
in (
4.65
). The sets have the following properties:

(a)
G_{L}^{\varepsilon}
is open in
X^{\varepsilon}
.

(b)
B_{L}^{\varepsilon}
closed in
X^{\varepsilon}
and
\mathcal{B}_{L}^{\varepsilon}
is also closed. Both are compact.

Proof.

proof of (a) :
Let
(x,v)\in G^{\varepsilon}_{L}
and
(y,u)\in X^{\varepsilon}
. By Lemma
4.17
,
\mathcal{N}(x,v,L)<\infty
. Also, from (c) in Lemma
4.10
, we can choose small
\delta_{1}>0
such that
\mathcal{N}(y,u,L)\leq\mathcal{N}(x,v,L)
if
|(x,v)-(y,u)|<\delta_{1}
. Let us write
\mathcal{N}_{x,v}=\mathcal{N}(x,v,L)
to simplify notation here. Since
(x,v)\in G^{\varepsilon}_{L}
, for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle|(x^{j}(x,v),v^{j-1}(x,v))-(x^{j}(y,u),v^{j-1}(y,u))|<\varepsilon,\quad\forall 1\leq j\leq\mathcal{N}_{x,v},

whenever
|(x,v)-(y,u)|<\delta
by using (a) of Lemma
4.10
. Moreover,
(x^{j}(y,u),v^{j-1}(y,u))

\notin\gamma_{0}^{I_{-}}
for all
1\leq j\leq\mathcal{N}_{y,u}(\leq\mathcal{N}_{x,v})
. Hence, if we take
\delta=\min\{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\}
, then
(y,u)\in G^{\varepsilon}_{L}
whenever
|(x,v)-(y,u)|<\delta
.

proof of (b) :
Note that
X^{\varepsilon}=G_{L}^{\varepsilon}\cupdot B_{L}^{\varepsilon}
. By (a) of Lemma
4.18
, we directly deduce
B^{\varepsilon}_{L}
is closed in
X^{\varepsilon}
. For
(x,v)\in B_{L}^{\varepsilon}
, there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(x^{k}(x,v),v^{k-1}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{I_{-}}
and
(x^{j}(x,v),v^{j-1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I_{-}}
for all
1\leq j\leq k-1
. By definition of
B_{L}^{\varepsilon}\subset X^{\varepsilon}
, the number of bounce in trajectory during travel length
L
is finite. Again, by Lemma
4.17
, forward in time trajectory from
\mathcal{B}_{L}^{\varepsilon}
is well-defined until it arrives
\overline{S_{0}}
. By Lemma
4.12
,
(X(s(\cdot);0,\cdot),V(s(\cdot);0,\cdot))
is continuous on
\mathcal{B}_{L}^{\varepsilon}
. Using local diffeomorphism
(\sigma,I^{2}):(\tau,\varphi)\mapsto(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))
and the fact that the inverse image of closed set under continuous function is closed, we finish the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.19
.

Recall the definition (
4.28
) of
\mathcal{N}(x,v,L)
. We have the following uniform finite number of bounce

\displaystyle\sup_{(x,v)\in B_{L}^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{N}(x,v,L)<K=K(\varepsilon,L),

where the constant
K
depends on
\varepsilon
and
L
.

i.e., Up to travel length
L>0
and for
(x,v)\in B_{L}^{\varepsilon}
, backward in time trajectory which experiences inflection grazing
\gamma_{0}^{I-}
within travel length
L
cannot enjoy arbitrarily large number of bounce.

Proof.

Let us recall
\mathcal{B}_{L}^{\varepsilon}
is compact by (b) in Lemma
4.18
.
Let us choose
(\tau,\varphi)\in\mathcal{B}_{L}^{\varepsilon}
. Using the forward trajectory, we define the number of bounce
\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,L)
during whole travel length
L
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,L):=\sup\left\{k\in\mathbb{N}:(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I_{-}}\hskip 9.24994pt\forall 1\leq j\leq k,\;\sum_{j=1}^{k}|x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau,\varphi)-x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi)|\leq L\right\},\end{split}

(4.68)

where we used abbreviation
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1})(\tau,\varphi)=(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1})(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))
. We suppose that
[x_{\mathbf{f}}^{i}(\tau,\varphi),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{i-1}(\tau,\varphi)]\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}
for all
1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,L)
. Next, we split two cases:

(Case 1)
There is no concave grazing in a finite travel length. i.e.,
[x_{\mathbf{f}}^{i}(\tau,\varphi),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{i-1}(\tau,\varphi)]

\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}
for all
1\leq i\leq\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,L)
.

(Case 2)
There is a concave grazing at least once. i.e., There exists
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
[x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau,\varphi),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi)]\in\gamma_{0}^{C}
.

In
(Case 1)
,
(x^{i}(\tau,\varphi),v^{i}(\tau,\varphi))
and
(X(s;0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi)),V(s;0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi)))
are continuous in
(\tau,\varphi)
by (a) and (b) of Lemma
4.10
. Hence, there exists
r^{1}=r^{1}_{\tau,\varphi,\epsilon}>0
such that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,L)-\mathcal{N}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*},L)|\leq 1,

whenever
|(\tau,\varphi)-(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*})|\leq r^{1}_{\tau,\varphi,\varepsilon}
.

In
(Case 2)
, we use the fact that there is no consecutive concave grazing in
\Omega
. Suppose that
[x_{\mathbf{f}}^{k}(\tau,\varphi),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{k-1}(\tau,\varphi)]\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}
for all
1\leq k<j
and
[x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau,\varphi),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi)]\in\gamma_{0}^{C}
. If we choose sufficiently small
r^{2}=r^{2}_{j,\tau,\varphi,\varepsilon}>0
such that
|(\tau,\varphi)-(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*})|<r^{2}_{j,\tau,\varphi,\varepsilon}
, then
[x_{\mathbf{f}}^{k}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*}),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{k-1}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*})]\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}
for all
1\leq k<j
by continuity argument. Similar to (Case 1), we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi))-\mathcal{N}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*},t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi))|\leq 1,

where
t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi):=t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))
. Moreover, one obtains that

\displaystyle|(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi))-(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*}),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-2}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*}))|<\delta,

(4.69)

where
\delta>0
was defined in Lemma
4.8
. Note that
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j+1}(\tau,\varphi),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau,\varphi))\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}
since there is no consecutive concave grazing from Lemma
4.7
.
We split
[x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*}),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*})]
into three cases:

(i)

\overline{x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*})x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*})}
does not bounce near
x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau,\varphi)
: This case coincides with (b-2) in Lemma
4.8
for
x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}
. Hence, from (b-2) in Lemma
4.8
and (
4.69
), we obtain

\displaystyle|(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j+1}(\tau,\varphi),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j+1}(\tau,\varphi))-(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*}),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*}))|<\varepsilon,

if
|(\tau,\varphi)-(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*})|<r^{2}_{j,\tau,\varphi,\varepsilon}
. Also, (a) in Lemma
4.10
implies that
t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}
is locally continuous function of
(\tau,\varphi)
up to some renumbered bounce number, that is,
|t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j+1}(\tau,\varphi)-t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*})|<\varepsilon
. Hence,
\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j+1}(\tau,\varphi))=\mathcal{N}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*},t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*}))+1
, and

\displaystyle|\left(\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j+1}(\tau,\varphi))-\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi))\right)-\left(\mathcal{N}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*},t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j+1}(\tau,\varphi)-\mathcal{N}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*},t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi))\right)|\leq 2.

(ii)

x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*})
is located near
x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau,\varphi)
and
[(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*}),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*})]\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}:
(b-1) in Lemma
4.8
includes this case. Applying (b-1) in Lemma
4.8
and (
4.69
) yields that

\displaystyle|(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{k}(\tau,\varphi),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{k}(\tau,\varphi))-(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{k}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*}),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{k}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*}))|<\varepsilon,\quad k=j,j+1,

if
|(\tau,\varphi)-(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*})|<r^{2}_{j,\tau,\varphi,\varepsilon}
. Similarly, in this case, it holds that
|t_{\mathbf{f}}^{k}(\tau,\varphi)-t_{\mathbf{f}}^{k}(\tau,\varphi)|<\varepsilon
for
k=j,j+1
due to continuity of
t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}
. Hence,

\displaystyle|\left(\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j+1}(\tau,\varphi))-\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi))\right)-\left(\mathcal{N}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*},t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j+1}(\tau,\varphi)-\mathcal{N}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*},t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi))\right)|\leq 1.

(iii)

x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*})
is located near
x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau,\varphi)
and
[(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*}),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*})]\in\gamma_{0}^{C}:
This case is also included in (b-1) in Lemma
4.8
. Thus, similar to (2-2), we obtain the same result above.

By Lemma
4.7
, the maximum number of bounces where concave grazing occurs is
[\frac{\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,L)}{2}]
during a finite travel length
L
. We set
r^{2}_{\tau,\varphi,\varepsilon}:=\min\{r^{1}_{\tau,\varphi,\varepsilon},\bigcup_{j\in\mathcal{C}_{\tau,\varphi}}r^{2}_{j,\tau,\varphi,\varepsilon}\}
where
\mathcal{C}_{\tau,\varphi}=\{l\in\mathbb{N}:(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{l}(\tau,\varphi),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{l-1}(\tau,\varphi))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}\}\;(\max\mathcal{C}_{\tau,\varphi}\leq\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,L)).
Considering three cases above, we derive that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,L)-\mathcal{N}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*},L)|\lesssim\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,L),

if
|(\tau,\varphi)-(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*})|<r^{2}_{\tau,\varphi,\varepsilon}
.

To get a uniform number of bounces, we use a compactness argument.
Since the range
\mathcal{B}_{L}^{\varepsilon}
of
(\tau,\varphi)
is compact, using a compactness argument gives uniform boundedness for the number of bounces. For each
(\tau,\varphi)\in\mathcal{B}_{L}^{\varepsilon}
, we construct a small ball
B_{\delta_{\tau,\varphi,\varepsilon}}(\tau,\varphi)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, where
\delta_{\tau,\varphi,\varepsilon}:=\min\{r^{1}_{\tau,\varphi,\varepsilon},r^{2}_{\tau,\varphi,\varepsilon}\}
. Then, for
(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*})\in B_{\delta_{\tau,\varphi,\varepsilon}}(\tau,\varphi)
, it satisfies that

|\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,L)-\mathcal{N}(\tau^{*},\varphi^{*},L)|\lesssim\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,L).

Thus, by compactness of
\mathcal{B}_{L}^{\varepsilon}
, there exists a finite open covering
\cup_{j=1}^{l}B_{\delta^{j}_{\tau_{j},\varphi_{j},\varepsilon}}(\tau_{j},\varphi_{j})
. Since we exclude
R_{x,\tau_{1,*}}^{\varepsilon},R_{x,\tau_{2,*}}^{\varepsilon}
in
X^{\varepsilon}
and
B_{L}^{\varepsilon}
is a subset of
X^{\varepsilon}
, for any
(\tau,\varphi)\in\mathcal{B}_{L}^{\varepsilon}
, we have

\mathcal{N}(\tau,\varphi,L)\lesssim\max_{1\leq j\leq l}\mathcal{N}(\tau_{j},\varphi_{j},L)<+\infty,

where the finiteness can be obtained by Lemma
4.6
. Then, trajectory is deterministic and reversible, which gives one-to-one correspondence between
B_{L}^{\varepsilon}
and
\mathcal{B}_{L}^{\varepsilon}
. Hence, we finish the proof.
∎

4.3.
Uniform number of bounce away from inflection grazing
\gamma_{0}^{I}

Through Proposition
4.19
, we obtain that an inflection grazing could only happen within the maximum
K
bounce in backward in time trajectory starting at
(x,v)\in B_{L}^{\varepsilon}
defined in (
4.65
). To remove all inflection grazing, for fixed
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
, we will construct bad sets
\{(B_{j})_{x}\}_{j=1}^{K}\subset\mathbb{S}^{2}
where inflection grazing does happen on the
j
-th bounce within the backward in time trajectory starting from
x
. Moreover, the bad sets also contain concave grazing to use analyticity such as Lemma
4.10
by excluding them.

Let
K
be an upper bound for the number of bounces in Proposition
4.19
. Now we make slightly different decomposition of
X^{\varepsilon}
as follows for fixed
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
,

\begin{split}(G_{1})_{x}&:=\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon},\ (x^{1}(x,v),v^{0}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I}\},\\
(B_{1})_{x}&:=\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon},\ (x^{1}(x,v),v^{0}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I}\},\\
(G_{2})_{x}&:=\{v\in(G_{1})_{x}:(x^{2}(x,v),v^{1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I}\},\\
(B_{2})_{x}&:=\{v\in(G_{1})_{x}:(x^{2}(x,v),v^{1}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I}\},\\
\vdots\\
(G_{K})_{x}&:=\{v\in(G_{K-1})_{x}:(x^{K}(x,v),v^{K-1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I}\},\\
(B_{K})_{x}&:=\{v\in(G_{K-1})_{x}:(x^{K}(x,v),v^{K-1}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I}\}.\end{split}

(4.70)

We note that for fixed
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
,

\begin{split}(X^{\varepsilon})_{x}&:=\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon}\}\\
&=(B_{1})_{x}\cupdot(G_{1})_{x}=(B_{1})_{x}\cupdot\big((B_{2})_{x}\cupdot(G_{2})_{x}\big)\\
&=\cdots\\
&=\Big(\bigcupdot_{j=1}^{K}(B_{j})_{x}\Big)\cupdot(G_{K})_{x}.\end{split}

(4.71)

For fixed
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
, we assume that
(B_{j})_{x}
in (
4.70
) has measure zero for each
1\leq j\leq K
. (this will be proved in Proposition
5.2
.) If we define (set of bad directions)

\displaystyle\mathcal{BK}_{x}:=\bigcup_{j=1}^{K}(B_{j})_{x}\subset\mathbb{S}^{2},

(4.72)

there exists an open set
\mathcal{BK}_{x}^{\varepsilon}\subset\mathbb{S}^{2}
such that

\mathcal{BK}_{x}\subset\mathcal{BK}_{x}^{\varepsilon},\quad\mathfrak{m}_{2}\big(\mathcal{BK}_{x}^{\varepsilon}\big)<\varepsilon.

(4.73)

Lemma 4.20
.

For
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
,
\varepsilon>0
, recall definition
\mathcal{BK}_{x}
in (
4.72
). For fixed
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
. For each
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
, there exists
r=r(x,\varepsilon)
such that

\mathcal{BK}_{y}\subset\mathcal{BK}_{x}^{\varepsilon},

if
y\in B(x,r(x,\varepsilon))\cap\overline{S_{0}}
.

Proof.

We use a contradiction argument to prove Lemma. Suppose that there exists
v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
satisfying

v\in(\mathcal{BK}_{y})\cap(\mathcal{BK}_{x}^{\varepsilon})^{c}.

Then, one obtains that
(x^{j}(y,v),v^{j}(y,v))\in\gamma_{0}
for some
1\leq j\leq K
and
(x^{k}(x,v),v^{k}(x,

v))\notin\gamma_{0}
for all
1\leq k\leq K
. By (a) in Lemma
4.10
, one obtains that
(x^{i}(x,v),v^{i}(x,v))
is locally continuous of
(x,v)
, which leads to a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 4.21
.

For fixed
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
, assume that
\mathfrak{m}_{2}((B_{j})_{x})=0
for all
1\leq j\leq K
where we defined
(B_{j})_{x}
in (
4.71
). Recall the definition (
4.62
) of
X^{\varepsilon}
in Lemma
4.16
. Then, there exists a compact set
\mathcal{K}^{c}\subset\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
such that if
(x,v)\in\mathcal{K}^{c}
,

(x^{i}(x,v),v^{i-1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}\cup\gamma_{0}^{C},\quad\text{for first $K$ bounce},

and

(x^{i}(x,v),v^{i-1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I},\quad\text{up to whole travel length $L$}.

Moreover, for
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\{\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash\mathcal{K}^{c})_{x})\lesssim\varepsilon,

where we have used the notation
A_{x}
in (
4.46
).

Proof.

Let us define

E:=\Big\{(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon}:v\in\mathcal{BK}_{x}\Big\},

(4.74)

where
X^{\varepsilon}
is defined in (
4.62
) and
\mathcal{BK}_{x}
is defined in (
4.72
). Recall that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(X^{\varepsilon})=\overline{S_{0}}
from (
4.63
) in proof of Lemma
4.16
. Using compactness of
\mathcal{P}_{1}(X^{\varepsilon})=\overline{S_{0}}
, Lemma
4.20
and (
4.73
) from our assumption, we obtain an open cover for
\mathcal{P}_{1}(X^{\varepsilon})=\overline{S_{0}}

\Big\{B(x_{i}^{\mathcal{K}},r_{i}^{\mathcal{K}})\cap\overline{S_{0}}\Big\}_{i=1}^{l_{K}},

(4.75)

and corresponding each open set
\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\mathcal{K}}\subset\mathbb{S}^{2}
which is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\mathcal{K}}:=\mathcal{BK}_{x_{i}^{\mathcal{K}}}^{\varepsilon},\quad 1\leq i\leq l_{K},

(4.76)

satisfying
\mathbf{m}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\mathcal{K}})<\varepsilon
for all
1\leq i\leq l_{K}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{BK}_{x}\subset\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\mathcal{K}},

(4.77)

for all
x\in B(x_{i}^{\mathcal{K}},r_{i}^{\mathcal{K}})
and sufficiently small
r_{i}^{\mathcal{K}}
.
Similar to (
4.47
) and (
4.59
), for given
x\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(X^{\varepsilon})=\overline{S_{0}}
, we define an index set

\displaystyle I_{x}^{\mathcal{K}}:=\Big\{i\in\mathbb{N}:x\in B(x_{i}^{\mathcal{K}},r_{i}^{\mathcal{K}})\cap\overline{S_{0}}\Big\}.

(4.78)

We consider the following set containing set
E
defined in (
4.74
):

\displaystyle\bigcup_{x\in\overline{S_{0}}}\Big\{(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon}:v\in\bigcap_{i\in I_{x}^{\mathcal{K}}}\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\mathcal{K}}\Big\}.

(4.79)

Similar to (
4.61
) in proof of Lemma
4.16
, we construct an open cover for (
4.79
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{l_{K}}\Big\{(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon}:x\in B(x_{i}^{\mathcal{K}},r_{i}^{\mathcal{K}})\cap\overline{S_{0}},\;v\in\bigcap_{j\in I_{x}^{\mathcal{K}}}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathcal{K}}\Big\}_{\varepsilon},

(4.80)

where we have used the notation
A_{\varepsilon}
in (
4.51
). Throughout this paper, we denote a compact set
X^{\varepsilon}\backslash\mathcal{K}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{c}:=X^{\varepsilon}\backslash\mathcal{K}.

(4.81)

Assume that
(x,v)\in\mathcal{K}^{c}=X^{\varepsilon}\backslash\mathcal{K}
.
Since definition (
4.80
) of
\mathcal{K}
and (
4.77
),
v\notin\mathcal{BK}_{x}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{K}(B_{j})_{x}
. This implies that

\displaystyle(x^{i}(x,v),v^{i-1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}\cup\gamma_{0}^{C},\quad\forall 1\leq i\leq K.

From Proposition
4.19
, during the travel length
L
, the maximum number of bounce that inflection grazing can occur is
K
. For
(x,v)\in\mathcal{K}^{c}
, there is no inflection grazing for first
K
bounce. Hence,
(x^{i}(x,v),v^{i-1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}
during the travel length
L
. For fixed
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{x}=\Big\{v\in(X^{\varepsilon})_{x}:v\in\bigcap_{j\in I_{x}^{\mathcal{K}}}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathcal{K}}\Big\}_{\varepsilon},

(4.82)

where we used notation
A_{x}
in (
4.46
). Notice that the notation
A_{\varepsilon}
in (
4.51
) is caused by the definition (
4.80
). From the definition (
4.81
) of
\mathcal{K}^{c}
, one obtains that

\displaystyle\{\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash\mathcal{K}^{c}=(\{\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash X^{\varepsilon})\cupdot\mathcal{K}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\{\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash\mathcal{K}^{c})_{x})&\leq\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\{\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash X^{\varepsilon})_{x})+\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\mathcal{K}_{x})\\
&\leq\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\{\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash X^{\varepsilon})_{x})+\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathcal{K}})_{\varepsilon})\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon,\quad\forall j\in I_{x}^{\mathcal{K}},\end{split}

(4.83)

by using (
4.64
),(
4.82
) and
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathcal{K}})\lesssim\varepsilon
for all
j=1,2,\dots,l_{K}
.
∎

Now we obtain uniform number of bounce on
\mathcal{K}^{c}=X^{\varepsilon}\backslash\mathcal{K}
.

Lemma 4.22
.

\mathcal{N}(x,v,L)
in (
4.28
) is pointwisely finite for each
(x,v)\in\mathcal{K}^{c}
by Proposition
4.6
. We have a uniform number of bounces on
\mathcal{K}^{c}
set, i.e.,

\sup_{(x,v)\in\mathcal{K}^{c}}\mathcal{N}(x,v,L)\leq M=M(\varepsilon,L),

where the constant
M
depends on
\varepsilon
and
L
.

Proof.

Since we have excluded
B_{L}^{\varepsilon}\subset\cupdot_{j=1}^{K}(B_{j})
uniformly in the definition of
\mathcal{K}^{c}
, there is no more inflection grazing bounce. Now, using similar argument which was used in the proof of Proposition
4.19
(for counting) and compactness of
\mathcal{K}^{c}
(because
\mathcal{K}
is open in
X^{\varepsilon}\subset\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
), we get the result.
∎

Using above number of bounces away from
\mathcal{K}
, let us assume that
M
of Lemma
4.22
is bigger than
K
of Proposition
4.19
WLOG. Since
(x^{j}(x,v),v^{j-1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}
for
(x,v)\in\mathcal{K}^{c}
and
K+1\leq j\leq M
, we define the following (similar to (
4.70
))

\begin{split}(G_{K+1})_{x}&:=\{v\in(G_{K})_{x}:(x^{K+1}(x,v),v^{K}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}\},\\
(B_{K+1})_{x}&:=\{v\in(G_{K})_{x}:(x^{K+1}(x,v),v^{K}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}\},\\
\vdots\\
(G_{M})_{x}&:=\{v\in(G_{M-1})_{x}:(x^{M}(x,v),v^{M-1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}\},\\
(B_{M})_{x}&:=\{v\in(G_{M-1})_{x}:(x^{M}(x,v),v^{M-1}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}\},\\
\end{split}

(4.84)

with decomposition

\begin{split}(X^{\varepsilon})_{x}:=\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:(x,v)\in X^{\varepsilon}\}=\Big(\cupdot_{j=1}^{M}(B_{j})_{x}\Big)\cupdot(G_{M})_{x}.\\
\end{split}

(4.85)

5.
Construction of Bad sets

5.1.
Construction of
\mathcal{IB}
set

In this section, we prove that
\mathfrak{m}_{2}((B_{j})_{x})=0
for all
1\leq j\leq M
which we have assumed from (
4.72
).

Lemma 5.1
.

(Lusin’s property)
Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Lipschitz continuous function. Then,
f(E)
has measure zero in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for a measure zero set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Proof.

Let
E
be a set of measure zero in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exist countably many open balls
E\subset\cup_{j=1}^{\infty}B(x_{j},r_{j})
with

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathfrak{m}_{n}(B(x_{j},r_{j}))\leq\varepsilon,

where
\mathfrak{m}_{n}
is Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
B(x,r)
be an open ball centered at
x
with radius
r
. By Lipschitz continuity of
f
, for each
j
,
f(B(x_{j},r_{j}))
is contained in a ball of radius
L\cdot r_{j}
centered
f(0)
where
L
is the Lipschitz constant of
f
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{n}^{*}(f(B(x_{j},r_{j})))\leq L^{n}\mathfrak{m}_{n}(B(x_{j},r_{j})),

where
\mathfrak{m}_{n}^{*}
be Lebesgue outer measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Hence, we get

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{n}^{*}(f(E))\leq L^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathfrak{m}_{n}(B(x_{j},r_{j}))<L^{n}\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary,
f(E)
has zero outer measure, which means
f(E)
is a null set in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
∎

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
M
be the constant defined in Lemma
4.22
and
S_{0}
be
\varphi=0
cross-section in (
1.49
). Here, the constant
M=M(\varepsilon,L)
depends on
\varepsilon
and travel length
L
. We denote
\overline{S_{0}}
in Definition
1.13
as closure of
S_{0}
. Recall that we constructed bad direction sets
\{B_{j}\}_{j=1}^{M}
in (
4.70
) and (
4.84
). Then, for fixed
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
,

\mathfrak{m}_{2}((B_{j})_{x})=0,\quad 1\leq j\leq M.

Proof.

For fixed
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
and
1\leq j\leq M
, we defined the set
(B_{j})_{x}
as

\displaystyle(B_{j})_{x}=\{v\in(G_{j-1})_{x}:(x^{j}(x,v),v^{j-1}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I}\},

where
(G_{j-1})_{x}
was constructed inductively in (
4.70
) and (
4.84
). To get
\mathfrak{m}_{2}((B_{j})_{x})=0
,
We separate into concave grazing (
\gamma_{0}^{C}
) and inflection grazing (
\gamma_{0}^{I}
).

Step 1
In this step, we deal with concave grazing case in
(B_{j})_{x}
. We claim

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\{v\in(G_{j-1})_{x}:(x^{j}(x,v),v^{j-1}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}\})=0.

(
Interior part
S_{0}
)
Firstly, we consider the interior of
\overline{S_{0}}
. Let
x\in{S_{0}}
. Note that we exclude
R_{x,\tau_{1,*}}^{\varepsilon}
and
R_{x,\tau_{2,*}}^{\varepsilon}
from
(X^{\varepsilon})_{x}
defined in (
4.85
). Moreover, inflection grazing does not happen in trajectory between
(x,v)
and
(x^{j}(x,v),v^{j-1}(x,v))
. From Lemma
4.6
, one obtains that trajectory is invertible. Using an invertible map
(X,V)(s(\cdot))
,
(x,v)
can be expressed by

\displaystyle\begin{split}(x,v)=(X,V)(s(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),\varphi,v_{c}(\eta));0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v_{c}(\eta)),\end{split}

where
s(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
be the arrival time defined in (
4.34
),
\sigma(\cdot,\cdot)
be the parametrization of boundary
\partial\Omega
in (
1.35
), and
v_{c}(\cdot)
be parametrization for concave grazing in (
3.34
).
We reparametrize into

\begin{split}Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}&:=\left\{(\tau,\varphi,\eta):\begin{array}[]{cc}(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v_{c}(\eta))=(x^{j}(y,u),v^{j-1}(y,u))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}\\
\ \text{for some}\ (y,u)\in{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2},\ u\in(G_{j-1})_{y}\end{array}\right\}.\end{split}

(5.1)

We consider

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{j}(\tau,\varphi,\eta):=X(s(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),\varphi,v_{c}(\eta));0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v_{c}(\eta))-x,\quad(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\in Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}.

First, for analyticity of the function
\tilde{F}_{j}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)
on
Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
later, we should claim that
Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
is open in
(\tau,\varphi,\eta)
space.
Note that
\sigma
in (
1.35
) and
v_{c}
in (
3.34
) are all locally smooth. Now, let us consider forward in time trajectory from
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v_{c}(\eta))=(x^{j}(y,u),v^{j-1}(y,u))
. By definition of
(G_{j-1})_{y}
and Lemma
4.17
, the trajectory is non-grazing until it arrives at
(y,u)
and hence the map

(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\mapsto(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v_{c}(\eta))\mapsto(X,V)(s(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),\varphi,v_{c}(\eta));0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v_{c}(\eta))\in S_{0}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}

is continuous with finite number of bounce by Lemma
4.12
. Now, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta\ll 1
such that if
|(\tau,\varphi,\eta)-(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})|<\delta
, then

|(y,u)-(y^{\prime},u^{\prime})|<\varepsilon,

where

(y^{\prime},u^{\prime}):=(X,V)(s(\sigma(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}),\varphi^{\prime},v_{c}(\eta^{\prime}));0,\sigma(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}),v_{c}(\eta^{\prime}))\in S_{0}\times\mathbb{S}^{2},

or equivalently,

(\sigma(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}),v_{c}(\eta^{\prime}))=(x^{j}(y^{\prime},u^{\prime}),v^{j-1}(y^{\prime},u^{\prime}))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}.

Since
(y,u)\in G_{L}^{\varepsilon}
and
G_{L}^{\varepsilon}
is open by (a) in Lemma
4.18
, choosing
\varepsilon
sufficiently small,
(y^{\prime},u^{\prime})\in G_{L}^{\varepsilon}
either. Moreover, since
x\in S_{0}
, if we choose sufficiently small
\varepsilon
so that
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}\text{dist}(x,\partial S_{0})
WLOG, applying Lemma
4.10
for forward in time trajectory from
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v_{c}(\eta))
, we obtain
u^{\prime}\in(G_{j-1})_{x^{\prime}}
.
And, by definition (
5.1
) of
Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
, we obtain that
(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\in Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
which means that
Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
is open. Moreover,
\tilde{F}_{j}
is locally analytic on
Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
by Lemma
4.12
.

Now, let us use coordinate vectors
\{\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{\varphi}\}
where
\hat{x},\hat{y}
are perpendicular coordinate in
S_{0}
and
\hat{\varphi}
is perpendicular to
S_{0}
such as (
1.50
). Specifically, since cross section
S_{0}
in (
1.49
) is on
xz
-plane,
\hat{x}=(1,0,0),\hat{y}=(0,0,1),
and
\hat{\varphi}=(0,1,0)
. Using this coordinate, we write
\tilde{F}_{j}
as

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{F}_{j}^{x}\\
\tilde{F}_{j}^{y}\\
\tilde{F}_{j}^{\varphi}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}X_{1}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)-x_{1}\\
X_{3}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)-x_{3}\\
X_{2}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)-x_{2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}X_{1}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)-x_{1}\\
X_{3}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)-x_{3}\\
0\end{bmatrix},

(5.2)

where
X(s(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),\varphi,v_{c}(\eta));0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v_{c}(\eta))=(X_{1}(\tau,\varphi,\eta),X_{2}(\tau,\varphi,\eta),X_{3}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)),

x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in S_{0}
.
By definition of
s(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),\varphi,\eta)
in (
4.34
), we know that
\tilde{F}_{j}^{\varphi}\equiv 0
. Note that we abbreviate notation:

\displaystyle(X,V)(s(\tau,\varphi,\eta);0,\tau,\varphi,\eta):=(X,V)(s(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),\varphi,v_{c}(\eta));0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v_{c}(\eta)).

(5.3)

We split things into several cases:

(Case 1)

X(s(\tau,\varphi,\eta);0,\tau,\varphi,\eta)
is not a constant vector with respect to
(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\in Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
.

Assume that
\tilde{F}_{j}=0
at some point
(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})\in Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
. Motivated by the
z
-axial symmetry structure, we further split cases:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\textit{(Case 1-a)}\;\left.\frac{\partial\tilde{F}_{j}}{\partial\varphi}\right|_{(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})}\neq 0,\\
\\
\textit{(Case 1-b)}\;\left.\frac{\partial\tilde{F}_{j}}{\partial\varphi}\right|_{(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})}=0.\end{cases}

In
(Case 1-a)
, at
(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})
, we have either

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial\tilde{F}_{j}^{x}}{\partial\varphi}\right|_{(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})}\neq 0\quad\textrm{ or }\quad\left.\frac{\partial\tilde{F}_{j}^{y}}{\partial\varphi}\right|_{(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})}\neq 0,

where
\tilde{F}_{j}=(\tilde{F}_{j}^{x},\tilde{F}_{j}^{y},\tilde{F}_{j}^{\varphi})
in (
5.2
). WLOG, we assume that
\left.\frac{\partial\tilde{F}_{j}^{x}}{\partial\varphi}\right|_{(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})}\neq 0
. Applying Lemma
4.3
, there exists an open set
U_{\tau_{0},\eta_{0}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
containing
(\tau_{0},\eta_{0})
such that there exists an analytic function
\varphi=\varphi(\tau,\eta)
such that

\displaystyle\varphi(\tau_{0},\eta_{0})=\varphi_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{F}_{j}^{x}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)=0\;\text{for all }(\tau,\eta)\in\tilde{U}_{\tau_{0},\eta_{0}},

where
\tilde{U}_{\tau_{0},\eta_{0}}:=\Big\{(\tau,\eta)\in U_{\tau_{0},\eta_{0}}:(\sigma(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta)),v_{c}(\eta))=(x^{j}(y,u),v^{j-1}(y,u))\in\gamma_{0}^{C}\;\text{for}

\text{some }(y,u)\in S_{0}\times\mathbb{S}^{2},u\in(G_{j-1})_{y}\Big\}
.
If
\tilde{F}_{j}^{y}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)
is not identically zero on
\tilde{U}_{\tau_{0},\eta_{0}}
, then the zero set
\mathcal{Z}_{x}^{F,j,C}
of
\tilde{F}_{j}^{y}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{x}^{F,j,C}:=\{(\tau,\eta)\in\tilde{U}_{\tau_{0},\eta_{0}}:\tilde{F}_{j}^{y}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)=0\}

(5.4)

has measure zero in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by Lemma
3.1
. Note that the map

\displaystyle(\tau,\eta)\mapsto V(s(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta);0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta)),v_{c}(\eta))

is locally analytic on
\tilde{U}_{\tau_{0},\eta_{0}}
because
\varphi(\tau,\eta)
is locally analytic on
U_{\tau_{0},\eta_{0}}
and Lemma
4.12
. Also, the zero set
\mathcal{Z}_{x}^{F,j,C}
of the analytic function
\tilde{F}_{j}^{y}
is compact. Due to the fact that analytic function on compact set is Lipschitz function, we can apply Lemma
5.1
, and then

\displaystyle\left\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:v=V(s(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta);0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta)),v_{c}(\eta))\quad\textrm{for }(\tau,\eta)\in\mathcal{Z}_{x}^{F,j,C}\right\}

(5.5)

has measure zero in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

Otherwise, if
\tilde{F}_{j}^{y}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)
is identically zero on
\tilde{U}_{\tau_{0},\eta_{0}}
, then
\tilde{F}_{j}^{x}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)=\tilde{F}_{j}^{y}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)
for all
(\tau,\eta)\in\tilde{U}_{\tau_{0},\eta_{0}}
. By differentiating
\tilde{F}_{j}
with respect to
\varphi
, then from
z
-axial symmetry of our domain
\Omega
,

\begin{split}\left.\frac{\partial\tilde{F}_{j}}{\partial\varphi}\right|_{(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})}&=\left.\frac{\partial X(s;0,\tau,\varphi,\eta)}{\partial s}\frac{\partial s}{\partial\varphi}\right|_{s=s(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0}),(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})}\\
&\quad+\left.\frac{\partial X(s;0,\tau,\varphi,\eta)}{\partial\varphi}\right|_{s=s(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0}),(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})}.\end{split}

(5.6)

We claim

\left.\frac{\partial X(s;0,\tau,\varphi,\eta)}{\partial\varphi}\right|_{s=s(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0}),(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})}=k_{\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0}}\hat{\varphi}(0),

for some constant
k_{\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0}}
, where
\hat{\varphi}(0)
in Definition
1.49
. First, let us define the
z
-axial rotation operator

\displaystyle R_{\Delta\varphi}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\Delta\varphi&-\sin\Delta\varphi&0\\
\sin\Delta\varphi&\cos\Delta\varphi&0\\
0&0&1\\
\end{pmatrix},

for some
\Delta\varphi\in\mathbb{R}
. If we write
(y,u)=R_{\Delta\varphi}(x,v)
, by axial symmetric property of
\Omega
, we have
R_{\Delta\varphi}\overline{\Omega}=\overline{\Omega}
for any
\Delta\varphi\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence,
R_{-\Delta\varphi}X(s;0,y,u)=X(s;0,x,v)
which is equivalent to

X(s;0,R_{\Delta\varphi}x,R_{\Delta\varphi}v)=R_{\Delta\varphi}X(s;0,x,v).

(5.7)

Therefore, for fixed
s\in\mathbb{R}
, using (
5.7
) gives

\begin{split}&\left.\frac{\partial X(s;0,\tau,\varphi,\eta)}{\partial\varphi}\right|_{(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})}\\
&=\left.\frac{\partial X(s;0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v_{c}(\eta))}{\partial\varphi}\right|_{(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})}\\
&=\lim_{\Delta\varphi\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{\Delta\varphi}\big(X(s;0,\sigma(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0}+\Delta\varphi),v_{c}(\eta_{0}))-X(s;0,\sigma(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0}),v_{c}(\eta_{0}))\big)\\
&=\lim_{\Delta\varphi\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{\Delta\varphi}(R_{\Delta\varphi}-I)X(s;0,\sigma(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0}),v_{c}(\eta_{0}))\\
&=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}X(s;0,\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0}).\\
\end{split}

(5.8)

At
s=s(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})=s(\sigma(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0}),\varphi_{0},v_{c}(\eta_{0}))
in Lemma
4.12
, from the calculation above, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left.\frac{\partial X(s;0,\tau,\varphi,\eta)}{\partial\varphi}\right|_{s=s(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0}),(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})}&=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}X(s(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0});0,\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})\\
&=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})\\
0\\
X_{3}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})\end{pmatrix}\\
&=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
X_{1}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})\\
0\end{pmatrix}\\
&=k_{\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0}}\hat{\varphi}(0),\end{split}

(5.9)

where
X(s(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0});0,\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})=(X_{1}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0}),0,X_{3}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0}))\in S_{0}
and
\hat{\varphi}(0)
was defined in Definition
1.49
. When we write vectors
V(s(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0});0,\sigma(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0}),v_{c}

(\eta_{0}))
and
\hat{\varphi}(0)
using
\{\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{\varphi}\}
coordinates as

\displaystyle V(s(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0});0,\sigma(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0}),v_{c}(\eta_{0}))=\begin{pmatrix}V_{1}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})\\
V_{3}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})\\
V_{2}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})\end{pmatrix},\quad\hat{\varphi}(0)=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
1\end{pmatrix},

using (
5.2
),(
5.6
), and (
5.9
) gives that

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial\tilde{F}_{j}}{\partial\varphi}\right|_{(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})}

\displaystyle=V(s(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0});0,\sigma(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0}),v_{c}(\eta_{0}))\frac{\partial s}{\partial\varphi}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})+k_{\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0}}\hat{\varphi}(0)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}V_{1}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})\frac{\partial s}{\partial\varphi}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})\\
V_{3}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})\frac{\partial s}{\partial\varphi}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})\\
V_{2}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})\frac{\partial s}{\partial\varphi}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})+k_{\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\left.\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial\tilde{F}_{j}^{x}}{\partial\varphi}\\
\frac{\partial\tilde{F}_{j}^{y}}{\partial\varphi}\\
0\end{bmatrix}\right|_{(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})}.

Now, let us project the last two lines onto the cross-section
S_{0}
. If
\tilde{F}_{j}^{x}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)=\tilde{F}_{j}^{y}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)
hold as our assumption, above equation yields that

\displaystyle V_{1}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})=V_{3}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0}).

However, this is not possible, since we have excluded
R_{sym}^{\varepsilon}
from
X^{\varepsilon}
in Lemma
4.16
. Therefore,
\tilde{F}_{j}^{y}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)
cannot be identically zero.

Lastly, we investigate
(Case 1-b)
. We use the calculation
\frac{\partial\tilde{F}_{j}}{\partial\varphi}
in
(Case 1-a)
. From conservation of angular momentum and (
4.38
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),\varphi,\eta)}\frac{\omega(\mathbf{x}(x,\varphi),v)}{|X(s;0,\mathbf{x}(x,\varphi),v)\times\hat{z}|}ds=\int_{0}^{s(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),\varphi,\eta)}\frac{\omega(x,v)}{|X(s;0,x,v)\times\hat{z}|}ds=-\varphi.

By taking
\varphi
-derivative from the equation above, we have

\frac{\partial s}{\partial\varphi}<0
(remind that we are assuming positive orientation, i.e., trajectory with positive angular momentum only) and

\left.\frac{\partial\tilde{F}_{j}}{\partial\varphi}\right|_{(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})}=V(s(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0});0,\sigma(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0}),v_{c}(\eta_{0}))\frac{\partial s}{\partial\varphi}(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0})+k_{\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0}}\hat{\varphi}(0)=0,

the vector
V(s(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0},\eta_{0});0,\sigma(\tau_{0},\varphi_{0}),v_{c}(\eta_{0}))
has
\hat{\varphi}
direction. This is not possible since we have excluded
R_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}
from
X^{\varepsilon}
in Lemma
4.16
. Hence,
(Case 1-b)
cannot happen.

(Case 2)

X(s(\tau,\varphi,\eta);0,\tau,\varphi,\eta)
is a constant vector for all
(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\in Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
where we defined the set
Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
in (
5.1
).

In this case, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\tilde{F}_{j}}{\partial\varphi}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}(X(s(\tau,\varphi,\eta);0,\tau,\varphi,\eta))=0,\quad\forall(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\in Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}.

From the same argument in
(Case 1-b)
, this case cannot happen.

Now we use compactness. Since
\tilde{F}_{j}
is continuous in its domain
Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
by Lemma
4.12
, the zero set
\mathcal{T}_{x}^{F,j,C}
of the function
\tilde{F}_{j}

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{x}^{F,j,C}:=\{(\tau,\varphi,\eta):\tilde{F}_{j}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=0\}

(5.10)

is compact. For each point
(\tau,\varphi,\eta)
in the set
\mathcal{T}_{x}^{F,j,C}
, we construct an open set
U_{\tau,\eta}
from Lemma
4.3
and zero set such as (
5.4
) by repeating above argument. If we consider projection
\Pi_{(\tau,\eta)}
onto
\tau\times\eta
plane,
\Pi_{(\tau,\eta)}\mathcal{T}_{x}^{F,j,C}:=\{(\tau,\eta):(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\in\mathcal{T}_{x}^{F,j,C}\}
is still compact. So, we have finite subcover for
\Pi_{(\tau,\eta)}\mathcal{T}_{x}^{F,j,C}
, and hence image under
V(s)
map as in (
5.5
) is still zero.

(Boundary
\partial S_{0}
)
Now, we consider the boundary
\partial S_{0}
. Let
x\in\partial S_{0}
. Using the map
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1})
, we have

(x,v)=(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1})(\tau,\varphi,\eta),

where
x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau,\varphi,\eta):=X(t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v^{c}(\eta));0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v^{c}(\eta)),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)

:=V(t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),

v^{c}(\eta));0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v^{c}(\eta))
. Define

\tilde{B}^{j,C}:=\{(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\in[a,b)\times(-2\pi\mathbf{w},0]\times(0,1):(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{i},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{i-1})(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I},\quad\forall 1\leq i\leq j\},

(5.11)

where
\mathbf{w}
be the winding number in (
1.48
). Similar to
Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
, we should check that
\tilde{B}^{j,C}
is open for analyticity of
x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}
. Note that
\sigma
and
v_{c}
are all locally smooth. Now, let us consider forward in time trajectory from
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v_{c}(\eta))
for
(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\in\tilde{B}^{j,C}
. By definition of
\tilde{B}^{j,C}
,

\displaystyle(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{i},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{i-1})(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I},\quad\forall 1\leq i\leq j.

Hence, by (a) of Lemma
4.10
, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta\ll 1
such that if
|(\tau,\varphi,\eta)-(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})|<\delta
, then
x_{\mathbf{f}}^{i}
and
v_{\mathbf{f}}^{i-1}
are analytic at
(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
for all
1\leq i\leq j
. Moreover,

\displaystyle(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{i},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{i-1})(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}\cup\gamma_{0}^{C},\quad\forall 1\leq i\leq j,

which implies
(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\in\tilde{B}^{j,C}
.
We consider

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{j}(\tau,\varphi,\eta):=x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)-x,\quad(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\in\tilde{B}^{j,C},

(5.12)

which is locally analytic due to (a) in Lemma
4.10
. Similar to the interior
S_{0}
case, we split:

(Case 1)

x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)
is a constant point in
\partial\Omega
for all
(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\in\tilde{B}^{j,C}
.

If we put
x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}
into the calculation (
5.8
) and (
5.9
) instead of
X(s;0,\tau,\varphi,\eta)
, then

\displaystyle\frac{\partial x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}}{\partial\varphi}=k_{\tau,\varphi,\eta}\hat{\varphi}(0),

(5.13)

for some constant
k_{\tau,\varphi,\eta}\in\mathbb{R}
, where we defined
\hat{\varphi}(0)
in Definition
1.13
. To ensure that
x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)
is a constant point for all
(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\in\tilde{B}^{j,C}
,
\frac{\partial x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}}{\partial\varphi}
must be a zero vector for all
(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\in\tilde{B}^{j,C}
. Thus, this case does not happen.

(Case 2)

x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)
is not a constant point in
\partial\Omega
for all
(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\in\tilde{B}^{j,C}
.

Recall the function
\tilde{H}_{j}
defined in (
5.12
). We assume that
\tilde{H}_{j}=0
at some point
(\tau_{1},\varphi_{1},\eta_{1})\in\tilde{B}^{j,C}
. Through the coordinate
\hat{x}=(1,0,0),\hat{y}=(0,0,1)
, and
\hat{\varphi}=(0,1,0)
such as (
1.50
) and the calculation (
5.13
) above, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{j}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{H}_{j}^{x}\\
\tilde{H}_{j}^{y}\\
\tilde{H}_{j}^{\varphi}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}{x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}}_{,1}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)-x_{1}\\
{x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}}_{,3}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)-x_{3}\\
{x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}}_{,2}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)-x_{2}\end{bmatrix}\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\frac{\partial\tilde{H}_{j}}{\partial\varphi}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial\tilde{H}_{j}^{x}}{\partial\varphi}\\
\frac{\partial\tilde{H}_{j}^{y}}{\partial\varphi}\\
\frac{\partial\tilde{H}_{j}^{\varphi}}{\partial\varphi}\end{bmatrix}=\frac{\partial x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}}{\partial\varphi}=k_{\tau,\varphi,\eta}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
1\end{bmatrix},

(5.14)

where
x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=({x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}}_{,1}(\tau,\varphi,\eta),{x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}}_{,2}(\tau,\varphi,\eta),{x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}}_{,3}(\tau,\varphi,\eta))
and
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
. Hence,
\left.\frac{\partial\tilde{H}_{j}^{\varphi}}{\partial\varphi}\right|_{(\tau_{1},\varphi_{1},\eta_{1})}\neq 0
. Using Lemma
4.3
, there exists an open set
V_{\tau_{1},\eta_{1}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
containing
(\tau_{1},\eta_{1})
such that there exists an analytic function
\varphi=\varphi(\tau,\eta)
such that

\displaystyle\varphi(\tau_{1},\eta_{1})=\varphi_{1}\quad\text{ and }\quad\tilde{H}_{j}^{\varphi}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)=0\text{ for all }(\tau,\eta)\in\tilde{V}_{\tau_{1},\eta_{1}},

(5.15)

where
\tilde{V}_{\tau_{1},\eta_{1}}:=\Big\{(\tau,\eta)\in V_{\tau_{1},\eta_{1}}:(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{i},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{i-1})(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I},\quad\forall 1\leq i\leq j\Big\}
. If
\tilde{H}_{j}^{x}
or
\tilde{H}_{j}^{y}
is identically zero on
\tilde{V}_{\tau_{1},\eta_{1}}
, then
\tilde{H}_{j}^{\varphi}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)=\tilde{H}_{j}^{x}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)
or
\tilde{H}_{j}^{\varphi}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)=\tilde{H}_{j}^{y}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)
for all
(\tau,\eta)\in\tilde{V}_{\tau_{1},\eta_{1}}
due to (
5.15
). Since
\frac{\partial\tilde{H}_{j}^{\varphi}}{\partial\varphi}\neq\frac{\partial\tilde{H}_{j}^{x}}{\partial\varphi}
and
\frac{\partial\tilde{H}_{j}^{\varphi}}{\partial\varphi}\neq\frac{\partial\tilde{H}_{j}^{y}}{\partial\varphi}
in (
5.14
),
\tilde{H}_{j}^{x}
and
\tilde{H}_{j}^{y}
cannot be identically zero. Thus, the zero set
\mathcal{Z}_{x}^{H,j,C}
of
\tilde{H}_{j}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{x}^{H,j,C}:=\{(\tau,\eta)\in\tilde{V}_{\tau_{1},\eta_{1}}:\tilde{H}_{j}^{x}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)=\tilde{H}_{j}^{y}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)=0\}

(5.16)

has measure zero in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by Lemma
3.1
. Applying Lemma
5.1
yields that

\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:v=v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi(\tau,\eta),\eta)\quad\textrm{for }(\tau,\eta)\in\mathcal{Z}_{x}^{H,j,C}\}

(5.17)

has measure zero in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Similar to the zero set
\mathcal{T}_{x}^{F,j,C}
in (
5.10
), we construct the zero set
{T}_{x}^{H,j,C}
of
\tilde{H}_{j}
as:

\displaystyle{T}_{x}^{H,j,C}:=\{(\tau,\varphi,\eta):\tilde{H}_{j}(\tau,\varphi,\eta)=0\}.

The zero set
{T}_{x}^{H,j,C}
is also compact. For each point
(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\in{T}_{x}^{H,j,C}
, we construct an open set
V_{\tau,\eta}
and zero set such as (
5.16
) by repeating above argument. If we consider projection
\Pi_{(\tau,\eta)}
onto
\tau\times\eta
plane,
\Pi_{(\tau,\eta)}{T}_{x}^{H,j,C}:=\{(\tau,\eta):(\tau,\varphi,\eta)\in{T}_{x}^{H,j,C}\}
is still compact. So, we have finite subcover for
\Pi_{(\tau,\eta)}{T}_{x}^{H,j,C}
, and hence image under
v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}
map as in (
5.17
) is still zero.

Step 2
In this step, we deal with inflection grazing
\gamma_{0}^{I}
in
(B_{j})_{x}
. We claim

\mathfrak{m}_{2}\{v\in(G_{j-1})_{x}:(x^{j}(x,v),v^{j-1}(x,v))\in\gamma_{0}^{I}\}=0,\\

where
(G_{j-1})_{x}
was constructed inductively in (
4.70
) and (
4.84
). Since
v\in(G_{j-1})_{x}
, trajectory does not graze between its first bounce and
(j-1)
-th bounce.

(Interior case
S_{0}
)
We firstly consider
x\in S_{0}
. Since
(G_{j-1})_{x}\subset(X^{\varepsilon})_{x}
and inflection grazing does not happen in trajectory between
(x,v)
and
(x^{j}(x,v),v^{j-1}(x,v))
, the map
(X,V)(s(\cdot))
is invertible by Lemma
4.6
. Using invertible map
(X,V)(s(\cdot))
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}(x,v)=(X,V)(s(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),\varphi,I^{2}(\tau,\varphi));0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi)),\end{split}

where
s(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
be the arrival time defined in (
4.34
),
\sigma(\cdot,\cdot)
be the parametrization of boundary
\partial\Omega
in (
1.35
), and
I^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)
be parametrization for inflection grazing in Lemma
3.6
.
Let us define

\begin{split}P_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}:=\Big\{(\tau,\varphi):(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))=(x^{j}(y,u),v^{j-1}(y,u))\in\gamma_{0}^{I}\ \text{for some}\ (y,u)\in X^{\varepsilon},\ u\in(G_{j-1})_{y}\Big\}.\\
\end{split}

(5.18)

Note that
P_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
is also disjoint for all
1\leq j\leq K
. By applying (b) of Lemma
4.10
to forward in time trajectory and Lemma
4.12
, We consider

F_{j}(\tau,\varphi):=X(s(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),\varphi,I^{2}(\tau,\varphi));0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))-x,\quad(\tau,\varphi)\in P_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}.\\

(5.19)

In
Step 1
, we showed that
Q_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
is open. Similarly, we can prove that the set
P_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
is open to obtain analyticity of the function
F_{j}
. Note that
\sigma
in (
1.35
) and
I^{2}
in Lemma
3.6
are all locally smooth. Now, let us consider forward in time trajectory from
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))=(x^{j}(y,u),v^{j-1}(y,u))
. By definition of
(G_{j-1})_{y}
and Lemma
4.17
, the trajectory is non-grazing until it arrives at
(y,u)
, and hence the map

(\tau,\varphi)\mapsto(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))\mapsto(X,V)(s(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),\varphi,I^{2}(\tau,\varphi));0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))\in S_{0}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}

is continuous with finite number of bounce by Lemma
4.12
. Now, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta\ll 1
such that if
|(\tau,\varphi)-(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})|<\delta
, then

|(y,u)-(y^{\prime},u^{\prime})|<\varepsilon,

where

(y^{\prime},u^{\prime}):=(X,V)(s(\sigma(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}),\varphi^{\prime},I^{2}(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}));0,\sigma(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}),I^{2}(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}))\in S_{0}\times\mathbb{S}^{2},

or equivalently,

(\sigma(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}),I^{2}(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}))=(x^{j}(y^{\prime},u^{\prime}),v^{j-1}(y^{\prime},u^{\prime}))\in\gamma_{0}^{I}.

Proving open property of
P_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
and analyticity of
F_{j}
is quite similar to what we did in
Step 1
. We know that
(y,u)\in G_{L}^{\varepsilon}
and
G_{L}^{\varepsilon}
is open by (a) in Lemma
4.18
. Thus, for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small,
(y^{\prime},u^{\prime})\in G_{L}^{\varepsilon}
. Using the fact that
x
is not on the boundary, choosing sufficiently small
\varepsilon
and applying Lemma
4.10
for forward in time trajectory from
(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),v_{c}(\eta))
, yields
u^{\prime}\in(G_{j-1})_{x^{\prime}}
.
And, by definition (
5.18
) of
P_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
, we obtain that
(\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\in P_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
which means that
P_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
is open. Moreover,
{F}_{j}
is locally analytic on
P_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
by Lemma
4.12
.

Repeat (
5.6
) of
Step 1
to claim the case of
F_{j}
being a constant vector in
P_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}
cannot happen by excluding
R_{sym}^{\varepsilon}
in
X^{\varepsilon}
. Then, the zero set
\mathcal{Z}_{x}^{F,j,I}
of the analytic function
F_{j}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{x}^{F,j,I}:=\{(\tau,\varphi)\in P_{L}^{\varepsilon,j}:F_{j}(\tau,\varphi)=0\}

has measure zero in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
due to Lemma
3.1
. We can use Lemma
5.1
directly since
\mathcal{Z}_{x}^{F,j,I}
has the same dimension with
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Thus, from Lemma
5.1
, we get

\displaystyle\{v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}:v=V(s(\tau,\varphi);0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))\;\textrm{for }(\tau,\varphi)\in\mathcal{Z}_{x}^{F,j,I}\}

has measure zero in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

(
Boundary case
\partial S_{0}
)
Next, let
x\in\partial S_{0}
. Using the map
(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1})
,

\displaystyle(x,v)=(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1})(\tau,\varphi),

where
x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau,\varphi):=X(t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi));0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi)),v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\tau,\varphi):=V(t_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\sigma(\tau,

\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi));0,\sigma(\tau,\varphi),I^{2}(\tau,\varphi))
. Define

\displaystyle\tilde{B}^{j,I}:=\{(\tau,\varphi)\in[a,b)\times(-2\pi\mathbf{w},0]:(x_{\mathbf{f}}^{i},v_{\mathbf{f}}^{i-1})(\tau,\varphi)\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I},\quad\forall 1\leq i\leq j\},

where
\mathbf{w}
be the winding number in (
1.48
). By the same reason for openness of
\tilde{B}^{j,C}
defined in (
5.11
), the set
\tilde{B}^{j,I}
is also open. From Lemma
4.10
, the function

\displaystyle H_{j}(\tau,\varphi):=x_{\mathbf{f}}^{j}(\tau,\varphi)-x,\quad(\tau,\varphi)\in\tilde{B}^{j,I},

is locally analytic on
\tilde{B}^{j,I}
. Using the same arguments in
Step 1
and Lemma
3.1
,
H_{j}
cannot be a constant vector and the zero set
\mathcal{Z}_{x}^{H,j,I}
of
H_{j}
has measure zero in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then, the image
v_{\mathbf{f}}^{j-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{x}^{H,j,I})
has measure zero in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
by applying Lemma
5.1
directly.

∎

Definition 5.3
.

From Lemma
4.22
, we define
\mathcal{BM}_{x}
similar to
\mathcal{BK}_{x}
replacing
K
into
M
in (
4.72
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{BM}_{x}:=\bigcup_{j=1}^{M}(B_{j})_{x}\subset\mathbb{S}^{2}.

(5.20)

Similar to (
4.73
), by Lemma
5.2
, there exists an open set
\mathcal{BM}_{x}^{\varepsilon}\subset\mathbb{S}^{2}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{BM}_{x}\subset\mathcal{BM}_{x}^{\varepsilon},\quad\mathfrak{m}_{2}\left(\mathcal{BM}_{x}^{\varepsilon}\right)<\varepsilon.

(5.21)

Lemma 5.4
.

(
\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
version) Let
S_{0}
be
\varphi=0
cross-section in (
1.49
) and
\varepsilon>0
. Recall the definition (
4.81
) of
\mathcal{K}^{c}
. There exists a compact set
\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}\subset\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
such that

\sup_{(x,v)\in\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{N}(x,v,L)\leq M,

(5.22)

where we defined
\mathcal{N}(x,v,L)
in (
4.28
), the constant
M
in Lemma
4.22

Moreover, if
(x,v)\in\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}
, then

[x^{j}(x,v),v^{j-1}(x,v)]\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}\cup\gamma_{0}^{C},\quad\forall 1\leq j\leq M,

(5.23)

and

\inf_{1\leq i\leq M}|v^{i-1}(x,v)\cdot n(x^{i}(x,v))|\geq C^{*}_{\varepsilon,L}>0,

(5.24)

where the constant
C_{\varepsilon,L}^{*}
does not depend on
x
. In addition, for
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
,

\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\{\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon})_{x})\lesssim\varepsilon,

(5.25)

where we used the notation
A_{x}
in (
4.46
).

Proof.

Consider the following set

\displaystyle F=\Big\{(x,v)\in\mathcal{K}^{c}:v\in\mathcal{BM}_{x}\Big\},

(5.26)

where
\mathcal{K}^{c}
in (
4.81
) is compact and
\mathcal{BM}_{x}
is defined in (
5.20
). Recall that the projection operator
\mathcal{P}_{1}
in (
4.53
). Note that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{K}^{c})=\overline{S_{0}}
by the same argument to deduce
\mathcal{P}_{1}((\mathcal{NB})^{c})=\overline{S_{0}}
in proof of Lemma
4.16
. For each
x\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{K}^{c})=\overline{S_{0}}
, by Lemma
4.22
and definition of
\mathcal{BM}_{x}
in (
5.20
),

\displaystyle(x^{i}(x,v),v^{i-1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}\cup\gamma_{0}^{C},\quad\text{up to whole travel length $L$},

for
v\notin\mathcal{BM}_{x}
. Notice that the trajectory with a finite number of bounces is reversible and deterministic. If convex grazing occurs during whole travel length
L
, the forward in time trajectory cannot be defined. Hence, there is no convex grazing during travel length
L
. Thus, there exists a positive constant
C_{x,\varepsilon,L}^{*}>0
depending on
x,\varepsilon,L
satisfying

\displaystyle|v^{i-1}(x,v)\cdot n(x^{i}(x,v))|\geq C^{*}_{x,\varepsilon,L}>0,\quad\text{up to whole travel length $L$}.

(5.27)

By compactness of
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{K}^{c})=\overline{S_{0}}
, Lemma
4.20
, Lemma
5.2
and (
5.21
), we have an open cover for
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{K}^{c})=\overline{S_{0}}

\Big\{B(x_{i}^{\mathcal{IB}},r_{i}^{\mathcal{IB}})\cap\overline{S_{0}}\Big\}_{i=1}^{l_{IB}},

(5.28)

and corresponding each open set
\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\mathcal{IB}}\subset\mathbb{S}^{2}
which is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\mathcal{IB}}:=\mathcal{BM}_{x_{i}^{\mathcal{IB}}}^{\varepsilon},\quad 1\leq i\leq l_{IB},

(5.29)

satisfying
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\mathcal{IB}})<\varepsilon
for all
1\leq i\leq l_{IB}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{BM}_{x}\subset\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\mathcal{IB}},

(5.30)

for all
x\in B(x_{i}^{\mathcal{IB}},r_{i}^{\mathcal{IB}})
and sufficiently small
r_{i}^{\mathcal{IB}}
.
Similar to (
4.78
) in Lemma
4.21
, for given
x\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{K}^{c})=\overline{S_{0}}
, we define an index set

\displaystyle I_{x}^{\mathcal{IB}}:=\Big\{i\in\mathbb{N}:x\in B(x_{i}^{\mathcal{IB}},r_{i}^{\mathcal{iB}})\cap\overline{S_{0}}\Big\}.

(5.31)

Now, we consider the following set

\displaystyle\bigcup_{x\in\overline{S_{0}}}\Big\{(x,v)\in\mathcal{K}^{c}:v\in\bigcap_{i\in I_{x}^{\mathcal{IB}}}\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\mathcal{IB}}\Big\},

(5.32)

which contains set
F
in (
5.26
). Next, we construct an open cover for (
5.32
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{IB}:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{l_{IB}}\Big\{(x,v)\in\mathcal{K}^{c}:x\in B(x_{i}^{\mathcal{IB}},r_{i}^{\mathcal{IB}})\cap\overline{S_{0}},\;v\in\bigcap_{j\in I_{x}^{\mathcal{IB}}}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathcal{IB}}\Big\}_{\varepsilon},

(5.33)

where we have used the notation
A_{\varepsilon}
in (
4.51
). In this paper, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{K}^{c}\backslash\mathcal{IB},

(5.34)

which is compact since
\mathcal{K}^{c}
is compact and
\mathcal{IB}
is open.
By definition (
5.34
) of the set
\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}
, we directly deduce the relation
\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}\subset\mathcal{K}^{c}
. Hence, from (
4.22
), we obtain (
5.22
). Assume that
(x,v)\in\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}
. By definition (
5.33
) of
\mathcal{IB}
and (
5.30
),
v\notin\mathcal{BM}_{x}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{M}(B_{j})_{x}
. This implies that

\displaystyle(x^{j}(x,v),v^{j-1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}\cup\gamma_{0}^{C},\quad\forall 1\leq j\leq M.

Also, (
5.24
) can be obtained if we choose
C^{*}_{\varepsilon,L}:=\min_{1\leq i\leq l_{IB}}C_{x_{i}^{\mathcal{IB}},\varepsilon,L}^{*}
in (
5.27
). For fixed
x\in\overline{S_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{IB}_{x}=\Big\{v\in(\mathcal{K}^{c})_{x}:v\in\bigcap_{j\in I_{x}^{\mathcal{IB}}}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathcal{IB}}\Big\}_{\varepsilon},

(5.35)

where the notation
A_{x}
is defined in (
4.46
) and the notation
A_{\varepsilon}
in (
4.51
) comes from the definition (
4.80
). Since

\displaystyle\{\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}=(\{\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash\mathcal{K}^{c})\cupdot\mathcal{IB},

we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\{\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash\mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon})_{x})&\leq\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\{\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash\mathcal{K}^{c})_{x})+\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\mathcal{IB}_{x})\\
&\leq\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\{\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash\mathcal{K}^{c})_{x})+\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathcal{IB}})_{\varepsilon})\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon,\quad\forall j\in I_{x}^{\mathcal{IB}},\end{split}

from (
4.83
),(
5.35
), and
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathcal{IB}})\lesssim\varepsilon
for all
j=1,2,\dots,l_{IB}
.
∎

Definition 5.5
.

Recall that the compact set
\mathcal{K}^{c}\subset\overline{S_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
in proof of Lemma
4.21
. Let us consider cross section
S_{\varphi}
defined in (
1.49
) for fixed
\varphi\in[0,2\pi)
. Similar to
\mathcal{K}^{c}
in (
4.81
), using
\varphi
-symmetry in our domain
\Omega
, there exists a compact set
\mathcal{J}^{c}\subset\overline{S_{\varphi}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
such that if
(x,v)\in\mathcal{J}^{c}
,

\displaystyle(x^{i}(x,v),v^{i-1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I}\cup\gamma_{0}^{C},\quad\text{for first $K$ bounce},

and

\displaystyle(x^{i}(x,v),v^{i-1}(x,v))\notin\gamma_{0}^{I},\quad\text{up to whole travel length $L$.}

To extend the compact subset
\mathcal{J}^{c}\subset\overline{S_{\varphi}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
to
\overline{S_{\varphi}}\times\mathbb{V}^{N}
, we define the following compact set

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{c}:=\Big\{(x,v)\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{J}^{c})\times\mathbb{V}^{N}:\frac{v}{|v|}\in(\mathcal{J}^{c})_{x}\Big\},

(5.36)

where we used the projection operator
\mathcal{P}_{1}
in (
4.53
) and
A_{x}
in (
4.46
). Note that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{J}^{c})=\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{V}^{c})=\overline{S_{\varphi}}
.

Remark 5.6
.

Recall the notation
A_{x}
in (
4.46
). Similar to (
4.83
) in proof of Lemma
4.21
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{2}((\{\overline{S_{\varphi}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}\backslash\mathcal{J}^{c})_{x})\lesssim\varepsilon,

for fixed
x\in\overline{S_{\varphi}}
. Since
\mathcal{V}^{c}
can be obtained by scaling velocities in
\mathcal{J}^{c}
from
1/N
to
N
, we also deduce that for fixed
x\in\overline{S_{\varphi}}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{3}((\{\overline{S_{\varphi}}\times\mathbb{V}^{N}\}\backslash\mathcal{V}^{c})_{x})\leq C_{N}\varepsilon,

(5.37)

where the constant
C_{N}
depends on
N
.

5.2.
Phase Decomposition theorem

Theorem 5.7
.

(Generalized version)
Let us consider cross section
S_{\varphi}
defined in (
1.49
) for fixed
\varphi\in[0,2\pi)
. Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a compact set
\mathcal{Z}^{\varepsilon}\subset\overline{S_{\varphi}}\times\mathbb{V}^{N}
such that the following hold: if
(x,v)\in\mathcal{Z}^{\varepsilon}
, then

[x^{j}(x,v),v^{j-1}(x,v)]\notin\gamma_{0}^{C}\cup\gamma_{0}^{I},\quad 1\leq j\leq M,

where
M
was defined in Lemma
4.22
. Hence its backward in time trajectory up to a travel length
L=NT
is always uniformly non-grazing. i.e.,

\displaystyle\inf_{1\leq i\leq M}|v^{i-1}(x,v)\cdot n(x^{i}(x,v))|\geq C^{*}_{\varepsilon,L}>0.

Here, the constant
C^{*}_{\varepsilon,L}
does not depend on
x
. Moreover, for fixed
x\in\overline{S_{\varphi}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{3}((\{\overline{S_{\varphi}}\times\mathbb{V}^{N}\}\backslash\mathcal{Z}^{\varepsilon})_{x})\leq C_{N}\varepsilon,

(5.38)

where the constant
C_{N}
depends only on
N
and the notation
A_{x}
was defined in (
4.46
).

Proof.

For any
x\in\overline{\Omega}
, there exists
\varphi\in[0,2\pi)
such that
x\in\overline{S_{\varphi}}
.
Since our domain is symmetric with respect to
\varphi
,
using compactness argument in proof of Lemma
5.4
, we can find a relatively open cover for
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{V}^{c})=\overline{S_{\varphi}}

\displaystyle\bigcup_{i=1}^{l_{JB}}\{B(x_{i}^{\mathcal{JB}},r_{i}^{\mathcal{JB}})\cap\overline{S_{\varphi}}\},

and corresponding velocity set

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{i}^{\mathcal{JB}}\subset\mathbb{S}^{2},

with
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\mathcal{J}_{i}^{\mathcal{JB}})\lesssim\varepsilon
for all
i=1,2,\dots,l_{JB}
such as (
5.28
) and (
5.29
). We define the following velocity subset of
\mathbb{V}^{N}

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\mathcal{JB}}:=\left\{v\in\mathbb{V}^{N}:\frac{v}{|v|}\in\mathcal{J}_{i}^{\mathcal{JB}}\right\},

and then
\mathfrak{m}_{3}(\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\mathcal{JB}})\leq C_{N}\varepsilon
for all
i=1,2,\dots,l_{JB}
because the set
\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\mathcal{JB}}
can be obtained by scaling vectors in
\mathcal{J}_{i}^{\mathcal{JB}}
from
1/N
to
N
. Here, the constant
C_{N}
increases as
N
increases. Similar as (
5.33
) in Lemma
5.4
, we construct an open cover
\mathcal{JB}
such as

\displaystyle\mathcal{JB}:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{l_{JB}}\Big\{(x,v)\in\mathcal{V}^{c}:x\in B(x_{i}^{\mathcal{JB}},r_{i}^{\mathcal{JB}})\cap\overline{S_{\varphi}},\;v\in\bigcap_{j\in I_{x}^{\mathcal{JB}}}\mathcal{V}_{j}^{\mathcal{JB}}\Big\}_{\varepsilon},

(5.39)

where
A_{\varepsilon}
was defined in (
4.51
) and

\displaystyle I_{x}^{\mathcal{JB}}:=\Big\{i\in\mathbb{N}:x\in B(x_{i}^{\mathcal{JB}},r_{i}^{\mathcal{JB}})\cap\overline{S_{\varphi}}\Big\}.

(5.40)

Let us consider a compact set

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{V}^{c}\backslash\mathcal{JB}.

(5.41)

Since

\displaystyle\{\overline{S_{\varphi}}\times\mathbb{V}^{N}\}\backslash\mathcal{Z}^{\varepsilon}=(\{\overline{S_{\varphi}}\times\mathbb{V}^{N}\}\backslash\mathcal{V}^{c})\cupdot\mathcal{JB},

it follows from (
5.37
),(
5.39
), and
\mathfrak{m}_{3}(\mathcal{V}_{j}^{\mathcal{JB}})\leq C_{N}\varepsilon
for all
j=1,2,\dots,l_{JB}
that for fixed
x\in\overline{S_{\varphi}}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{m}_{3}((\{\overline{S_{\varphi}}\times\mathbb{V}^{N}\}\backslash\mathcal{Z}^{\varepsilon})_{x})&\leq\mathfrak{m}_{3}((\{\overline{S_{\varphi}}\times\mathbb{V}^{N}\}\backslash\mathcal{V}^{c})_{x})+\mathfrak{m}_{3}(\mathcal{JB}_{x})\\
&\leq\mathfrak{m}_{3}((\{\overline{S_{\varphi}}\times\mathbb{V}^{N}\}\backslash\mathcal{V}^{c})_{x})+\mathfrak{m}_{3}((\mathcal{V}_{j}^{\mathcal{JB}})_{\varepsilon})\\
&\leq C_{N}\varepsilon,\quad\forall j\in I_{x}^{\mathcal{JB}},\end{split}

(5.42)

where we have used the notation
A_{x}
in (
4.46
) and
A_{\varepsilon}
in (
4.51
).

For
v\in\mathbb{V}^{N}
, the size of velocity
v
must be
N
for the highest number of bounces during a fixed time travel length
T
. This case is the same as the size of velocity is
1
and time travel length
NT
. Hence, in the two cases away from bad directions, the bouncing points
(x^{i}(x,v),v^{i-1}(x,v))
are the same for all
i=1,2,\dots,M
. From Lemma
5.4
, if
(x,v)\in\mathcal{Z}^{\varepsilon}
